## Preface to the Fifth Edition

The first edition of this book came out in the wake of what I saw as a heroic act by a group of Palestinian militants at the 1972 Munich Olympics.

I make no secret of the fact that this act of resistance, which I regarded as a great one and which Zionist and imperialist propaganda portrayed as an act of terrorism, inspired me to write this book. I wanted to make the case for the legitimacy of revolutionary violence on the part of a people whose land had been taken from them, who had been driven out and isolated by the world — even by the Arab world — and whose struggle had been misrepresented both within Palestine and beyond its borders.

I had two main aims in publishing the book. The first was to contribute to the struggle itself. I wanted to revisit the revolutionary heritage of Islam, to look at some of the movements that challenged those in power, and to show what they were up against — politically, economically, socially and even in the world of ideas. How did they overcome these difficulties? They made a wise choice: they began to organise in secret, laying the groundwork for a successful social revolution. One of the critics I debated at the time picked up on this. He went so far as to call the book a manifesto for revolutionary youth, accusing me of trying to stir them into action and encourage them to engage in politics “in back rooms and underground cellars”.

<!-- PDF page 6 | printed page 8 -->

My second aim was to change the way we understand history. With the approach I took to Islamic history, I wanted to clear away the rubble left behind by traditional ways of thinking. They had turned that history into a collection of myths, tales of the unseen, miracles, wondrous deeds and stories glorifying great men. This book was a first step towards exposing the bankruptcy of this crude way of presenting the past. It was also an attempt to sow the seeds of a more scientific approach, one that understands society through the struggles and conflicts that shape it.

The book stirred up a storm of debate across the Arab world, and it continues to do so. I found myself accused of being a foreign agent, of promoting immorality and of being a heretic. Such accusations are relics of an age of decline, trotted out whenever people cannot defend their own views with arguments and evidence.

None of this stopped me from carrying on, at home or abroad. I continued to write; my later books became widely known outside Egypt, where they circulated freely, even as they were confiscated within the country. I also continued teaching at university, where my classes attracted more students year after year. It is enough for me to know that before long, many writers, thinkers and historians throughout the Arab world began to take the same approach to history that I had introduced in this book. They went on to play a leading role in Arab intellectual life, at scholarly conferences, historical research seminars and discussion groups. I also had the pleasure of seeing dozens of my students become researchers in their own right and earn their places in Arab universities.

What matters here is that the years since the first edition have borne out the book's starting points and ideas, both politically and intellectually. During those years it was published four times and reproduced just as often without my knowledge. In politics, events in Egypt, Palestine and the Arab world as a whole have exposed the failure of the property-owning class’s strategy of “negotiation” with imperialism and Zionism. That strategy <!-- PDF page 7 | printed page 9 --> stripped the victory of 1973 of its substance and entrenched dependence, subservience and underdevelopment.

The Arab and Islamic worlds were beset by two ills: fragmentation and extremism. Both were the result of suppressing rational, enlightened and revolutionary thought. Wars broke out between “brothers turned enemies”, who hid what were primarily economic conflicts behind slogans of ethnic rivalry and sectarian division. These wars helped colonial powers regain the ground they had lost. Their political dominance and the region's economic and cultural dependence on them became more firmly established, especially after the collapse of the socialist bloc.

With no national project for renewal and no compelling set of ideas to fill the void, religious and sectarian extremism became an ever greater danger. Like an octopus, it spread its tentacles across the Arab and Islamic worlds, taking on an international character and threatening the very existence of the nation-state and civil society. It gave imperialist and Zionist projects a free hand while paralysing efforts towards Arab unity.

As for the world of ideas, our book helped draw attention to the need to rethink the Arab-Islamic heritage in light of the revolution in knowledge and methods of research that the world had witnessed since the middle of the century. Several studies of that heritage followed in its footsteps. They undoubtedly helped reaffirm the value of science, knowledge, enlightenment and respect for human dignity. They also helped uncover the cultural identity of Arab-Islamic society and opened the way to thinking about a future intellectual renewal that could bring us together again after years of division and fragmentation.

This is where the importance of *Secret Movements in Islam* lies. It was a pioneering work in the effort to enlighten minds and awaken a revolutionary spirit. The story of this book, and the debate that surrounded it over fifteen years, have shown that the time has come to present the heritage of <!-- PDF page 8 | printed page 10 --> Islam in a rational and scientific light. It is time to set aside a vision of the past that relies on appeals to ancestral authority, convenient excuses, prohibitions and coercion. In its place we need a broad view that treats the heritage of those who held power and that of those who opposed them as two inseparable parts of the same whole.

It is time to revive the revolutionary spirit of the Kharijites, the commitment to justice of the Shia and the faith in reason of the Mu'tazilites. They represent the bright side of our heritage, the threads we need to pick up again. They stand in contrast to its darker side: the rigid reliance on texts and submission to authority associated with Ash'arite thought, and the retreat from the real world associated with Sufism. It is time to give Ibn Rushd and Ibn Khaldun the place now occupied by al-Ghazali and Ibn Taymiyya. It is time for a movement of religious reform that will free the Arab-Islamic heritage from the grip of the clergy of al-Azhar, the Zaytuna Mosque and the University of Kairouan.

And finally, it is time to present a revolutionary Islam, a set of ideas for the property-owning class and working people alike, in place of an Islam that lulls people into submission and serves as a justification for military cliques and the tyrants of extremist religious rule.

I hope this book will make a worthwhile contribution to laying the foundations of the religious reform we seek.

**Mahmoud Ismail**  
January 1997

## Preface to the First Edition

What inspired me to undertake this study was the action of a group of men from the Black September organisation at the Munich Olympics last year, an act I regarded as heroic. The forces of world imperialism and Zionism joined in presenting the incident as mindless terrorism. World opinion followed their hostile propaganda, which misrepresented the legitimate struggle of the Palestinian people and overlooked the real reasons why people in desperate circumstances are driven to carry on their struggle in secret. Those against whom the struggle is directed may see such activity as terrorism. But the legitimacy of underground resistance, I argue, remains as long as the conditions that drive national movements to adopt it remain, and as long as those taking part are pursuing a noble goal.

I have tried to find examples in Islamic history of the underground political activity that movements opposed to the religious rule of the Umayyads and Abbasids resorted to in the face of tyranny. Those governments had abandoned justice and strayed from the path laid out by Islamic law. I wanted to make the case for the legitimacy of underground resistance, both because of the nobility of its aims and because of the ferocity with which those in power crushed popular uprisings demanding the justice promised by Islam.

<!-- PDF page 10 | printed page 12 -->

Making that case was no easy task. Most of the writings handed down to us defend the Byzantine-style monarchy of the Umayyads and the Persian-style rule of the Abbasids. They show little mercy towards any movement or popular uprising that challenged these rulers.

Politically, such movements were condemned as acts of insolence, rebellion and unrest against those in authority. Socially, they were dismissed as uprisings of the common rabble. Religiously, they were branded as departures from the faith, unbelief and heresy. Their ideas were treated as little more than imitations of pagan thought.

The picture of the opposition that has taken root in our minds is therefore a distorted one. This applies to the Kharijites and the Shia, whom we might call the parties of the left, and to the Mu'tazilites and the Murji'ites, whom we might call the parties of the centre, if we may borrow the political language of our own age.

It is a picture painted by the right. It is official history — history written, as Ibn Arabi put it, by historians for kings.

What about the other side of the story? Much of it is beyond our reach. Most of the opposition's writings were burned or destroyed by its enemies. What survived often remained hidden and difficult to access. And the little that has come down to us is filled with legends, superstitions and tales of miracles, which lessen its value as historical evidence.

There is another difficulty. Underground movements, by their very nature, depend on secrecy. This gives their enemies ample room to cast doubt on their beliefs, their organisations and the people behind them.

If today's historian has little choice but to rely mainly on the writings of these movements' enemies, then all the more reason to proceed with care. Before drawing evidence from these sources, we must examine them critically. We need to understand the political loyalties, religious biases and social circumstances of those who wrote them.

<!-- PDF page 11 | printed page 13 -->

The fact is that most of the “official” historians were politically loyal to the rulers and Sunni in their religious beliefs. They belonged to the class known as the “people of the pen”, or scribes — men employed in government offices who enjoyed the favours of those in power and were rewarded with grants of land. Little wonder that they heaped praise on their benefactors and defended everything they did, while showing no such generosity in their judgement of the opposition. We should not simply take their writings at face value. As al-Dhahabi observed:

> Pay little attention to what people say against their peers, especially when it seems to stem from hostility, religious differences or envy.

Al-Maqbali made much the same point:

> Each sect has taken to telling of the evil deeds of its opponents while keeping quiet about the good. Some even spread lies and false accusations.

Little wonder that sayings were falsely attributed to the Prophet and accounts of the righteous early Muslims were made up to serve the purposes of rival groups.

Sadly, many modern scholars pay no heed to these pitfalls. They accept the words of past generations as established truths. The historian al-Dimashqi put it this way:

> This only came about when blind imitation took hold and the words of those who introduced new beliefs were treated as though they were the words of someone who could never be wrong, or the very words of scripture.

Accepting the past uncritically, under the illusion that it is free from error, leads to what Dr Zaki Naguib Mahmoud called “the drawing of thick curtains between people and the advances in science and knowledge that time has brought”.

We therefore need to rethink what has been handed down to us about the forces in the shadows of Islamic history. We need a view of that history as a whole, one that takes into account the heart of Islam's message, the high value it places on human beings and its far-reaching principles of justice. The teachings of Islam can then serve as the yardstick by which we judge the different political forces and parties, whether they held power or stood in opposition to it.

<!-- PDF page 12 | printed page 14 -->

A brief look at Islamic law is enough to bring out the basic principles of political, social and cultural life that it sets forth. Democratic consultation lies at the heart of government and politics. Justice and equality are the backbone of economic life and social relations. Reason is at the heart of the Islamic approach to thinking. How closely, then, did the Islamic governments and the forces that opposed them live up to these principles?

What we know of the Umayyads is that theirs was a hereditary monarchy, about as far removed from democracy as one could imagine. They favoured Arabs over all other groups of Muslims, betraying the very spirit of justice and equality. The belief that everything was determined in advance by God held sway throughout this period, providing a justification and a rule for accepting things as they were.

Under the Abbasids, the caliphate — the system of government headed by a caliph, the leader of the Muslim community — followed the model of the old Persian kings and rested on the belief that the right to rule came directly from God. The Persians, followed by the Turks, secured a privileged position at the top of society at the expense of everyone else. Traditionalist religious thought dominated the period, apart from a brief few years in which a current of rational thought came to the fore.

The movements opposed to these governments, by contrast, were more faithful to the teachings of Islam and more determined to put them into practice. From the second half of the [first] century of the Islamic calendar onwards, the Kharijites, the Mu'tazilites and the Murji'ites all agreed that any Muslim had the right to become caliph, regardless of their ancestry or group loyalties. Their political ideas were inspired by the democratic spirit of Islam. All of these movements, including the Shia, took up the cause of justice and fought to make it a reality. Their religious beliefs, too, were marked by a strong spirit of reason, particularly in the case of the Shia and Mu'tazilites.

We may therefore say that Islam does not stand at odds with the activities of the opposition, nor does it generally give its blessing to the policies of the Umayyads and Abbasids. It thus affirms the legitimacy of underground resistance as a means of bringing down the Umayyad government, backed by what I would call the rabble of Syria, <!-- PDF page 13 | printed page 15 --> and the Abbasid caliphate, which rested on the support of armies of uncouth Turks or Persians hostile to Arab dominance.

It is only natural that the opposition movements should have drawn on all kinds of ideas and methods in their legitimate underground struggle. In this respect they had much to gain from the heritage of the ancient world. The lands of Islam were rich in the legacy of many civilisations, from Greek and Eastern traditions to Jewish lore and pagan beliefs. Islamic thought could hardly close its ears and eyes to the vast inheritance that humanity had built up over the centuries. Instead, it opened itself up to that heritage, drank deeply from it, made it its own and took from it whatever did not conflict with the essence of Islam. This openness gave it the strength to withstand the attacks of those hostile to Arab supremacy and those who rejected the faith. The Islamic mind ventured into the charitable institutions associated with the Library of Alexandria, the monasteries and churches of the Christians, the synagogues of the Jews and the Zoroastrian fire temples. It uncovered treasures of wisdom and knowledge, adapted them to the needs of the age and gave them a distinctively Islamic character.

It is no surprise that the opposition movements were among the first to draw on these inherited and newly encountered currents of thought. They did so both in shaping their religious ideas and beliefs and in laying the foundations of their movements and political organisations. The influence of Jewish traditions can clearly be seen in the underground organisations of the left. Greek and Eastern traditions also left their mark on their political and social arrangements, so long as the spirit behind those ideas did not conflict with that of Islam.

In studying these secret movements, we have tried to show the extent of these outside influences and how faithfully the opposition parties followed the teachings of Islam. We have also tried to understand the real conditions — political, economic, social and intellectual — that drove them to organise in secret. We have looked at how their religious beliefs and ideas shaped their political and social activity, and how that activity in turn shaped their ideas. Throughout, we have been careful to judge their actions in light of the circumstances of their own time.

<!-- PDF page 14 | printed page 16 -->

Of course, we have not covered every underground movement in Islam. We have focused on some of them: the Kharijites, the Murji'ites, the Abbasid movement, the Mu'tazilites and the Qarmatians. We hope soon to study those we have left out of this book, such as the Fatimid movement, the groups that sought hidden meanings in religious teachings, the Brethren of Purity and others.

I should mention that this study first appeared as a series of short articles in the magazine *Rose al-Yusuf*. At the time, it prompted a great deal of criticism and discussion among writers, researchers and readers, some in favour and some opposed. I am sincerely grateful to all of them. I benefited from their comments when I came to explore the subject more fully and in greater depth. I hope I have managed to clarify what was obscure and to expand on what was too brief. I now offer the fruits of my efforts to the reader.

Whoever makes a sincere effort to arrive at the right judgement and succeeds receives two rewards. If they are mistaken, they still receive a reward for making the effort.

Success is in God's hands.

**Mahmoud Ismail**  
Cairo, March 1973

---

<!-- PDF page 15 | printed page 17 -->

## The Kharijites

### From Revolutionary Violence to an Organised Underground Movement

The Kharijites emerged as a political party out of a question that has troubled Muslims for centuries: who has the right to lead the Muslim community as its imam or caliph? The question has stirred debate and disagreement among scholars, ancient and modern. It also led to bloody conflict among Muslims and tore the Islamic community apart. That rupture became known as the Great Civil War.[^KH-1] It saw the death of the third caliph, Uthman ibn Affan, and the division of the Muslim community into bitterly opposed factions and parties. The Kharijites grew out of this conflict, and their origins are closely bound up with the famous attempt to settle the dispute between Ali ibn Abi Talib and Muawiya ibn Abi Sufyan through arbitration — a settlement entrusted to two judges.

There are clues[^KH-2] and evidence that tell a different story from the one we have heard so often. According to that familiar account, the Kharijites forced Ali to accept arbitration, only to pressure him later into rejecting it and going back on his promise to stop fighting <!-- PDF page 16 | printed page 18 --> and accept the judgement of the two arbitrators. What we can say with confidence is that those who became known as the Kharijites[^KH-3] were Ali's supporters. They were among his finest soldiers and believed more deeply than anyone in the justice of his cause. They opposed arbitration from the very beginning, because accepting it meant calling into question Ali's rightful claim to leadership. They broke with him precisely because he refused to continue fighting at Siffin and yielded to the wishes of most of his soldiers, who wanted the fighting to end when the Syrian army raised copies of the Quran on the tips of its spears.

In religious terms, then, the Kharijites stood for the “small band of believers” who would neither bargain away the truth nor compromise it to please others. It is hardly surprising that their leaders came from among the readers of the Quran and the scholars of Islamic law. They were deeply committed to the Quran and the teachings and example of the Prophet, without evasion or reinterpretation. We are told that “their foreheads were sore from long hours spent kneeling with their heads to the ground, and their hands were as calloused as a camel's knees from long hours of worship”.

Most of them came from the tribes of northern Arabia, particularly the Tamim, who had left the desert behind and settled in the two garrison towns of Kufa and Basra. The tribe played a prominent part in the uprising against Uthman, when he abandoned Abu Bakr and Umar's commitment to Islamic justice and gave his own kinsmen free rein to oppress Muslims in the provinces.

Abu Bakr had been determined to see social justice done, regardless of tribal loyalties or ethnic origin. In setting people's allowances and sharing out the revenues and spoils of conquest, he followed the Quran and the Prophet's example to the letter. As a result, the Muslim community accepted his rule, and there was no division during his reign. Umar's rule was likewise marked by an uncompromising commitment to justice, inspired by the teachings of Islam. He never departed from the Quran or the Prophet's example, but he added something else: <!-- PDF page 17 | printed page 19 --> the use of one's own judgement to interpret the teachings of Islam and apply them to new situations. Umar had to make this a third source of law, to keep pace with the changes brought about by the great Islamic conquests of Iraq, Syria and Egypt during his reign. Yet in exercising his judgement, he never lost sight of the heart of the Islamic ideal of justice. It is to his credit that neither tribal loyalties nor ethnic prejudice influenced the institutions and laws he introduced. He set an outstanding example of how to remain true to high ideals while taking real-life circumstances into account when putting them into practice. Under his rule, ethnic prejudice and old tribal grudges disappeared.

Those prejudices and grudges began to resurface, however, in the unrest that broke out under Uthman. He gave prominent companions of the Prophet who belonged to the Quraysh tribe a free hand to use their privileged position to acquire estates and build up fortunes in the conquered lands. They formed a religious ruling elite drawn from the Quraysh. Scholars of Islamic law and devout Muslims were appalled at this violation of Islamic justice, while the other tribes looked on with envy and resentment. In this way, Uthman revived tribal rivalries and bitterness that dated back to the days before Islam.

At the same time, a new elite of officials loyal to the family of Abu Sufyan emerged in provinces whose government had fallen into the hands of Uthman's Umayyad relatives. This angered the other branches of the Quraysh and rekindled the old struggle between the Umayyads and the family of Hashim. There was another important difference between Uthman and his two predecessors. Where they had seen the caliphate as a burden and a responsibility, he saw it as “a garment God had clothed him in”. This drove the scholars of Islamic law and the Quran still further away from him. They demanded his removal because he had violated the principles of the faith and departed from the letter of Islamic law. They did not hesitate to call on the Arabs in the provinces to rise up and issued religious rulings declaring the revolt lawful. Thus the Arabs of the provinces, from outside the Quraysh, made up the ranks of the revolution, while the scholars led it. The result, as we know from the history books, was Uthman's death. The rebels rushed to pledge allegiance to Ali as caliph, or rallied to his side against those who refused to recognise him, <!-- PDF page 18 | printed page 20 --> whether they came from the Quraysh elite, like Talha and al-Zubayr, or from the elite headed by Muawiya ibn Abi Sufyan.

From all this, we can see that the rise of the Kharijites was an expression of conflicts rooted in the economy and society. Those conflicts took on a religious character because they erupted around the question of who should lead the Muslim community. The Kharijites were therefore a political party committed to the social justice that Islam called for. They were not, as Wellhausen argued, simply a religious and political party. What he overlooked was that political parties in the Middle Ages, for all their outward religious trappings, had an unmistakable social dimension. This was true of the Christian world just as much as of the Islamic world.

In those days religion and the state were inseparable. In the lands of Islam, for example, the caliph held both religious and worldly authority: he was the successor to the Prophet and the ruler of the Muslims. Medieval Europe, for its part, owed its political unity to Christianity. This explains the enormous power of the Church. The struggle between the papacy and the empire over who should have supreme authority is well known. The Church itself was not only an institution run by the clergy; it was also part of the feudal system of landownership and power.

This explains why political and social questions in those days became questions of religious belief, and why disagreements were judged in terms of faith and unbelief rather than right and wrong. Muslim movements that opposed the rulers were branded apostates and heretics. Those who rose against the papacy were cut off from the Church by decrees of excommunication. The point, in short, is that what seemed to be a religious and legal dispute over the leadership of the Muslim community was in reality a struggle between social forces, reflecting the opposing interests of different classes. <!-- PDF page 19 | printed page 21 --> Ibn Khaldun[^KH-4] therefore only scratched the surface when he dismissed the conflict over the caliphate as nothing more than a “difference of judgement on purely religious questions where certainty was not possible”.

The Kharijites were one of the opposition parties in Islam, and their political ideas expressed the feelings of a large section of the population who were deeply unhappy with the caliphate. They truly deserve the name “the republicans of Islam”. While the Sunnis held that only members of the Quraysh could lead the Muslim community, and the Shia reserved that right for the family of the Prophet alone, the Kharijites insisted that every Muslim had the right to lead, regardless of ancestry or tribal affiliation.[^KH-5] Politically, they represented the democratic wing among the Islamic parties.

Their strict devotion to religion, their adherence to the Quran and the Prophet's example without reinterpretation or distortion, and their insistence on following the righteous early Muslims — especially the way the Prophet, Abu Bakr and Umar had governed — made them, in Dozy's words, “the Calvinists” or “the Puritans of Islam”.[^KH-6] Their belief in the duty to “encourage what is right and forbid what is wrong”, and their call to rise up against unjust rulers, earned them the title “the revolutionaries of Islam”. Some scholars have even called them “the Bolsheviks of Islam”, on account of the extreme position they took in declaring it lawful to kill those who disagreed with their beliefs, their practice of indiscriminate killing, and their view that anyone who committed a grave sin was an unbeliever. Yet their ideas of justice and equality reveal a distinctively Islamic form of socialist thought. For all these reasons, Muir[^KH-7] described the Kharijite creed as “revolutionary, democratic and socialist”.

The history of the Kharijites up to the closing years of the Umayyad era reflects their uncompromising and openly revolutionary beliefs. They rose against Ali ibn Abi Talib time and again <!-- PDF page 20 | printed page 22 --> and gave him no peace, with constant raids on Basra and Kufa and repeated uprisings in the eastern provinces. Ali responded to every Kharijite revolt with violence and cruelty. Many of his battles ended in the destruction of entire Kharijite armies. This explains why they plotted against his life. Ali died from two sword wounds inflicted by Abd al-Rahman ibn Muljam al-Muradi on 17 Ramadan, in the year 40 of the Islamic calendar (AH).

The uprisings continued after Ali's death. When Abd Allah ibn al-Zubayr challenged the Umayyads in the Hijaz and Iraq, he appeared sympathetic to the Kharijites' beliefs, so they joined him and fought on his side for a time. But when they began to suspect that his public sympathies concealed very different intentions, they turned against him. Many battles followed, costing the lives of thousands of Kharijites.

When Ibn al-Zubayr was killed, the Kharijites found themselves face to face with the full might of the Umayyads. There is no need to recount every act of persecution and brutality they endured under Umayyad rule. The conduct of the governors of Iraq, men such as Abd Allah ibn Ziyad and al-Hajjaj al-Thaqafi, tells us enough. They killed Kharijites “on the strength of accusation or suspicion” and sent armies after them “from one town to the next, defeating them in battle after battle”.[^KH-8] Whole branches of the movement were wiped out and disappeared from political life for good.

What weakened the Kharijites still further under the Umayyads was their division into more than twenty rival groups, each declaring all the others unbelievers. Their efforts were scattered, leaving their enemies free to hunt them down and crush their revolts. This led a historian like Wellhausen[^KH-9] to say that their politics were “anything but <!-- PDF page 21 | printed page 23 --> political”. By the closing years of the first Islamic century, they had grown so weak that carrying on their political activity openly was no longer possible. They had to change the way they fought for their cause. They abandoned open revolt at the heart of the Islamic world in favour of organising in secret, and shifted their activity to outlying regions beyond the caliphate's immediate reach.

This new period in the Kharijites' struggle is extremely difficult to study. The very nature of underground activity makes it hard to discover much about the organisation, its advocates, plans and methods. On top of that, the Kharijites suffered persecution and terrible hardships that led to the destruction of most of their books and documents. We know, for example, that the Shia Fatimids burned the Kharijites' archives and libraries in North Africa, along with the books they contained on the movement's beliefs and the history of its followers. The al-Ma'suma library in Tahert, burned by Abu Abd Allah al-Shii, is said to have held thousands of written works. Only a tiny fraction of the Kharijites' literary heritage survived the fire.

Even today, the Kharijites who live in Libya and Algeria guard their religious books carefully against outsiders. Ibn al-Nadim described their books as “hidden away” in his own day, with no way of discovering their contents. The problem is still with us. Scholars of the East such as Émile Masqueray, Percy Smith and Lewicki have tried in vain to get hold of some of these books. Arab historians who tried to do the same have had little more success: all they obtained were a few manuscripts of biographical and historical works that were already known. The original sources remain locked away within the communities themselves, which <!-- PDF page 22 | printed page 24 --> live in relative isolation in Libya's Nafusa Mountains and the Mzab Valley in the Algerian desert.[^KH-star-24]

We have little choice, then, but to piece together the scattered references and fragments that survive in chronicles, books of Islamic law and religious rulings, collections of biographies, literature and other works. From them we can build up a picture, faint though it may be, of the secret organisation the Kharijites created towards the end of the first Islamic century and the beginning of the second.

We were fortunate to discover the only document we have on this subject among a collection of handwritten religious rulings at the Egyptian National Library. No previous researcher had noticed its historical importance. The document does not give us a detailed look into the secrets of the underground movement, but it offers conclusive proof that the movement existed. It sheds some light on one of its leaders and tells us where the organisation was based and how its central leadership was connected to its advocates in the provinces. It is a letter sent by the head of the movement in Basra, Abu Ubayda Muslim ibn Abi Karima, to his followers in North Africa early in the second Islamic century. Here is part of what he wrote:[^KH-10]

> In the name of God, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful. May God bless our master Muhammad, the unlettered Prophet, and grant peace to him, his family and his companions. Your letter has reached me. You tell me how God has blessed you by bringing you together and uniting you in purpose, though there are so many around you who disagree with you. By my life, however numerous they may be, those who opposed your predecessors outnumbered them no less. Follow your predecessors’ example, despite the numbers ranged against you [the wording of this clause is uncertain]. We ask God to help you and grant you success in all your affairs, to protect us and you from their hostility, and <!-- PDF page 23 | printed page 25 --> to turn the tide against them for us, for you and for all Muslims, to heal the hearts of believers and take away their anger. By my life, I was delighted to hear what you have achieved, though it had not escaped our notice. We believe what you have written to me. May God bring every good thing to fulfilment for you, with His help and blessing…

Anyone who follows the development of Kharijite political thought will notice a profound change in their beliefs as they adapted to these new circumstances. The first thing that stands out is that new followers turned away from the views of extreme groups like the Azraqites (named after Nafi ibn al-Azraq) and the Najdites (after Najda ibn Amir al-Hanafi). Instead, they were drawn to the less extreme Sufrites, named after their leader Ziyad ibn al-Asfar, and to the moderate Ibadis, named after Abd Allah ibn Ibad.

Both groups welcomed newcomers, whom they called “emigrants”. They dispensed with the harsh tests they had once used, out of caution, to make sure those joining them were sincere and genuinely devoted to the cause. They also threw open the doors to the *mawali* — Muslims of non-Arab origin — who had previously been shut out. As we shall see, many of them went on to lead the Kharijite movement. The Sufrites even abandoned some of their old extreme beliefs, such as declaring everyone who disagreed with them an unbeliever. They now allowed their followers to associate with and marry people of other beliefs.

More important still was their acceptance of the principle of *taqiyya*[^KH-11] — the idea that one may conceal one's beliefs to avoid danger and hardship. In the past they had clung to their duty to encourage what was right and forbid what was wrong, even at the cost of their own lives. Needless to say, Ibadi political thought grew more moderate too, so much so that scholars of the different Islamic schools consider the Ibadis the closest of all such groups to Sunni Islam.[^KH-12]

<!-- PDF page 24 | printed page 26 -->

With this document and these changes in Kharijite political thought in mind, we can now study their organised underground movements with greater confidence. We should point out, however, that the information available to us concerns the Ibadis alone. What little we know about the Sufrites comes from passing references in Ibadi books. Scant and scattered as these references are, they do shed some light on the early stages of the movement and show that the Sufrites were organised along similar lines to the Ibadis.

We know so little about the Sufrites because all their own sources were lost when the Shia invaded their state in North Africa in 297 AH. Ubayd Allah al-Mahdi was being held prisoner in Sijilmasa, the capital of the Sufrite state. Despite the pleas of the Fatimid commander Abu Abd Allah al-Shii, its ruler refused to release him. After capturing Sijilmasa, al-Shii took particularly harsh revenge on the Sufrites. He let his soldiers sack the city and then set it ablaze, destroying the whole of the Sufrites' written heritage. The survivors scattered across North Africa, and their school of thought died out for good.[^KH-star-26]

In any case, we know for certain that Basra was the centre of the Ibadi branch of the Kharijite movement. The Sufrite branch was probably based in the Jazira, a region north of Iraq, since most of the Sufrites' activity in the eastern Islamic world took place there. At first, there was some cooperation between the two movements. We know, for example, that the representatives of the Ibadis and Sufrites reached North Africa “on the back of the same camel”.[^KH-13] But that harmony soon faded. Rivalry took the place of cooperation, and each group began working towards its own goals. When their interests clashed as they competed <!-- PDF page 25 | printed page 27 --> for influence in the same regions, war broke out, ending with the Ibadis gaining the upper hand. Faced with a danger that threatened them both, however, the two groups set aside their quarrels and agreed to live in peace.

As we have seen, Basra was the centre of the Ibadi movement. From there, the movement sent out its representatives, known as “bearers of knowledge”, to the provinces once they had been taught the principles of the cause and how to spread its message. It goes without saying that the organisation operated in secrecy. Its meetings took place in cellars beneath the homes of elderly women so as not to attract suspicion. Those attending often dressed as women, while lookouts around the meeting place warned them of anything that might cause concern. The representatives were educated in the beliefs of the movement and the various fields of knowledge of their time, both those based on religious tradition and those based on reason. They also learned about administration and government, how to win people over to the cause and how to organise them into small groups, or “cells”. Once the movement's elders were satisfied with their training, they supplied them with money and provisions for the journey to the regions where they were to spread its message. From there they began their work. They wrote regularly to the head of the organisation in Basra to keep him informed of their progress and received his instructions and advice on the problems they encountered. The central organisation also had people watching over their activities to judge their loyalty and see whether they were carrying out its instructions. We can infer this from Abu Ubayda's letter quoted above, which suggests that the news sent by his representative had already reached him.

It seems likely that Jabir ibn Zayd, one of the leading Ibadi thinkers, founded the movement's organisation in the last decade of the first Islamic century. It reached its most developed form under his successor, Abu Ubayda Muslim ibn Abi Karima. He was a remarkably gifted scholar, <!-- PDF page 26 | printed page 28 --> a man of great learning and wisdom who also understood politics and all its subtleties. His famous debates with Wasil ibn Ata, leader of the Mu'tazilites in Basra, are proof enough of his learning. His political judgement can be seen in the fact that his movement was not exposed, despite his imprisonment and torture during the governorship of al-Hajjaj al-Thaqafi.

During Abu Ubayda's lifetime, Ibadi representatives travelled to Khurasan, the Arabian Peninsula and North Africa. Those sent to Khurasan failed in their mission because the inhabitants were drawn to Shi'ism. They did win some followers, but their numbers were too small to establish an openly declared Ibadi government and confront their enemies. They took the risk of rising against Nasr ibn Sayyar, the last Umayyad governor of Khurasan. All their venture achieved was to expose their movement, making them easy prey, not long afterwards, for the armies of Abu Muslim of Khurasan, leader of the Abbasid revolution.

The Ibadis were more successful in Oman, where they founded a state of their own. Abu Ubayda sent them considerable sums of money and additional arms. They went on to conquer Yemen and advance into the Hijaz, where the Kharijite leader Abu Hamza delivered his famous historical speech from the Prophet's pulpit.[^KH-14] But the Umayyads sent armies from Syria, forcing him to withdraw from the Hijaz and Yemen and return to Oman and Hadramawt. Their state survived there for a long time. Ibadi communities later migrated from there to East Africa, where they played a part in spreading Islam among the local population. Ibadi communities still live in what is now Tanzania.

In North Africa, political, economic and social conditions were ripe for the spread of the Kharijite cause. Politically, the region had fallen into chaos and unrest under the Umayyads because of the conflict between the northern Qaysi and southern Yemeni Arabs, <!-- PDF page 27 | printed page 29 --> which the Umayyad caliphs kept alive by siding with one faction against the other.[^KH-15]

What is more, the Umayyads favoured Arabs in general over the Berbers, the original inhabitants of the region. Though the Berbers had embraced Islam, they were not treated as equals. They were regarded as *mawali*, and denied their rightful share of the spoils of war and the revenues of conquest, even though they fought alongside the Arabs in the campaigns launched by the governors of North Africa in the Mediterranean islands and Muslim Spain.[^KH-16] Worse still, these governors treated North Africa as enemy territory, sending out military expeditions that plundered the country and took its people captive.[^KH-17] Instead of following the teachings of Islam by lifting the poll tax on those who had converted to Islam, they burdened the population with taxes that had no lawful basis.

The caliph Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz tried to put an end to the abuses of the Umayyad administration in North Africa. He ordered that the poll tax be lifted from Muslim Berbers, that Berber women who had been enslaved be set free, and that the land be returned to its owners, who would harvest its produce and pay a fixed land tax. He hoped in this way to restore the Berbers' trust in the government.[^KH-18] But his successors turned their backs on this wise policy. After his reign, the governors of North Africa resorted to “mistreating the people and abusing their power in collecting alms and dividing the spoils”. They claimed a fifth of the Berbers themselves as the share of the state, maintaining that they were spoils belonging to the Muslims,[^KH-19] “whether they had become Muslims or not”. They went to extremes in burdening the population with levies and taxes to satisfy the caliphs in Damascus, whose appetite <!-- PDF page 28 | printed page 30 --> for the luxuries of North Africa and enslaved Berber girls had become insatiable.[^KH-20]

Small wonder, then, that the Berbers had had enough of Umayyad rule. They longed to resist the dynasty after all the humiliation and oppression they had suffered. North Africa was fertile ground in which to sow the seeds of the Kharijite cause.

The Ibadi organisation in Basra therefore took a particular interest in North Africa. Abu Ubayda, the head of the movement, sent his representative Salama ibn Said there. He succeeded in winning over the tribes of eastern North Africa to the Ibadi cause. His successor made the Nafusa Mountains a “place of refuge and migration”, after the Berbers of Nafusa embraced the Ibadi faith and rallied to its defence.

Meanwhile, Sufrite beliefs were spreading among the tribes of central and western North Africa. Ikrima, a client of Ibn Abbas and the head of the Sufrites in the region, played a prominent part in this. From his base in Kairouan, he secretly contacted tribal leaders and persuaded them to embrace the Sufrite faith. He won most of them to his cause, and they in turn became advocates of the movement among their own tribes. As a result, the Sufrite faith spread rapidly among the Berbers. It also attracted some oppressed ethnic minorities who were not Berbers, such as the people known as the *Afariqa* (the descendants of older North African communities)[^KH-21] <!-- PDF page 29 | printed page 31 --> and Black Africans,[^KH-22] as well as a number of prominent Arabs.

The Sufrite faith thus came to dominate much of central and western North Africa, and the Sufrites were now in a position to launch a revolt. In 121 AH, Maysara “the water carrier”, leader of the rebels, rose up against unjust rulers and inflicted a series of defeats on the Umayyad armies. The revolt continued until 140 AH, when the rebels succeeded in founding an independent state in the south-west of the region: the state of the Midrarid dynasty, with its capital at Sijilmasa.[^KH-23] It is worth noting that its founder was a Black man named Isa ibn Yazid al-Aswad. This speaks volumes about the sincerity of the Kharijites and their commitment to democratic principles, in practice as well as in theory.

The Ibadi movement, on the other hand, suffered a series of setbacks for a time. The reason was that its activity centred on eastern North Africa, close to Egypt, so the Umayyad caliphate could quickly send armies to crush its revolts. The Ibadis went on preparing for revolution patiently, biding their time until conditions were favourable. They strengthened their ties with the central organisation and worked together towards the revolt while waiting for the right moment. That moment came with the fall of the Umayyad state and the rise of the <!-- PDF page 30 | printed page 32 --> Abbasid caliphate in 132 AH. The Abbasids moved the capital of the caliphate from Damascus to Baghdad. Preoccupied with affairs in the East, they had little time to watch over events in North Africa. The rise of their own state brought a host of problems that demanded all their efforts. This gave the underground Ibadi movement some room to act. Four of the Ibadi leaders of North Africa travelled to Basra, where they spent five years preparing for the revolt. Before they returned, Abu Ubayda advised them to put one of their number, Abu al-Khattab al-Maafiri, in charge, after preparing him for the role. He also entrusted the administration of justice to Ismail al-Ghadamisi, who had shown great skill in Islamic law and in issuing legal rulings.[^KH-24]

The group returned to North Africa in 140 AH, and its leaders got to work straight away. They met secretly with the tribal chiefs outside Tripoli. At the appointed time their armies gathered outside the city and took it by surprise in an episode reminiscent of the fall of Troy. According to the Ibadi sources,[^KH-25] the soldiers had hidden in sacks carried by camels, and entered the city in what appeared to be a trading caravan. When the caravan reached the marketplace, the soldiers emerged with their weapons drawn, proclaiming, “Judgement belongs to God alone, and obedience to Abu al-Khattab alone”. Whether this account is true or a product of the imagination of Kharijite historians, the city fell without resistance. From Tripoli, the Ibadi armies moved on to Kairouan, the seat of government, and captured it too. But the fall of Kairouan enraged the caliphate. It sent its armies from Egypt, killing and capturing many Ibadis.

The blow the Kharijites suffered was very nearly a crushing one. They went <!-- PDF page 31 | printed page 33 --> underground once more, and after Abu al-Khattab's death they secretly pledged allegiance to one of their own as imam, giving him the title “imam of defence”. Abu al-Khattab's deputy, a Persian named Abd al-Rahman ibn Rustam, fled into the desert. Some tribes rallied around him and helped him declare an openly established imamate once more in 162 AH. Abd al-Rahman succeeded in founding an Ibadi state in central North Africa, centred on the city of Tahert, capital of the Rustamid state.

The central organisation in Basra was delighted at the founding of this state. It saw in it the beginnings of a great Ibadi state that would one day unite East and West. The Kharijites in the East therefore did everything in their power to support the young state. They sent its imam large sums of money to help him consolidate his authority. The two sides remained closely connected throughout Abd al-Rahman's reign.

When his son Abd al-Wahhab succeeded him, however, quarrels and divisions broke out between the new ruler and the Ibadi leaders. They were alarmed to see their imamate turn into a hereditary monarchy. The Ibadis of the East repeatedly tried to heal the rift and settle the dispute. They sent religious rulings in the hope of bringing the two sides together, and dispatched representatives with the same mission. But the rebels were devoted to their democratic political beliefs and refused to yield. They continued to oppose the imamate until the imam's death. When one of his sons then inherited power, revolt followed revolt without respite, and many breakaway groups emerged within the Ibadi faith. The central organisation lost faith in the Rustamid imamate now that it had become a hereditary monarchy. From that time on, ties between the two sides were severed.[^KH-star-33] Religious divisions continued to weaken the <!-- PDF page 32 | printed page 34 --> Rustamid state until it fell to the Shia Fatimids in 297 AH.

Whatever else we may say about it, the Kharijites' success in establishing a state in Oman and Hadramawt and three states in North Africa is clear evidence of the effectiveness of their underground organisation compared with the reckless uprisings that had served only to weaken their movement.

---

<!-- PDF page 33 | printed page 35 -->

## The Murji'ites

### From Defending the Rulers to Revolution

Ideas change as society changes. We have already seen this clearly in the political thinking of the two wings of the left: the moderate Shia and the more radical Kharijites. But something particularly striking happens when beliefs that once served to justify the existing order turn into revolutionary ideas, rooted in the life of society and seeking to transform it. We can see this very clearly in the evolution of the Murji'ites' political thought.

Those who have studied the history of the Islamic religious movements have recognised the political meaning of Shia beliefs. Yet few have noticed the same thing about the parties of the centre — the Murji'ites and the Mu'tazilites. The Murji'ites have been studied simply as a religious school concerned with questions of faith and unbelief.[^MJ-1] Similarly, studies of the Mu'tazilites have rarely gone beyond abstract theological debates about the nature of God.[^MJ-2]

<!-- PDF page 34 | printed page 36 -->

Perhaps the political side of Murji'ite thought has been neglected because the movement emerged relatively late in history. Its political role also seemed small beside the growing influence of the Shia and the Kharijites. The social groups that made up the centre were not revolutionary by nature. Nor did they have strong leaders who could compete for influence with the Umayyad right or with either the moderate or the radical left. So they cared far less about changing the existing order than about protecting their own interests. A victory for the left threatened those interests, and the Murji'ites sympathised with the right without openly joining it.

Once the right had won, they threw their weight behind it. They defended its rule rather than joining the left in opposing it. That explains why the Murji'ites and the Mu'tazilites stayed out of the political struggle during the first half of the first century AH, when the Kharijites and the Shia were in the thick of it.

Like parties of the centre in any age, the Murji'ites kept a close eye on the struggle between right and left. When the right began to reel under blow after blow from the Kharijites and the Shia, the Murji'ites swung towards the left, hoping to share in the fruits of what looked like an imminent victory. They joined many of the revolts against the Umayyads and became their enemies. But when these revolts were crushed, they turned to independent, organised underground work. Like the left before them, the Murji'ites looked to the great masses of the *mawali* — Muslims of non-Arab origin living under Arab patronage. They reshaped their beliefs into a revolutionary cause that spoke to the changing needs of society. Once their movement, with its new social message, had taken root, they rose in revolt. The result was to weaken the right in the East and pave the way for the eventual downfall of the Umayyads.

The development of Murji'ite political thought was thus a response to the changing realities of society.[^MJ-3] <!-- PDF page 35 | printed page 37 --> It was also a change made necessary by the very nature of the movement: a party that seized opportunities to advance its own interests and took whatever position suited them, as parties of the centre generally do.

Let us follow the changes in Murji'ite beliefs and political activity, from their beginnings as a neutral party to their support for the right, their growing sympathy for the left, and their eventual emergence as a revolutionary party of the left in its own right.

At first, the Murji'ites remained politically neutral between right and left. Their social origins were quite different from those of the classes that made up the left. It is telling that it was Nafi ibn al-Azraq, the most radical of the leaders of the far left — the Kharijites — who first gave them the name Murji'ites.[^MJ-4] They had stood aside when Muslims fell out with one another towards the end of Uthman's rule. They took no part in the revolt against him; nor, after his death, did they join Muawiya ibn Abi Sufyan and the supporters of Uthman in seeking to avenge him. Instead, they cautiously waited to see how the struggle would turn out.

It is extremely difficult to trace the origins of this opportunistic neutrality. The earliest writings setting out Murji'ite beliefs are lost. Their later books have not survived either, because the movement was persecuted during the Abbasid age — the very age when the history of Islam was being written down.[^MJ-5]

The books of the Kharijites and the Shia are full of stories, traditions and sayings attributed to the Prophet — some fabricated or falsely attributed — in which one can glimpse the first seeds of their ideas, before they emerged as political parties. We have no similar accounts to shed light on the origins of Murji'ite thought. Ahmad Amin,[^MJ-6] however, suggested that signs of this way of thinking could already be found among some of the Prophet's earliest companions, such as Abu Bakra, Abd Allah ibn <!-- PDF page 36 | printed page 38 --> Umar, Imran ibn Husayn and others who remained neutral in the conflict that broke out towards the end of Uthman's caliphate. Amin bases his argument on a saying of the Prophet reported by Abu Bakra:

> There will be times of civil strife. The one who sits still will be better off than the one who walks, and the one who walks will be better off than the one who rushes into it. When it comes, let those who have camels go to their camels, those who have sheep go to their sheep, and those who have land go to their land.

In Amin's view, then, this neutrality had a religious basis: uncertainty about where the right lay in a difficult dispute.[^MJ-7] He believed that this attitude continued to guide the Murji'ites during the Great Civil War, and drew on an account given by Ibn Asakir:

> The doubters were those who had been away on military campaigns. When they returned to Medina after Uthman was killed, they found that the people, whom they had last known to be united and free of disagreement, were divided. They said, “We left you united, without a quarrel among you. We have come back to find you at odds. Some of you say that Uthman was killed unjustly, and that he and his supporters had the stronger claim to justice. Others say that Ali and his supporters were in the right. We trust them all and believe them. We will not disown or curse either of them, nor testify against them. We leave their case to God, so that God himself may judge between them.”[^MJ-8]

It seems that Amin was influenced in forming this view by Goldziher,[^MJ-9] who suggested that “the way the Murji'ites thought can be traced back to a moderate view that emerged in an earlier age ... They took no part in the dispute and left it to God to judge the matter.”

It seems to us that this neutrality, arising from doubts about <!-- PDF page 37 | printed page 39 --> which side was in the right, does not really describe the Murji'ites. It applies instead to a number of leading companions of the Prophet, such as Abd Allah ibn Umar and Saad ibn Abi Waqqas, who wanted no part in the dispute and preferred to keep their religious lives free of it. The Murji'ites, by contrast, kept out of it, I believe, because they wanted to stay safe in this world and protect their own interests. One group among them, as the saying of the Prophet quoted above suggests, thought it wiser to look after their worldly affairs, their farms and their flocks. Another group had been loyal to Uthman before his death and did not join the rebels. But they did not side with Muawiya either, for fear of endangering their interests, since at first the struggle seemed to be going Ali's way. This is apparent from the account by Ibn Asakir quoted earlier.

Their neutrality was a cautious, calculated wait for an opportunity. When the struggle left Ali in a weaker position and Muawiya's star began to rise, they were quick to come to his aid. They recognised the legitimacy of his caliphate and justified the rule of whoever held power. Al-Nawbakhti was therefore entitled to brand them as “the Hashwiyya [a disparaging label], followers of kings and helpers of whoever prevails. They were called Murji'ites because they befriended all the conflicting parties and claimed that everyone who prayed towards Mecca was a believer, provided they openly professed the faith.”[^MJ-10]

We can find evidence of this in the sources. The first group included those whom al-Khayyat[^MJ-11] called the *Nawabit* — in his words, “supporters of Uthman who believed that human actions were predetermined and that judgement should be deferred.” The other group included people like Ghaylan ibn Abi Ghaylan of Damascus. His father had been a client of Uthman ibn Affan and joined Muawiya in Syria when the latter secured the caliphate.[^MJ-12] Because both groups were opportunists seeking to advance themselves, <!-- PDF page 38 | printed page 40 --> al-Zabidi[^MJ-13] called them *Hashwiyya*. As al-Nawbakhti says, the Hashwiyya are followers of kings and supporters of whoever comes out on top.

When Muawiya won the struggle, the Murji'ites followed him and became a pillar of his rule. They put their beliefs to work in justifying his caliphate and persuading Muslims to obey him. Their view of what happens to a person who commits a grave sin makes this clear. The Kharijites declared such a person an unbeliever. The Mu'tazilites held that the person occupied a position somewhere between belief and unbelief. The Murji'ites, however, refused to pass judgement, leaving the matter to God.[^MJ-14] (This principle of deferring judgement is known as *irja*.) In taking this view, they stood apart from all the opposition parties, which regarded the Umayyads as having seized the caliphate unlawfully.

They also held that faith consisted in affirming belief in words, not in deeds.[^MJ-15] In other words, what a person does is neither an essential part of faith nor included in its meaning.[^MJ-16] This explains how the Murji'ites could refrain from opposing rulers and submit to them without believing that their own faith suffered in any way from such passivity. The Kharijites took the opposite view: they believed it was essential to oppose their enemies, in keeping with the principle of “enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong”.

The Murji'ites held that “any sin short of worshipping other gods alongside God can be forgiven, and if a person dies believing in the oneness of God, the sins and wrongs they have committed will not harm them”.[^MJ-17] Here we have a clear justification for the Umayyads' seizure of the right to rule by deceit, assassination, bribery and intimidation. For this reason, deferring judgement was the “religion of kings”. Unlike the other religious movements, the Murji'ites did not oppose the Umayyad government. Instead, they recognised its legitimacy and called for <!-- PDF page 39 | printed page 41 --> obedience to it, “and that in itself was support enough”.[^MJ-18]

This helps explain why the Umayyads dealt so ruthlessly with every Islamic religious movement except the Murji'ites. There can be little doubt that the early Umayyad rulers looked after the leaders of this school. Some lived in Damascus,[^MJ-19] even as the leaders of other movements suffered persecution and all manner of torture. The Kharijites faced such harsh treatment at the hands of the governors of Iraq that they were forced to shift their activity eastwards. The Shia in Kufa, meanwhile, were persecuted and made to help their governors fight the Kharijites. The aim was to set the two movements against each other, weaken both and rid the Umayyads of their bitterest enemies in one stroke.

The Murji'ite leaders enjoyed a peaceful life in Basra, free from harassment by its governors. They set about spreading their beliefs among the city's people. Hassan ibn Bilal al-Muzani was the first to preach the doctrine there,[^MJ-20] and his message was warmly received. The people of Basra found in the idea of deferring judgement just what they had been looking for. They were weary of war. After the horrors of the battles of the Camel, Siffin and al-Nukhayla, they longed for safety and peace. Murji'ite teaching gave their desire to live quietly and avoid further conflict a religious justification.[^MJ-21] It also served as an antidote <!-- PDF page 40 | printed page 42 --> to the excesses of the radical movements,[^MJ-22] whose beliefs had little appeal to them. Small wonder that most turned to the Murji'ites and “occupied themselves with their own affairs”,[^MJ-23] regardless of the kind of government in power. By the standards of their faith, that government was neither a rebel nor a government that had gone astray.[^MJ-24] All this brings out the concern for practical advantage, in the spirit of the philosopher Jeremy Bentham, that characterised the Murji'ites while they defended the existing order — or, at the very least, a tendency to put their own interests above all else. It is a familiar feature of parties of the centre in every age.

The Murji'ites could not remain loyal to the right for long, however. Anger against the Umayyads soon reached boiling point, and social problems grew ever more acute.[^MJ-25] The successors of the devout caliph Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz turned their backs on the reforms he had introduced. The left — the Kharijites and the Shia — struck ever harder, despite the ruthless repression of the Umayyads. The dynasty's downfall now looked inevitable. The Murji'ites sensed that the tide was turning in favour of the left. They turned with it and sympathised with the new movements fighting for justice.

Ideas that had once justified the existing order now became a programme of reform, in tune with the new realities of society. Naturally, reform became the defining feature of Murji'ite thought in this period. They called for “putting the Quran and the Prophet's teachings into practice and adopting the principle of consultation in government”.[^MJ-26] What, then, were the defining features of this new phase, in its ideas, its social basis and its political activity?

In the world of ideas, we see Murji'ite beliefs taking a revolutionary turn. They were influenced by the Kharijites and the Shia, who called for revolt against injustice. They also adopted some of the <!-- PDF page 41 | printed page 43 --> Qadarites' ideas about human freedom. The *mawali*, the non-Arab Muslims living under Arab patronage, flocked to the movement and became the main force behind this new form of Murji'ite belief. This was a momentous social change, and it had far-reaching political consequences: the Murji'ites took part in movements against the Umayyads, including those led by Abd al-Rahman ibn al-Ashath and Yazid ibn al-Muhallab.

We believe this change began in the 60s of the first century AH and was centred on the city of Kufa, just as Basra had earlier been the centre of the movement's defence of the established order. Kufa offered ideal conditions for the growth of the Murji'ites' new ideas. The different religious schools competed in an atmosphere of learning and tolerance. Their leaders debated and tested their ideas against one another in “an environment that respected knowledge, the demands of religious law and the rules of reasoned thought”.[^MJ-27] The Murji'ites were able to draw on these schools as they developed their new outlook.

They joined all the opposition parties in holding that the leadership of the Muslim community could only be established by the agreement of the community as a whole.[^MJ-28] That meant denying the Umayyads the right to rule, since not everyone recognised the legitimacy of their caliphate. The Murji'ites borrowed the democratic political ideas of the Kharijites, declaring that someone outside the tribe of Quraysh could legitimately lead the community and that anyone who upheld the Quran and the Prophet's teachings had the right to do so.[^MJ-29]

They also abandoned their earlier beliefs, which had separated deeds from faith in order to justify the established order. Following the Kharijites and the Qadarites, they now insisted that action was essential to faith: a believer had to stand up against wrongdoing and seek to put things right. “Whoever has truly and sincerely surrendered to God in their heart and loves Him will not disobey Him by committing a sin.”[^MJ-30] Action, then, is inseparable from faith and flows naturally from it, <!-- PDF page 42 | printed page 44 --> especially since, according to their belief, “faith neither increases nor decreases”.[^MJ-31] In its new form, Murji'ite teaching thus set a higher standard for faith, one that demanded action. This made the Murji'ites, in the words of a modern scholar, a strict religious school.[^MJ-32] We can trace the roots of this strictness to the Kharijites, who insisted on enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong. Perhaps it was this convergence between the two schools that led people to call the Murji'ites of that period “the Kharijite Murji'ites”.

The Murji'ites were also influenced by Shia thought. From the Shia they adopted the principle of interpreting religious texts beyond their literal meaning,[^MJ-33] which gave them a degree of flexibility in adapting to the changing realities of society. They also drew on the ideas of *taqiyya* (concealing one's beliefs under threat) and the awaited Mahdi, the leader expected to restore justice. We will examine both in more detail later. Some Murji'ite leaders, indeed, were quite willing to profess Shia sympathies openly for political reasons.[^MJ-34] They also sympathised with the Prophet's family in spite of the brutal repression of the Umayyad governors.[^MJ-35]

<!-- PDF page 43 | printed page 45 -->

The Murji'ites had no qualms about adopting some of the ideas of the Qadarites, the forerunners of the Mu'tazilites, and turning their backs on their own earlier beliefs. “They gave up the belief that people have no choice in their actions in favour of human freedom, and forged a close bond between faith and reason.” This is clear from the following statement:

> Faith concerns what reason tells us must be done. What reason tells us we may leave undone is no part of faith.[^MJ-36]

Both action and reasoned thought were thus necessary to faith. Merely believing in the oneness of God without thinking it through did not amount to faith.[^MJ-37] Some Murji'ite groups also adopted the Qadarites' belief that the Quran was created rather than eternal.[^MJ-38] It is hardly surprising that some scholars of the Islamic religious movements confused the Murji'ites with the Mu'tazilites,[^MJ-39] calling them “the Mu'tazilite Murji'ites”. Mistaken though this was, it shows just how deeply Mu'tazilite thought had influenced the movement at this stage.[^MJ-40]

In short, in its new revolutionary form, the Murji'ite movement had become the third party opposing the Umayyad caliphate, alongside the Kharijites and the Shia. Its once moderate views had become radical, and the very principles it had once used to justify the established order had become revolutionary. So much so that al-Nakhai declared: “Given their numbers, I fear the Murji'ites pose a greater danger to this community than the Azariqa.”[^MJ-41] And the Azariqa, as we know, were the most radical and revolutionary of all the Kharijite groups.

<!-- PDF page 44 | printed page 46 -->

The movement could never have become a revolutionary force until the *mawali* embraced it. They joined the opposition parties because they wanted to give their revolt a legitimate basis in religious principles. They were divided among the three opposition parties. Ibn Saad gives us a revealing account of this:[^MJ-42]

> The jurist Ibn Abi al-Jad al-Ghatafani had a client with six sons. Two were Shia, two were Murji'ites, and two held the views of the Kharijites.

The *mawali* of Kufa were the first to take up Murji'ite beliefs. They learned them from their Arab patrons, who were at odds with the Umayyad government because it favoured the Arabs of Syria over those of Iraq. Some leaders of the Iraqi Arabs therefore turned to revolt in their struggle for equal treatment. They spread the same demand among their Persian clients under the banner of the Murji'ite movement. Muhammad ibn al-Ashath ibn Qays, himself a Murji'ite, was bitterly opposed to the Umayyads and fought against them in the ranks of Musab ibn al-Zubayr. His son Abd al-Rahman inherited that bitterness and went on to lead the famous revolt that we will examine shortly.

Umar ibn Qays al-Masir learned Murji'ite teachings from Muhammad ibn al-Ashath and spread them among the *mawali*.[^MJ-43] Dharr ibn Abd Allah ibn Zurara al-Hamdani, who inherited his father's hatred of the Umayyads and their governors in Iraq,[^MJ-44] and Amr ibn Murra ibn Abd Allah al-Murari were also leading figures in spreading Murji'ite beliefs among the *mawali* of Kufa.[^MJ-45] It was from Iraqi Arabs such as these that the *mawali* of Kufa learned the doctrines of the movement. Qays al-Masir, a captive from Daylam and a client of Ali ibn Abi <!-- PDF page 45 | printed page 47 --> Talib, was the first of the city's *mawali* to embrace them.[^MJ-46] His son Umar, a client of the Thaqif tribe, learned the beliefs from him. He studied their fundamental principles and their details so thoroughly that he became the leader of the Murji'ite group known as the Masiriyya.[^MJ-47] Ubayd al-Muktib, leader of the Ubaydiyya group, was a client of the Dabba tribe.[^MJ-48] Ghassan of Kufa, head of the Ghassaniyya group, was also one of the *mawali*. He lived at the same time as Abu Hanifa and was said to hold views similar to his.[^MJ-49]

Kufa had thus become the centre of the new Murji'ite movement. Here, the hopes of the *mawali* for an end to the injustices of Umayyad rule came together with the Iraqi Arabs' desire to be treated on an equal footing with the Arabs of Syria. It was natural that many of the Murji'ites of Basra, angered by the policies of al-Hajjaj, would move to Kufa.[^MJ-50] Once they had grown strong enough, they turned to revolt.

There is no shortage of evidence that the Murji'ites took part in the revolt of Abd al-Rahman ibn al-Ashath (80–84 AH).[^MJ-51] Among their leaders who joined it were Amir al-Shabi, Muhammad ibn al-Saib al-Kalbi[^MJ-52] and Abd al-Rahman ibn Abi Layla. Great numbers of the *mawali* also fought alongside the Iraqi Arabs in Ibn al-Ashath's army, known as the “Army of Peacocks”, which comprised a hundred thousand Arabs and as many *mawali*. The Murji'ites pledged their allegiance to Ibn al-Ashath on the basis of “the Book of God and the teachings of His Prophet, and the removal of leaders who have gone astray”.

<!-- PDF page 46 | printed page 48 -->

The rebels gathered in Kufa and defeated the armies of al-Hajjaj in several battles. The caliph Abd al-Malik ibn Marwan tried to win over the Arabs with concessions that excluded the *mawali*. He offered to dismiss al-Hajjaj, give the Iraqi Arabs the same state allowances as the people of Syria, and let Ibn al-Ashath govern a province of his choice. But the *mawali* insisted that the caliph himself must go. The caliph therefore sent troops from Syria to reinforce al-Hajjaj, who defeated the rebels at Dayr al-Jamajim, outside Kufa, and killed many of them. He forced the prisoners to declare themselves unbelievers, and many did so out of fear and to conceal their true beliefs.

Ibn al-Ashath's revolt was more than a clash between Iraq and Syria, as Wellhausen believed.[^MJ-53] Nor was it merely a struggle between the Qaysi and Yemeni tribal groupings, or simply the product of one man's ambitions. We cannot overlook the part played by the *mawali*, who rose up demanding equal access to state allowances and the right to be included on the official register of those receiving them. All these groups shared one aim: opposition to Umayyad rule. Murji'ite teaching, now transformed into a revolutionary creed, was the religious bond that held them together. This was, then, a social revolution clothed in religious language. Perhaps that explains why al-Hajjaj insisted that the defeated rebels declare themselves unbelievers and was so determined to drive the *mawali* out of Kufa and send them back to their home regions. Ibn Abd Rabbih reports[^MJ-54] that al-Hajjaj berated them, saying: “You are foreign barbarians, non-Arabs; your own villages are where you belong.” He then broke up their communities and sent them wherever he pleased, marking each man's hand with the name of the town to which he was being sent.

Just as the Murji'ites joined the coalition led by Ibn al-Ashath against the Umayyads, they played much the same role in supporting the movement of <!-- PDF page 47 | printed page 49 --> Yazid ibn al-Muhallab (100–102 AH). They gave this revolt a revolutionary character, socially as well as religiously. The similarities between the two revolts are striking. Personal motives played a part in Ibn al-Muhallab's movement, too, given the hostility between him and the caliph Yazid ibn Abd al-Malik.[^MJ-55] The rivalry between the Qaysis and the Yemenis was also very much in evidence: Yazid ibn Abd al-Malik had cut the Yemenis' allowances to half what the Qaysis received.[^MJ-56] Great numbers of *mawali* in southern Iraq joined the revolt.[^MJ-57] It received the blessing of prominent Murji'ites such as al-Sumayda al-Kindi, Abu Ru'ba and Thabit Qutna, who were followed by the mass of Iraq's Murji'ites.[^MJ-58]

Murji'ite principles were plainly reflected in the demands of the revolt: justice and equality, an end to obedience to the Umayyad caliph, and a return to government by consultation. Ibn al-Muhallab received pledges of allegiance based on the Book of God and the teachings of His Prophet.[^MJ-59] He managed to take Basra and the adjoining areas of Persia and Ahwaz, but was soon defeated and killed <!-- PDF page 48 | printed page 50 --> when the caliph sent his Syrian armies against him. The Murji'ites suffered severe persecution and brutal repression, and their leaders endured the harshest ordeals.[^MJ-60]

It had become clear that the Murji'ite doctrine, once a source of support for the Umayyad government, had turned into a cause that gave its backing to movements against it. The government went to great lengths to hunt down the Murji'ites and wipe them out, especially under Hisham ibn Abd al-Malik, who imposed a harsh governor on Iraq, Khalid ibn Abd Allah al-Qasri.[^MJ-61] Hisham also persecuted the Murji'ite leaders in Syria, putting some of them to death, including Ghaylan of Damascus.[^MJ-62] Khalid followed much the same course, killing Murji'ites and pursuing the remnants of the movement. He is said to have personally slaughtered al-Jad ibn Dirham, a prominent figure of the movement, on the morning of the Feast of Sacrifice, like a sheep being sacrificed.[^MJ-63]

After these ordeals, the Murji'ites had to change the way they carried on their struggle, and they did indeed begin to adopt a new policy. It seems to us that this policy had two main aims. The first was to try to bring about a transformation of the caliphate in Syria, winning the caliphs over to their beliefs and persuading them to pursue a policy of reform. The second was to build an organised underground movement in Khurasan, following the example of the Shia <!-- PDF page 49 | printed page 51 --> and the Kharijites. It seems that they worked with the Mu'tazilites towards the first of these goals, while drawing on the Hashemite movement to achieve the second. We know that the ideas of the Murji'ites in Syria had come very close to those of the Mu'tazilites. They persuaded the caliph Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz to abandon the policies of his predecessors and place Arabs and *mawali* on an equal footing in their rights and obligations. They also guided the caliph al-Walid ibn Yazid in his efforts to revive the policies of Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz after the latter's reforms had been undone following his death. Small wonder: al-Walid sought the friendship of the Qadarites and the Murji'ites[^MJ-64] and acted on the advice of their leaders.

The caliph Marwan ibn Muhammad, too, sought to tackle the mounting problems of his reign in a spirit of reform. He drew close to the Murji'ites and courted their leaders. He is even said to have embraced their beliefs, learning them from his tutor al-Jad ibn Dirham, which earned him the name Marwan al-Jadi.[^MJ-65] Perhaps the appeal of the Murji'ite movement to the last Umayyad rulers can be explained by their desire to delay the downfall of their state. They hoped to enlist the help of the centre against the threat from the left. With this in mind, Wellhausen was quite right[^MJ-66] <!-- PDF page 50 | printed page 52 --> to say that “the devout and God-fearing rebels had gained a firm foothold and grown stronger in Syria”.
