Yet a policy of reform could not achieve its aims. The situation called for a revolution on a far greater scale. The underground movements of the Shia and the Kharijites were already making their influence felt in the eastern and western parts of the state, and in the south as well. The Murji'ites followed the example of both in organising underground. At first they tried to make Iraq the centre of their activity. They turned to Basra, their old stronghold from the days when they had defended the established order, but their message found little support there.[^MJ-67]

The Murji'ites then looked east, where the movement in support of the Prophet's family was spreading among the *mawali*. They took a similar path, adapting their religious beliefs to the circumstances of underground activity and drawing on Shia political thought. They held that a person who believed in their heart was a Muslim believer, even if they outwardly professed Judaism or Christianity.[^MJ-68] This was clearly the idea of *taqiyya*, concealing one's true beliefs when necessary. They also endorsed interpretation beyond the literal meaning of religious texts and “reading disputed passages in a figurative sense”.[^MJ-69] This gave them the flexibility to adapt their positions to changing circumstances and the needs of the moment. They took up the idea of the awaited Mahdi, too, saying he would appear to tear down the walls of Damascus and bring Umayyad rule to an end.[^MJ-70] <!-- PDF page 51 | printed page 53 --> They were preaching, even before the Abbasids, the coming of the man with the black banners, who would rise in the East and fill the earth with justice after it had been filled with oppression.[^MJ-71] Black banners had long been associated with the fight against injustice and aggression, going back to the days of the Prophet.[^MJ-72]

Whereas the Abbasid movement called for an agreed-upon leader from the family of Muhammad, the Murji'ites called for one whom the Muslims at large could agree on. This made their cause more popular. In this respect, it was closer to the “democracy” of the Kharijites than to the rule of a small elite in the Hashemite movement, which reserved the right to lead the community for the Prophet's family alone.

The efforts of Murji'ite advocates in the East therefore met with success. People flocked to Abd Allah al-Jamali al-Muradi, one of their advocates,[^MJ-73] and Abu Muawiya the Blind enjoyed the same success as his predecessor.[^MJ-74] The people of Jurjan gathered around Jawwab ibn Abd Allah al-Taymi of Kufa and learned the basic principles of the faith from him.[^MJ-75] By far the most famous of these advocates was Jahm ibn Safwan. He is credited with completing the spread of the movement in Khurasan, to such an extent that Murji'ite beliefs there came to be associated with his name.[^MJ-76] He was himself from Khurasan — either Tirmidh or Samarkand, it is said. He embraced Islam and settled in Kufa as a client of the Banu Rasib of the Azd tribe.[^MJ-77] He learned the more developed form of Murji'ite teaching from al-Jad ibn Dirham, and held that the Quran was created and that human beings were responsible for their own actions,[^MJ-78] in opposition to those who relied on the sayings of the Prophet and the traditions handed down from earlier generations.

Jahm had <!-- PDF page 52 | printed page 54 --> long debates with the leaders of other religious schools in Khurasan. He came out on top, “which drew people towards him”.[^MJ-79] He began spreading Murji'ite beliefs in Tirmidh, then moved to Balkh. He made contact with al-Harith ibn Surayj, who had come forward to lead the revolt against the Umayyads in Khurasan. Jahm became his secretary[^MJ-80] and the leading voice of his cause, reading his letters aloud at public gatherings and urging people to support his stand against Umayyad rule.[^MJ-81] Thanks to his efforts, the *mawali* embraced the beliefs of the Murji'ites and joined al-Harith's revolt.[^MJ-82] And so, before the second century AH was over, Murji'ite beliefs had come to dominate the eastern regions of the Umayyad state.[^MJ-83] Wellhausen[^MJ-84] was right to say that in Khurasan the movement was a way of bringing people together. It was above all a moral reaction against the outward show of Islam that passed for the religion of the Arab government at the time.[^MJ-85]

The Murji'ites raised inspiring demands: equality regardless of tribal ties or ethnic origins, and a return to the practice of <!-- PDF page 53 | printed page 55 --> Islamic law,[^MJ-86] founded on tolerance and consent[^MJ-87] rather than the violence and bloodshed associated with the Kharijites.[^MJ-88]

That is why the people of Khurasan rallied around al-Harith ibn Surayj, who recruited seventy thousand of them[^MJ-89] and drew on their support in launching his famous revolt. We need not dwell on the sequence of events.[^MJ-90] What matters more for our purposes is understanding the motives, nature and aims of the revolt.

We disagree with Ahmad Amin's view[^MJ-91] that the revolt arose out of tribal disputes and personal feuds. Tribal rivalry played no part whatsoever in a movement whose character was wholly social and religious. Arabs and *mawali* stood side by side in it, fighting against Arab supremacy and drawing their inspiration from <!-- PDF page 54 | printed page 56 --> Islam.[^MJ-92] It was in the name of Islam that the revolution took up the cause of justice, once the Umayyad government had strayed from the path of Islamic law. It gave voice to a revolutionary Islam, one that made those principles the very grounds on which it challenged the existing system of government. The rebels called on people to fight for God's cause against the Umayyads and their officials, and for justice against tyranny and oppression.[^MJ-93] Religious scholars came forward to urge the people to join what they regarded as a legitimate revolution and keep its spirit alive. They would say:

> Fight them, and do not feel guilty for killing them if you act with sincere conviction. Fight them for their sins, for the injustice of their rule and for their tyranny in matters of religion.[^MJ-94]

Nor was this simply an ethnic uprising in which Arabs fought Persians. The oppressed classes of both peoples came together against the Arab aristocracy of Khurasan and the members of the old Persian aristocracy who supported it. Among the rebels were dissident Arabs from the frontier cavalry and the educated religious class, the scholars of Islamic law, drawn from both the Qaysi and Yemeni groupings.[^MJ-95] Alongside them were small landowners from among the *mawali* and villagers who were impoverished peasants. On the other side stood the Umayyad Arab troops in Khurasan and the Persian landowning class known as the *dahaqin*, who formed the backbone of the Umayyad administration. The dividing line between the rebels and their enemies — between what the rebels saw as God's army and the government's army — was therefore a matter of class.

Personal ambition also falls away as an explanation once we understand the character of al-Harith ibn Surayj, the leader of the revolt. He was not driven by ambitions of his own, nor did he dream of achieving <!-- PDF page 55 | printed page 57 --> worldly glory for himself. The historical sources depict him as a devout Muslim, a man of simple habits and a reformer who devoted his life to the defence of justice. He had once fought the Turks in the ranks of the Muslims, when the war was a jihad. Later, after the Turks had embraced Islam, he fought on their side against the Muslims, when the Islamic government disregarded the teachings of Islam and strayed from the path of its law.[^MJ-96] The words al-Tabari records give us an insight into the cause to which he devoted himself: “Nothing brings my heart joy except that God should be obeyed.”[^MJ-97]

His deeds were true to his words. When Nasr ibn Sayyar, the Umayyad governor of Khurasan, tried to persuade him to abandon the revolt by sending him money, furnishings and horses, al-Harith kept none of them for himself but shared them equally among his followers. He also turned down the promise of a governorship and great wealth — the governor offered him a hundred thousand dinars. Instead, he sent him a letter:

> ... I want none of this world, none of its pleasures, nor the chance to marry women from noble Arab families. All I ask for is the Book of God, that the teachings of the Prophet be put into practice, and that good and honourable people be entrusted with public office.[^MJ-98]

Al-Tabari tells us how simply and modestly he lived: “He would sit on a pack saddle, with a coarse cushion folded for him.”[^MJ-99] His high principles and genuine chivalry are evident from another account, according to which he forbade his followers to take part in looting, apart from seizing weapons and horses.[^MJ-100] His movement therefore sought to make the Quran and the Prophet's teachings the guiding principles of government.[^MJ-101] Its aim was a government acceptable to the majority,[^MJ-102] one that would put the Islamic ideal of justice into practice.

<!-- PDF page 56 | printed page 58 -->

It is telling that he never laid claim to the caliphate himself, nor received pledges of allegiance as caliph.[^MJ-103] We therefore believe that the movement of al-Harith ibn Surayj was a political and social revolution[^MJ-104] expressed through religion, not a product of personal ambition or simply a clash of tribal and ethnic loyalties.

This explains why the rebels were prepared to stop fighting, in spite of their victories, when the caliph appeared willing to meet their demands for justice.[^MJ-105] Nor did they hesitate to resume the struggle once they realised he had broken his promises. Their guiding principle was that blood should not be shed without just cause.[^MJ-106]

That principle continued to guide them throughout their wars, which ended only when they were defeated and their leader was killed in 128 AH.[^MJ-107]

Despite its failure, the revolt of al-Harith is evidence of how thoroughly the Murji'ites had become a revolutionary movement in their own right. It was fundamentally different from the earlier revolts in which they had fought alongside Ibn al-Ashath and Ibn al-Muhallab. The revolt of al-Harith ibn Surayj truly marked a bright period in Murji'ite history, when they took up the social cause and defended it in a genuinely <!-- PDF page 57 | printed page 59 --> Islamic spirit. It was, in other words, a revolution from below, not from above.[^MJ-108]

Though the revolt was defeated, it had extremely important positive consequences, some direct and others indirect. After al-Harith was killed, the Umayyad government was forced to abandon some of its oppressive financial policies in Khurasan[^MJ-109] and adopt a gentler and more moderate approach. These concessions to the will of the rebels were a sign of things to come. The Abbasid movement undoubtedly benefited from the Murji'ite revolt in Khurasan when it brought down the Umayyad caliphate in 132 AH.

It is hardly surprising that the Hashemites, both the supporters of Ali's family and the Abbasids, welcomed the victories the rebels won against the Umayyad governors of Khurasan. Their advocates watched closely as Umayyad power faltered under the rebels' blows. They were able to see where the ruling system was vulnerable and where its military power had failed, and they drew on what they had learned when they launched their own revolution under the leadership of Abu Muslim al-Khurasani.[^MJ-110] It seems that for a time they considered joining the rebels themselves. But they thought it wiser to wait cautiously and contented themselves with urging al-Harith to press on in his struggle against the Umayyads. We can see this from a poem that al-Kumayt, the poet of the Hashemites, sent from Kufa to al-Harith ibn Surayj. He urged him to fight the Umayyads and apologised for being unable to send men and weapons. Here are some of its lines:

> Carry to the people of Marw,  
> Across all the miles that lie between us,  
> The greeting of a friend who wishes you well  
> And urges you to stand firm in what you have begun.  
> Carry also our apologies to al-Harith:  
> The people here are struggling themselves.  
> Were it not so, you would have more horses  
> From the two cities, galloping to you with their riders.  
> <!-- PDF page 58 | printed page 60 -->
> Do not lose heart, or bow to humiliation,  
> And let no one deceive you with a promise.  
> To let yourselves be cheated and accept a scoundrel's oppression  
> Is to be no better than women who sell themselves.  
> Otherwise, raise the black banners  
> Against those who have gone astray and commit injustice.

And so the Murji'ites, a party of the centre that began with moderation and went on to justify those in power out of self-interest, turned into a movement of opposition demanding reform. In the end it became every bit as revolutionary as the parties of the left. It played a major part in bringing down the Umayyad right, whose rule was finally ended by the Abbasid revolution.

---

<!-- PDF page 59 | printed page 61 -->

## The Abbasids

### Riding the Revolutionary Wave

We would naturally expect an abundance of historical sources from the Abbasid age. After all, this was a time when people were recording their own history and when science, art and literature flourished. As the machinery of government grew more sophisticated, drawing particularly on Persian experience, so too did the means of preserving official documents. The government offices were packed with countless records, including the papers of the revolution itself. Yet none of these original documents has come down to us.

We believe the Mongol invasion of Iraq was as much to blame for their disappearance as it was for the destruction of the millions of books and reference works held in the library of Baghdad's House of Wisdom. The familiar story of the Mongols crossing the Tigris on a bridge of books and papers may owe much to exaggeration and a vivid imagination. But there can be little doubt about what it conveys: the wanton destruction of the heritage of a flourishing civilisation by a brutal, warlike people.

<!-- PDF page 60 | printed page 62 -->

Even so, the writings of al-Tabari, al-Yaqubi, al-Masudi, Ibn Qutayba and al-Baladhuri remain among our most important sources for this age. Historians today can build a much clearer picture of how the Abbasid period unfolded by reading widely beyond these works, taking in books on Islamic law, religious movements, geography, literature and the lives of notable people.

For our particular study, it is literature — poetry and prose alike — that really comes into its own. We rely on it above all to recover the history of the secret Abbasid movement. Literature can give us a remarkably true picture of the social, cultural and economic life of an age. Sometimes it even offers glimpses into political affairs that are missing from the very sources where we would expect to find them. A good example is the correspondence of government officials and rulers that has survived as literature.

Ahmad Zaki Safwat gathered a wealth of these letters in his well-known collection, *Arab Letters from the Golden Ages of Arabic*. Its third volume covers the early Abbasid period. It is our main source for shedding light on the organisation of the underground movement just before the Abbasid caliphate was founded in 132 AH. It confirms precise details that we find elsewhere. More importantly, it is the only source rich in otherwise unavailable information about how the underground organisation continued to operate even after the caliphate was established. At the end of this study, we will reproduce selected passages from letters exchanged between the caliphs and the movement's leaders.

The great scholar Ibn Khaldun writes in his *Muqaddima* (Introduction)[^AB-1] that two things are necessary for the rise of a state: <!-- PDF page 61 | printed page 63 --> a strong sense of solidarity among people and a just cause. A cause without a united body of people to embrace and support it will not achieve its aim. Equally, solidarity alone is not enough to found states with “wide dominions and mighty kingdoms”. A religious cause lends greater strength to that solidarity:

> Religion sweeps away the rivalry and jealousy among people bound together by solidarity, giving them a single direction in their pursuit of what is right. Once they see their cause clearly, nothing can stand in their way. They are all heading in the same direction, they all want the same thing, and they are prepared to give their lives for it.

Ibn Khaldun's theory of how states come into being makes sense up to a point. But if we dig a little deeper, we find that the aims of a movement must go hand in hand with the living conditions and hopes of the people who unite behind it.

Unless a movement — any movement — takes up the demands of ordinary people and gives voice to their hopes, it is bound to fail. And no matter how noble its aims, it will get nowhere without popular support. How many grand ideals and visions of a perfect society have withered and died because they had no roots in the real world and could never be put into practice?

A just cause alone cannot bring about revolutionary change. Nor can a strong sense of solidarity among people achieve it on its own. What matters is the bond between the two. A revolution succeeds when the principles of the movement answer the needs of the people who rally behind it.

The Abbasid movement succeeded because it embraced justice and raised the banner of equality among people who had long suffered social injustice and ethnic discrimination. I mean the *mawali* — Muslims from Persia and Khurasan who lived under Arab patronage. What were the actual conditions that made this success possible? That is the question we will now try to answer.

<!-- PDF page 62 | printed page 64 -->

Let us begin with the people who embraced the movement and gave it their strength. What was life like for the Persians and the people of Khurasan under the Umayyads? What place did they occupy in the politics, society, economy and culture of the age?

The Persians and Khurasanis, heirs to an ancient civilisation, were shut out of government and politics. The Arabs alone held the reins of power. These other peoples lived as a subordinate class, working to support an Arab elite of soldiers and government officials who took the fruits of their labour.[^AB-2] Islam called for equality and justice between Arabs and non-Arabs alike, yet the mawali saw a glaring contradiction between the teachings of their religion and the way they were governed in its name. It was this contradiction that made them receptive to any movement that offered deliverance from Umayyad oppression and a return to the social justice prescribed by Islamic law. The new movement was bound to be religious in character, but social in its aims.

Let us look more closely at what happened. In politics, the Umayyad caliphs had departed from Islamic law. They abandoned the democratic principle of consultation and turned the caliphate into a hereditary monarchy, the exclusive possession of the Umayyad family. Most of its members devoted themselves to a life of luxury and indulgence. Men such as Yazid ibn Muawiya, Yazid ibn Abd al-Malik and al-Walid ibn Abd al-Malik preferred drinking wine, pleasure and music to seeing that justice was done and fulfilling their religious duties.

Just as the Umayyads monopolised the caliphate, the Arabs monopolised provincial governorships <!-- PDF page 63 | printed page 65 --> and military service. Out of this grew a wealthy Arab elite of officials whose fortunes were built at the expense of the mawali. They acquired palaces and estates and embezzled vast sums of tax revenue. Ubayd Allah ibn Ziyad, the governor of Iraq, tried to draw on the skills of the Persians and Khurasanis. He turned to the *dahaqin*, the great landowners, and entrusted some of them with collecting the land tax.

These men did indeed know more about tax collection and were more trustworthy. Yet the arrangement did nothing to stop Arab officials in the eastern provinces extorting money and amassing enormous fortunes. The governors turned a blind eye to the disgraceful conduct of their fellow Arabs, out of loyalty to their own people.[^AB-3] The only real result was to revive the class divisions of pre-Islamic Iran. The great landowners regained some of their old power at the expense of the working people. Eager to please their Arab masters and safeguard their new position, they subjected the peasants to ever harsher treatment and heavier financial burdens. Many farmers were driven to abandon their land and move to the towns.

An “Arab military aristocracy” emerged too. Arab soldiers regularly received generous payments from the military pay office, as well as the abundant spoils and revenues they took in their continual raids into the lands of the Turks in Central Asia. Historians tell us that most Persian and Khurasani mawali who fought in these battles were denied their rightful share. This ethnic discrimination, and the departure <!-- PDF page 64 | printed page 66 --> from the teachings of Islamic law, stirred a number of religious scholars and pious Muslims to speak out. They denounced Umayyad policy and urged the mawali to rise against it.

To make matters worse, Umayyad policy discriminated even among the Arabs themselves. The Quraysh enjoyed a special standing among the tribes because the caliphate belonged to them. Some of the Arab tribes of Khurasan deeply resented this favouritism. One poet gave voice to their feelings:

> Quraysh took the pleasures of life for themselves,  
> And used us to shield them from every dusty pass in Khurasan.  
> Would that Quraysh might wake one night  
> To find themselves swimming in the depths of a green sea!

In response, the Umayyads deliberately rekindled the old feuds and tribal rivalries between the Arabs of the north and those of the south. Their aim was to prevent the Arab tribes from uniting to dislodge Quraysh from its privileged position, and to maintain a balance of power that would keep the dynasty in office. But the revival of those rivalries damaged Arab political influence in the provinces. War after war broke out between the tribes, bringing turmoil and unrest. Khurasan was especially torn by these conflicts, and its own people suffered in the process. Their resentment towards the Arabs as a whole deepened, and they longed to see the end of Arab rule.[^AB-4]

Unjust taxation was one of the chief causes of the Khurasanis' anger at the Umayyad caliphate. Its financial policy was a flagrant violation <!-- PDF page 65 | printed page 67 --> of Islamic teachings. The authorities continued to levy the *jizya*, the tax on non-Muslims, on mawali who had embraced Islam. They devised new taxes for them that had no basis in Islamic law.[^AB-5] On top of that came the gifts they were required to present to the caliphs, governors and officials at the two festivals of Nowruz and Mihragan.[^AB-6] Then there was the humiliation tax collectors inflicted on taxpayers and the brutal treatment of those who could not meet their payments. Abu Yusuf tells us[^AB-7] that collectors forced people to carry stones or water jars around their necks, made them stand on one leg for hours, and then bound them so that they could not bow down in prayer.

The ascetic caliph Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz tried to put an end to these injustices. He abolished all the earlier laws and measures and followed Islamic law in his treatment of Muslims and protected non-Muslim communities alike. But he did not live long. His reforms were reversed, and things went back to the way they had been.

For all these reasons, the Persians and Khurasanis came to resent Umayyad rule and its policies. They rose in revolt time and again, but their uprisings lacked organisation and preparation. The Umayyad armies crushed them with brutal force, thanks to a handful of daring Arab commanders utterly devoted to the ruling family and the preservation of “Arab supremacy”. New social developments in Khurasan, however, <!-- PDF page 66 | printed page 68 --> were paving the way for enormous political changes.

The solidarity that bound the Arabs together had been weakened by the long struggle between the Yemeni and Mudar tribes. Their unity fell apart. They lost their military edge as they settled into lives of ease, luxury and pleasure. Tired of life in the military camps, they abandoned the very source of their strength. Meanwhile, Arabs and mawali had mingled, giving rise to a new generation of mixed ancestry that cared much less about loyalty to Arab blood. The solidarity of the Arabs — the very pillar of the Umayyad state — was cracking, and this hastened the state's downfall at the hands of a new force united in Khurasan. As Ibn Khaldun aptly observed,[^AB-8] “When a life of luxury and ease overtakes the people whose solidarity holds a state together, that state begins to grow old.”

At the same time, class conflict within Persian and Khurasani society itself reached its height. As we have seen, the old class of great landowners had regained its former influence. Besides collecting taxes, its members held important posts in the administration. They had become, in effect, “servants of the ruling power”. The governors naturally used them as informers and spies on their own fellow countrymen, the peasants and farmers. There is no doubt that the lives of the latter grew worse. They were prey to two sets of wealthy masters: the old Persian landowning class and the new Arab elite.

As the financial burden grew, some peasants had no choice but to leave the land and settle in the towns. Some governors forced great numbers of these migrants back to their villages, while others let them be. Some of the migrants went to work for <!-- PDF page 67 | printed page 69 --> the Arabs; others took up crafts and trades. They made good profits and formed the beginnings of an ambitious new middle class, a *bourgeoisie* eager to rise to prominence.[^AB-9] The growing exodus to the towns, meanwhile, left farms in ruin. Agricultural output fell, and land tax revenues fell with it. The state lost much of its income and was sometimes unable to pay its soldiers. They revolted more and more frequently against their governors, weakening the provincial army.

As peasants continued to pour into the towns, there simply were not enough jobs to go round. Wages fell and unemployment spread. Out of these conditions emerged a discontented working class, the beginnings of a *proletariat*, whose members joined the underground revolutionary organisations. It is hardly surprising that the wealth and cultural heritage of the new urban middle class made it the leading force in the Abbasid movement.[^AB-10] The movement drew its strength from the peasants and oppressed people of the countryside and the angry workers in the towns. They became its loyal foot soldiers. In this way, the conditions in Khurasan were ripe for the seeds of a revolution that was more social in nature than political or religious.[^AB-11]

So much for the people who rallied behind the cause. What about the movement itself?

That brings us to its leading figures, to what drove them to wage their struggle in secret, and then to the movement's slogans <!-- PDF page 68 | printed page 70 --> and aims. We will also look at how its underground organisation was structured and how it evolved as the movement encountered new difficulties.

One thing needs to be said at the outset. The Abbasid family did not lay the foundations of this movement or sow its seeds. Circumstances alone delivered into their hands the leadership of a carefully built underground organisation, complete with its own branches, organisers and followers. They reaped the harvest that their cousins, the descendants of Ali, had sown. They rode the rising tide of revolution, as the saying goes, and turned their backs on the pioneers who had endured the bitterest trials to build that organisation.

We have already looked at the emergence of political and religious parties in Islam. The Kharijites appeared as a revolutionary, democratic party in the wake of the arbitration dispute. They opposed Ali until his assassination and regarded the Umayyads as usurpers of the caliphate. Their many revolts throughout the second half of the first century AH ended in failure, driving them to take their struggle underground.

The second party, the Shia, remained loyal to Ali ibn Abi Talib. They condemned the Kharijites for rebelling against him and the Umayyads for seizing his rightful place as caliph. After Ali's death, they rallied around his son Hasan. He preferred peace and willingly gave up his claim to put an end to the civil strife. But his decision did not break up the party. Its leaders remained close to Hasan, who urged them to be patient and wait for circumstances to turn in their favour. When Hasan died, the Shia rallied around his brother Husayn. He claimed the caliphate and denounced the Umayyads for turning it into a hereditary monarchy.

Husayn's martyrdom was a victory for the Shia cause. His well-known tragic death fuelled <!-- PDF page 69 | printed page 71 --> their anger at the Umayyads. The mawali began embracing Shi'ism in large numbers, and the family of Ali came to represent popular opposition to the ruling dynasty. Like the reckless uprisings of the Kharijites, the Shia revolts were social revolutions dressed in religious language. The Umayyads had no qualms about crushing their leaders without mercy. Many members of Ali's family lost their lives, and the Shia went through a terrible ordeal.

After one disaster followed another, the Shia turned away from open rebellion for a time. They adopted a new way of carrying on their struggle through organised underground activity. Their political thinking at the time clearly reflects this change.

It is worth noting how much Shia thought as a whole drew from ancient Persian culture. This tells us a great deal about the role the Persians played in Shi'ism. They were drawn to the movement not simply because of the special standing of the Prophet's family, or because Husayn ibn Ali had married a Persian woman. The family of the Prophet represented the strongest opposition to Umayyad policy, and it had taken up the cause of justice in the true Islamic sense.

There can be no doubt that the Prophet's family understood Islam better than anyone else, was among the most devoted to its principles and was deeply committed to putting its teachings into practice. From their first imam, Ali ibn Abi Talib, they had inherited a tradition of deep religious learning and wide knowledge. Their long experience in exploring every branch of knowledge and drawing on it in the service of their cause is beyond dispute. They saw no harm in adopting many Persian ideas, giving them an Islamic form and using them to support their rightful claim to leadership and to urge people to reclaim it from those who had seized it. It is hardly <!-- PDF page 70 | printed page 72 --> surprising, then, that Shia underground activity drew on Persian experience of organisation and gave voice to Persian hopes of liberation from social injustice.

The imprint of Persian ideas on Shia political thought at this time is particularly striking. As the writer al-Aqqad observes:[^AB-12]

> Whenever the movement for the descendants of Ali is mentioned, so too are the study of ideas, the schools of philosophy, wisdom and Sufism, and every form of inquiry that challenges what is openly visible and gives weight to what lies hidden beneath it. The supporters of Ali's family favoured change precisely because they opposed the ruling power and the existing order. Philosophy in its many branches had become intertwined with politics, especially with questions of the caliphate, kingship and religious leadership. The rightful imam was now one thing, and the ruler who had usurped power quite another.

Persian influence can be seen in the Shia idea of *wasiyya*, the designation of a successor. The imam whom the movement supported would name his successor before his death and inform his leading organisers to avoid divisions. Since Islam calls for consultation in choosing the imam, we must trace this idea of appointment to Persian traditions of government. There, kingship had been the exclusive preserve of the Sasanian royal family, passed down from generation to generation.

There is also the idea of the awaited Mahdi: the imam who has gone into hiding until he returns at <!-- PDF page 71 | printed page 73 --> the right moment to fill the earth with justice after it has been filled with oppression and wrongdoing. This idea tells us a great deal about the Shia movement's new approach. It expressed a continuing hope of liberation, despite the harsh conditions that forced the imam into hiding. His disappearance was known as the *ghayba* (absence); the period of waiting for him as *tawaqquf* (suspension of judgement); and his reappearance as the *raj'a* (return). The Persian imagination played a large part in shaping these and other beliefs.

Likewise, the principle of unquestioning obedience to the imams — one of the chief foundations of successful underground work — found its way into Shia political beliefs through Persian tradition, in which obedience to the Persian kings was regarded as a sacred duty.

The belief in hidden meanings, known as *ta'wil batini*, held that things have both an outward and an inner meaning. It entered Shia political thought from Zoroastrianism and Manichaeism.[^AB-13] Clearly, the Shia movement drew on this belief when interpreting the Quran and the Prophet's teachings in ways that supported the claim of Ali's family to leadership.

But the Shia belief that most clearly justified underground activity was *taqiyya*: concealing one's true beliefs in the face of danger. It also underlined the importance of discretion and secrecy in supporting an imam who remained hidden, through loyal organisers who obtained secret pledges of allegiance on his behalf from <!-- PDF page 72 | printed page 74 --> followers and supporters. When conditions were favourable, the revolution would break out, and upon its success its champion, the awaited Mahdi, would appear.

In short, the Shia movement's turn to underground activity grew out of the intellectual, religious, economic and social conditions of its time. Ultimately, it was born of bitter suffering and harsh persecution, which drove the oppressed to adopt this approach against a formidable enemy. The organised underground movement of the Shia began after Husayn's martyrdom and reached its height under Abu Hashim ibn Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya.[^AB-14] What we need to explain now is how and why the Abbasids took the movement for themselves, assumed control of its carefully built organisation and denied its true pioneers the fruits of their labour.

It is generally accepted that the Abbasids, descended from al-Abbas ibn Abd al-Muttalib, the Prophet's uncle (peace and blessings be upon him), played no leading role in the dispute over the imamate that gave rise to the political parties. Al-Abbas could not have claimed the caliphate after the Prophet's death, since he had embraced Islam relatively late. The accounts say that he offered to pledge allegiance to his nephew Ali as caliph. But for reasons we need not go into here, the caliphate passed to Abu Bakr, then to Umar, and then to Uthman.

When Ali became caliph after Uthman's murder, his cousins Abd Allah and Ubayd Allah, the sons of al-Abbas, supported him. Ali made the first governor of Basra and the second governor of Yemen. But when Ali's fortunes declined after the arbitration, his cousins deserted him and sided with his rival Muawiya. Power settled in the hands of the <!-- PDF page 73 | printed page 75 --> Umayyad family. The descendants of Ali launched unsuccessful revolts and, as we have seen, turned to organised underground activity.

The Abbasids then sought to join the movement of Ali's descendants. Ali ibn Abd Allah ibn al-Abbas cultivated a close relationship with Abu Hashim ibn Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya, the imam of the time whom the organisers supported. He lived with him in the village of Humayma, the centre of the movement.[^AB-15] When Abu Hashim felt his death approaching, he had to choose a successor. Not one man of Ali's family was left who could take on the responsibility: the swords of the Umayyads had cut down their young men. Abu Hashim saw no alternative but to entrust the task to Ali ibn Abd Allah ibn al-Abbas. He made him his designated successor, let him into the movement's secrets, told him who its organisers were and gave him the written instructions of succession to present to them after his death. Abu Hashim died in 100 AH. Ali took up the task and became the imam of the time. The young men of Ali's family acknowledged his leadership until they were strong enough to resume their place at the head of the movement.

The organisers accepted his leadership because he belonged to the Prophet's family. They made no distinction at the time between a leader from Ali's family and one from the Abbasid family, provided the cause remained in the Prophet's lineage. The masses of mawali cared little about the identity of the imam. What mattered much more to them was whether the movement achieved its aims. They were ready to support any imam who could free them from the grip of Umayyad rule. This helps explain how little questions of doctrine mattered compared with the social forces driving the movement. Nor did an Abbasid assuming its leadership mean giving up hope that the descendants of Ali might one day lead it again. It is certain that most of them turned their attention to scholarship <!-- PDF page 74 | printed page 76 --> and devoted themselves to learning. The trials and atrocities they had suffered had left them with little appetite for political activity. Divisions within their own family also helped the Abbasids remain at the head of the underground movement.

The Abbasids proved remarkably effective at running the movement. They showed a rare gift for organisation, and the Ismailis and Qarmatians would later follow in their footsteps. They had made up their minds to take charge, but they kept their intentions to themselves. Had they made them public, the family of Ali and its loyal organisers would have turned against them, as would the masses who held the children of Ali and Fatima in such high regard.

So the Abbasids came up with a clever slogan that would spare them an open dispute by deliberately blurring the issue. They called for “an acceptable leader from the family of Muhammad”. The slogan was vague enough to give them a claim to the leadership without provoking the problems that would have arisen had they openly promoted their own branch of the family alone.

Their brilliance can also be seen in the attractive slogans they devised to speak to the hopes and aspirations of ordinary people. Take their call for equality. Equality is a fundamental principle of Islamic law, rooted in the Quran and the teachings of the Prophet. But as a political slogan, it was broad and undefined, leaving the Abbasids free to interpret it later as they saw fit. Nevertheless, the call for equality won over religious scholars and men of religion, who blessed the movement and joined it. It also drew in Arabs who had suffered as a result of the conflict between the Qays and Yemeni tribes. And after all the ethnic discrimination the mawali had endured under the Umayyads, the promise of equality was bound to draw them to the cause in great numbers. In short, everyone who resented Umayyad policy came together under the banner of equality. This testifies to the Abbasids' remarkable gift for building a movement <!-- PDF page 75 | printed page 77 --> and organising it.

They also raised the banner of reform. Here was another attractive slogan that exposed the evils of Umayyad rule. It would allow the Abbasids, should they come to power, to claim the right to sweep away the old institutions and replace them with new ones within the bounds of Islamic law.

The Abbasids' decision to concentrate their efforts on Khurasan showed judgement and foresight. Its people were renowned for their strength and courage, and they had remained relatively untouched by the political disputes and religious rivalries that had taken hold at the heart of the state. Ali ibn Abd Allah, the imam of the time, told his organisers:[^AB-16]

> ... Seek out the people of Khurasan. They are many in number and clearly a hardy people. Their minds are unclouded and their hearts are open, not divided by rival passions or torn apart by deceit. ... I look with hope to the East, to the place where the lamp of the world and the light of creation rise.

Those last words bring us to something on which the Abbasids placed great importance, and which was one of the main reasons their movement spread so widely: prophecy. Prophecies gave people hope, encouraged them to look forward to a new age and led them to believe that they were the soldiers and strength of the revolution, chosen by God to bring it about and help it triumph. The Abbasids spread the prophecies that circulated in their age and turned them to their own political ends. This shows their extraordinary talent for building and organising the movement.

<!-- PDF page 76 | printed page 78 -->

Belief in prophecies has been widespread in East and West alike. It is not a peculiarity of the Eastern mind, as Van Vloten claims.[^AB-17] By prophecy here we mean guessing at the unseen and claiming to reveal the secrets of the future. We find echoes of it in ancient traditions, such as the legends of Typhon and Horus and the Persian story of Mithra. Indian and Chinese beliefs are likewise rich in stories and legends about knowing what the future holds. In Greek tradition, the prophecies of Apollo were famous, as were his priests at Delphi, who foretold the signs of things to come and claimed to know the mysteries of the future. Scandinavian folklore and the legends of Central Asia offer many more examples of belief in prophecies. Even today, people in both East and West believe in such things and promote them in books.

We believe this phenomenon tells us something about the human condition: people find themselves in distress, feel powerless in the face of difficult circumstances and long for a way out. It is hardly surprising that these beliefs spread in times of disaster and severe crisis. Islam forbade belief in magic, sorcery and superstition, and condemned fortune-tellers, magicians and astrologers, “even if they happen to speak the truth”.

Belief in such predictions therefore entered Islamic thought from outside it, although it had already left its mark on pre-Islamic Arab tradition. It spread among Muslims from the early days of Islam through Jews and Christians. Ibn Khaldun deals with the subject in his *Muqaddima*[^AB-18] under the heading “The Nature of Prophecy, <!-- PDF page 77 | printed page 79 --> Divination, Dreams, Fortune-tellers and Other Ways of Perceiving the Unseen”. He distinguishes prophecy from divination:

> The defining quality of prophecy is truth; falsehood can never enter into it. It comes from a connection made by the prophet himself with the higher realm, without an intermediary or any outside aid. The diviner, however, is not able to do this and must rely on impressions from outside himself. These impressions enter into his perception and mingle with what he is trying to perceive. His perception becomes confused by them, and falsehood finds its way in.

We do not deny the prophecy of the prophets themselves. What we reject is the claim that other people can foretell the future. Ibn Khaldun, however, believed that:

> Among human beings are people who, by virtue of the nature they were born with, can tell of things before they occur. These include fortune-tellers, those who gaze into transparent objects or examine the hearts and livers of animals, those who read omens in birds and wild beasts, those who cast pebbles and grains, and so forth.

Ibn Khaldun was certainly influenced here by Hellenistic philosophy, particularly its ideas about the nature of the human soul and its different levels. Of particular importance was Aristotle's theory of emanation — the idea that things flow from a higher source — which had a marked influence on philosophy through the translation of Aristotle's works and through Neoplatonism.

In any case, the Abbasids used prophecies in their appeals to win the hearts of the mawali. They certainly drew on ancient Persian prophecies for this purpose. Perhaps the most important prophecy the Persians believed in, and which the Abbasid organisers promoted, was that of the return of the awaited Mahdi. It acquired a distinctly Abbasid character. Among the movement's followers there was widespread talk of “the appearance of the man with the black banners, who will come from the East and bring down the throne of the Umayyads”.

This was why the Abbasids adopted black banners, giving them names such as the Banner of the Shadow and the Banner of the Clouds. They interpreted the shadow <!-- PDF page 78 | printed page 80 --> and the clouds to mean that just as clouds cover the earth with their shade, and the earth is never without shade, so it will never be without an Abbasid imam. They had a third banner called the Banner of Victory, which encouraged their followers to believe that victory was inevitable.

The Abbasids were also influenced by Jewish prophecies through books of *malahim* (predictions of momentous events) held by Jews in the Islamic world. A *malhama* is a momentous event that cannot fail to happen. These predictions consisted of combinations of obscure symbols that were supposed to reveal what the future held. One such prophecy spread by the organisers at the time said that “A son of A son of A will kill M son of M”. Its interpretation was that the movement founded by Ali ibn Abd Allah ibn al-Abbas would destroy the state of Marwan ibn Muhammad, the last Umayyad caliph.[^AB-19]

In short, the spread of these prophecies and the hold they had over people's minds testify to the skill of the secret Abbasid movement and the effectiveness of its psychological appeals. They captured the imagination of ordinary people and prepared them for revolution.

Let us now examine the structure of the underground Abbasid organisation and how it rallied the people in the years leading up to the revolution. Its leadership clearly lay with the imam, who remained out of sight in the village of Humayma. He directed the movement, selected organisers whom he trusted and assigned their areas of activity. The organisers would come to him at set times to keep him informed of their progress. He, in turn, sent them letters containing his instructions, <!-- PDF page 79 | printed page 81 --> guidance and advice. We have found none of these letters from the years of preparation, but we have many of them from the period after the revolution's success.

During those years of preparation, three men from the Abbasid family rose to prominence, none of whom would live to reap the fruits of their efforts. The first was Ali ibn Abd Allah ibn al-Abbas. He led the movement from the death of Abu Hashim ibn Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya until 117 AH. He is credited with organising its advocates and representatives in Iraq and Khurasan. His son Muhammad then took over after his death and remained in charge until 125 AH. He was inactive and unenthusiastic, preferred to play safe and stopped corresponding with the organisers. It appears that he came under pressure from the family of Ali and was accused of seeking power for himself. This caused confusion among the organisers in Khurasan and allowed one of them, a man named Khidash, to try to turn the movement back towards the family of Ali.[^AB-20] It was also said that he sought to build a following for himself on the basis of Persian nationalism, drawing on a revival of the old Persian religion of the Khurramiyya. Perhaps this was one reason the imam stopped writing to the organisers in Khurasan.

Ibrahim ibn Muhammad then took over and performed his task well. Under his leadership, the movement reached the height of its strength, and he eventually called on its organisers to emerge from secrecy. But the Umayyad Marwan ibn Muhammad quickly had him arrested and imprisoned, then killed. By now the movement had grown strong, and it soon brought down both Marwan and the Umayyad state.

The imams were assisted by a group of organisers drawn from the mawali and close associates of the Abbasid family. They therefore knew the movement's secrets and the identity of the imam of the time. They were all of a similar kind and possessed considerable military <!-- PDF page 80 | printed page 82 --> talent, were devoted heart and soul to the cause, and were adept at diplomacy, tact, dealing with changing circumstances and winning supporters. They spoke with courtesy, eloquence and skill, adapting their words to the occasion.[^AB-21] The imams took care to prepare them especially for their task before entrusting them with the work of the movement. Their military, psychological and cultural preparation lay behind the success of their efforts. They travelled the country in the guise of merchants and scholars, spreading the movement's ideas and winning followers. They remained devoted to the cause despite the killing, crucifixion and torture they suffered. Among the best-known organisers in Iraq were Maysara al-Abdi, Bukayr ibn Mahan and Abu Salama al-Khallal, who became known as the “Minister of Muhammad's Family”.

Among the most famous organisers in Khurasan were Sulayman ibn Kathir and Abu Muslim al-Khurasani, the man who was destined to proclaim the revolution and lead it to victory.

The next rank in the movement consisted of the *nuqaba*, or representatives, who answered to the organisers. Each organiser had twelve representatives under him, chosen for their abilities and in contact only with their own organiser. Their role was to carry out the movement's plans, recruit people for the next rank down and oversee their activities. Each representative was assisted by seventy agents, who administered the underground organisation and formed its local groups among the people of the towns and villages. Van Vloten[^AB-22] suggested that the Abbasid organisation was influenced on the one hand by the Jewish council of disciples and on the other by the council of consultation in the Prophet's time. Whatever the truth of that may be, what is certain is that <!-- PDF page 81 | printed page 83 --> the Abbasids built a tightly organised movement, bringing every psychological, military and cultural means to bear on its success.

Within the first three decades of the second century AH, their cause had spread throughout most of the eastern Islamic world. There were organised groups ready and eager to support it at the first signal in the Khurasani towns of Damghan, Sarakhs, Nasa, Nishapur, Qumis, Merv, Herat, Bushanj and Abiward. Their support extended to some sixty villages in Khurasan. In these towns and villages, the movement brought together a wide range of Iranian, Arab and Turkish peoples and groups, uniting them all under the Abbasid banner.

On 25 Ramadan 129 AH, Abu Muslim al-Khurasani proclaimed the revolution and raised the black banners.[^AB-23] After a series of victories, he gained control of Khurasan. The revolutionaries then turned towards Iraq under the leadership of an Arab, Qahtaba ibn Shabib, who defeated the Umayyad armies. In Jumada al-Akhira 132 AH, the Abbasid forces led by Abd Allah ibn Ali, a member of the Abbasid family, defeated the army of Marwan ibn Muhammad at the Zab River. This was the decisive battle that brought down Umayyad rule and established the Abbasid caliphate.[^AB-24]

The underground movement had achieved its aims, and the Islamic world underwent a profound transformation. The role of the Arabs in shaping its affairs diminished. The mawali stepped onto the stage to take on that same role with skill and ability. The consequences were of enormous importance in political, economic, <!-- PDF page 82 | printed page 84 --> social and cultural life.

The underground Abbasid organisation did not disappear with the success of the revolution. It was no longer confined to Iraq and Khurasan, but spread throughout all the provinces. It took on new duties and responsibilities suited to the circumstances that followed the revolution.

Its original purpose had been to spread the movement's ideas and prepare for the revolution. Its duty was now to strengthen the loyalty of the Abbasid supporters to the caliphate and its policies, protect the gains won by the revolutionaries, and uncover enemies of the revolution and any plots they might be hatching. It also kept watch over the governors and officials to see that they followed state policy in their provinces. It kept the caliphs informed of shortcomings and failings in the provinces that might cause unrest among the people, and offered advice on the best ways to put things right.

We learn all this and more from the letters the caliphs sent to the heads of the underground organisation in the provinces. They were known as the “Thursday Letters”. We do not know why they were given that name, but perhaps the organisers read them aloud to members of the organisation at special meetings held on Thursdays. A number of these letters survive. They were composed in the state correspondence office in the name of the caliph. Here are selected passages from a letter sent by the caliph al-Ma'mun, the Commander of the Faithful:

> To those among the people of Khurasan and the other Muslims who have pledged allegiance to the truth and come to the aid of the faith ... You, people of Khurasan, have enjoyed three distinctions under the rule of the Commander of the Faithful. God has singled you out for their blessings and established <!-- PDF page 83 | printed page 85 --> their degrees of honour. The first of these distinctions is the support your forebears gave to the Prophet's family and to the last of his heirs among the forefathers of the Commander of the Faithful. The second is the support God commanded you to give him in his second call. The third is the sincerity of your hearts and the wholehearted loyalty you have shown.
>
> So, you loyal followers of the Commander of the Faithful, remind yourselves often of the favour made easy for you, of the difficulties smoothed away for you, and of the victory over those who departed from the faith and opposed the people of the Qibla [those who pray facing Mecca]. Their homes and wealth were made available to you, and through God's grace you became the guardians of the faith, the supporters of rightly guided imams and the strongholds of all Muslims.
>
> Those who bear you ill will, followers of the Commander of the Faithful, have their eyes upon you. They look at you with envy and rivalry. Some are openly hostile, others secretly flatter you, and still others have joined your ranks and mingled with your people. They feel safe among you, and it is easier for them to strike at your state through false appearances and deception, inflicting deeper wounds and greater harm. Be on your guard against such people, for it is often those who lack the strength to act openly who resort to trickery. Even a resolute man in the hour of his victory must beware of subtle deceit and hidden enticements.
>
> Let the first thing you cultivate in yourselves, and persist in cultivating, be good conduct. Be fair to one another and give those among you of virtue and honourable deeds the precedence and respect they deserve.
>
> Know that the Commander of the Faithful takes greater care over your education, your good conduct and your honourable way of life than he does over those of other people. If he considers it his duty to set his subjects on the right path and guide them towards what will help them live uprightly, then he owes <!-- PDF page 84 | printed page 86 --> even greater care to those who are closest to him and have the strongest claim on his attention. For giving priority to your moral instruction and guidance carries its own risks [the wording of this clause is uncertain].
>
> The Commander of the Faithful asks God, who in His generosity has invited us to pray and has promised to answer us, saying, “Call on Me, and I will answer you,” to unite you in all that pleases Him, to bind you together in obedience, to let you enjoy the finest of the blessings He has bestowed upon you and inspire you with such gratitude that He will continue to give you more. May He protect you from the schemes of the unbelievers and the envy of the wrongdoers. May He preserve the Commander of the Faithful among you as He has best preserved a rightly guided imam among his friends and followers, and bear for him the weight of the responsibility he carries for you. In God the Commander of the Faithful seeks help as he strives to reward you with goodness and guide you along the best path. He is content with God as his helper and protector. God is sufficient as a protector, and God is sufficient as a helper. Peace be upon you, and God's mercy and blessings.[^AB-25]

Here are some further excerpts, this time from a letter that one of the heads of the underground organisation sent to the caliph al-Rashid. In it he pledges his loyalty, offers counsel and praises the caliph's virtues and the wisdom of his rule:

> Now then, I ask God to grant the Commander of the Faithful in the years ahead the best of what He has granted him in the past: the safety with which He has guarded him against misfortune, the strength with which He has overcome his enemies, and the victory with which He has established him in the land. Through these blessings, both those already given him and those yet to come, may he be remembered for his goodness longer than any of God's other caliphs, may the marks of his justice endure the longest, may his life be the longest and may his return to God be the finest of all.
>
> We give praise to God, who has made His blessings upon the Commander of the Faithful a testimony to <!-- PDF page 85 | printed page 87 --> his standing and rank in God's sight. With these, he has no need of the testimony of those who praise him or the descriptions of those who sing his praises. God has made it a duty for His servants to speak of His blessings upon the Commander of the Faithful, to serve them faithfully and to strive against those who plot against them. He has made this a test by which to try them and distinguish one from another. Whoever among his subjects makes it his chief concern to speak of the caliph, to recount his good qualities and virtues and to acknowledge his rightful claim to obedience, has chosen the worthiest course and the one with the best outcome in this world and in his faith. Whoever changes course, whether emboldened by some power of his own or won over by a new doctrine, will find that God’s argument in favour of the Commander of the Faithful is enough to deal with him. ... Given the kinship God has granted me with him — that is, with the Commander of the Faithful — and the great duty I know I owe him for all the kindness he has shown me, I have hoped that no one who looks upon him with solicitude will be more ready than I to offer what God has made him worthy of. If I achieve what I intend, it will be through God's help. If I fall short, it will be the shortcoming of one who has done his utmost.
>
> ... I wanted the Commander of the Faithful to know that in everything he has done for his subjects, he has a clear justification and a recognised defence. If someone speaks of it in the company of his closest advisers, it will be well received. If a letter explaining it is read aloud to the people, it will strengthen the case for it.
>
> Praise be to God, who has made him and his descendants the guardians of these blessings and singled them out for these virtues. We ask God to preserve him and them for the faith whose breaches He has repaired through them, for the truth whose path He has made clear through them, and for the justice whose guiding signs He has made clear through them.[^AB-26]

In conclusion, the letters exchanged between the caliphs and their organisers prove that the underground organisation continued to exist and spread throughout the provinces during the early Abbasid period, right up to the reign of al-Mutawakkil. After that, the organisation began to break apart and eventually <!-- PDF page 86 | printed page 88 --> disappeared as the Turks seized control of the affairs of the caliphate and monopolised power and influence. This was followed by the Buyid conquest of Iraq and then the Seljuk conquest. Out of these developments came the collapse of the system of the caliphate, the rise of independent states and the disintegration of the unity of the Islamic world.

---

<!-- PDF page 87 | printed page 89 -->

## The Mu'tazilites

### From Reason to Political Action

For all that has been written about the Mu'tazilites, parts of their history are still far from clear. Most studies deal with their ideas and beliefs; relatively few explore the part they played in politics during the closing years of Umayyad rule and throughout the Abbasid era. As a result, the subject has attracted more attention from scholars of philosophy and Islamic thought than from historians. Even those who have written the history of the Mu'tazilites have treated them as little more than a school of religious thought, unlike other schools that stood for particular religious, political and social causes. One historian writes[^MT-1]: “The Mu'tazilites had no such organisations, nor were they separate from the Sunni community.” Scholars of philosophy, meanwhile, have studied their ideas in isolation from the social and historical conditions that gave rise to them and <!-- PDF page 88 | printed page 90 --> shaped them as they developed. We therefore need to bring together the perspectives of the historian and the student of ideas. Only then can we see the full significance of the part the Mu'tazilites played in Islamic history and civilisation.

Perhaps the focus on their religious thinking is understandable: the Mu'tazilites pioneered one of the most important currents in Islamic thought, the movement that placed its trust in reason. Yet their role was no less important in politics and in the social development of the Islamic world. Admittedly, their political and social role in early Islam and under the Umayyads seems modest compared with that of the other opposition parties. This is partly because they came onto the scene later, around the beginning of the second century AH, whereas the other groups had emerged much earlier, during the great civil war. But it also had to do with the different ways they chose to fight for their cause.

The Mu'tazilites put their faith in education and enlightenment. The other opposition parties resorted first to open revolutionary violence and later to organised underground rebellion. These different approaches brought different results. The Mu'tazilites gained a foothold within the government, came to dominate it and steered it towards their own aims. The struggles of the left, on the other hand, led to the founding of independent states opposed to the existing government.

The deep differences between Mu'tazilite and Sunni thought, the different ways the Mu'tazilites and the parties of the left confronted the caliphate, and the moderation of some Mu'tazilite beliefs — such as the view that a Muslim guilty of a serious sin occupies a position between faith and unbelief — have led some <!-- PDF page 89 | printed page 91 --> scholars to regard the Mu'tazilites as a party of the centre, somewhere between the government and the opposition. Even if we accept this view, we must distinguish them from the Murji'ite centre, which began by justifying the government and only much later turned against it. The Mu'tazilites were enlightened liberals. At the heart of their political and social ideas lay a concern for human dignity. They hoped to put their principles into practice through education and reform rather than revolution and the overthrow of governments. Even when they turned to organised underground activity, their first aim was a revolution in culture and thought. Their second was to support the political and social opposition led by the descendants of Ali. Rarely did they seek to establish independent states founded on their own beliefs.

There can be no doubt that the Mu'tazilites pioneered the use of reason in Islam. There is a close connection between a commitment to reason and opposition to conservative government claiming religious authority. That is why Mu'tazilite ideas found little favour with the Sunni caliphate, except under a few enlightened caliphs. They aroused the anger of scholars who based their teachings on transmitted accounts of the Prophet and traditionalists loyal to the caliphate. These men branded the Mu'tazilites political rebels, social anarchists and religious deviants. The Sunni scholar al-Shahrastani, for instance, called them the “Magians of the Muslim community”[^MT-2] — a comparison with followers of the old Persian religion. Unable to find fault with their enlightened ideas or their noble aims, their enemies turned their anger against the individuals themselves. They accused them of moral corruption, debauchery, contempt for religion and an attempt to destroy Islam and replace it with pagan thought.[^MT-3] All the evidence they put forward on these charges was taken from the slanders of <!-- PDF page 90 | printed page 92 --> a man who had broken with the Mu'tazilites, Ibn al-Rawandi,[^MT-4] without any attempt to check or examine his claims.[^MT-5]

Sadly, the works and essays of the Mu'tazilites have been lost, leaving the writings of their enemies as a source of information about their ideas. It hardly needs saying that the leading Mu'tazilite thinkers wrote extensively on all kinds of subjects in their day. Wasil ibn Ata wrote eleven books; every one of them has been lost.[^MT-6] Abu al-Hudhayl wrote twelve hundred treatises in answer to his opponents, and not a trace of any of them has been found.[^MT-7] The Mu'tazilite thinker al-Nazzam wrote many essays on philosophy, the oneness of God and arguments against those who disagreed with him. All have disappeared.[^MT-8] Al-Jahiz wrote hundreds of treatises on the same subjects, of which only a tiny fraction remains.[^MT-9] During the persecution of the Mu'tazilites under al-Mutawakkil, the traditionalist scholars competed with one another to burn these works, hoping to draw closer to God and strike a blow at their opponents.[^MT-10] This is why books about the different schools of Islam — particularly the works of Ash'arite and Hanbalite writers such as al-Baghdadi, al-Shahrastani, Ibn Hazm, Ibn al-Qayyim and others — are so bitterly hostile to the Mu'tazilites, presenting <!-- PDF page 91 | printed page 93 --> their ideas as a kind of paganism and heresy.

It is hardly surprising that modern scholars have been influenced by this approach, whether they agree with it or react against it. Some have greatly exaggerated the influence of foreign ideas on the Mu'tazilites. Others have gone to the opposite extreme, treating their thought as a purely Islamic development. Among those who take the first view are Western scholars of the East such as Thomas Arnold,[^MT-11] De Boer[^MT-12] and MacDonald,[^MT-13] who link the origins of the Mu'tazilites to Christian and Jewish theology and the heritage of ancient Greek thought. Zuhdi Jar Allah[^MT-14] followed their lead. He traced the Mu'tazilite belief that the Quran was created to Jewish thought, and their rejection of distinct divine attributes, their willingness to interpret scripture beyond its literal meaning and their belief in free will to Christian thought. He also pointed to their use of Aristotle's logic in shaping these ideas. A modern researcher,[^MT-15] by contrast, has tried to establish the Islamic roots of Mu'tazilite thought, arguing that the questions it dealt with had already been raised in the days of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and the first caliphs, before the Arabs had opened themselves to foreign ideas.

Other scholars have taken a middle course. Becker,[^MT-16] for example, held that the Quran was the wellspring from which the Mu'tazilites drew their basic beliefs, although Greek philosophy influenced how they expressed them. He also noted that they tackled certain fundamental philosophical questions in the manner of <!-- PDF page 92 | printed page 94 --> the Greek philosophers, such as whether motion occurs in leaps, how one action produces another, and the distinction between a thing itself and its changing qualities. In much the same vein, Ahmad Amin[^MT-17] described Mu'tazilite thought as “a blend of both currents, Islamic and foreign”.

We believe that the questions the Mu'tazilites raised are among the basic questions that arise naturally in the human mind, with no need for one culture to influence another. Indeed, most of them — questions of free choice and fate, of God and his attributes, and so on — have been asked since ancient times and are still being asked today. The Quran addresses these matters in many chapters and verses, and from a very early date Muslims offered different interpretations. The heart of the disagreement is how far we should make use of reason. Should the mind be given free rein to interpret a text and discover its deeper meaning, or must it stick to the literal meaning of the words and go no further?

The Mu'tazilites took the first path. It had been opened up in early Islam by the Qadarites, who defended human freedom of choice. The Mu'tazilites developed this approach further, drawing on other religions and ancient philosophies that shared their way of thinking. In other words, they used reason to establish the soundness of their understanding of what the Quran said about these questions. Or perhaps we might say that they “gave faith a rational foundation”, supporting, in the words of H. A. R. Gibb,[^MT-18] “the moral philosophy derived from the Quran by drawing on Greek philosophies and the writings of Hellenistic Christian religious debate”. Thanks to the Mu'tazilites, “the Quran and Aristotle were read side by side and were sincerely regarded as complementing one another”.[^MT-19] They succeeded in bringing “Greek thought and the Islamic religion into harmony — the oneness of God and philosophy”.[^MT-20]

<!-- PDF page 93 | printed page 95 -->

There is no need to dwell at length on how the Mu'tazilites enriched Islamic thought by turning to reason whenever they faced a difficult question.[^MT-21] Scholars agree that they laid the foundations for the reasoned study of Islamic beliefs, paved the way for Islamic philosophy and defended Islam against its critics. A few points are worth drawing attention to here.

**First.** We owe the Mu'tazilites the recognition that reason is a source of knowledge. They challenged superstition, magic, trickery and other claims that could not stand up to rational thought, such as sightings of jinn (unseen beings) and stories of the moon splitting in two. They did not hesitate to question these legends, even when they were attributed to some of the Prophet's leading companions.[^MT-22] In doing so, they laid the first foundations of a scientific approach to thinking.

**Second.** Their scientific way of thinking can also be seen in the importance they placed on doubt and experiment. They looked for the causes behind things, whether in the universe, the material world, society or human life. One of their leading thinkers put it this way:[^MT-23]

> A cause may be present without producing its effect, because something can get in the way and prevent that effect from arising. But once the cause is present and all obstacles are removed, the effect must follow, just as an action inevitably follows when all the motives for it are in place.

In this way, they were forerunners of <!-- PDF page 94 | printed page 96 --> the great philosophers of Islam, such as al-Kindi, al-Farabi and Ibn Sina. They also paved the way for Ibn al-Haytham and Jabir ibn Hayyan to apply the method of experiment to the study of nature.

**Third.** At its heart, Mu'tazilite thought is constructive and life-affirming. It places great value on the individual, breaks the chains that limit freedom of thought and action, and opens new horizons for the mind to think and create. It does this by affirming our freedom to choose.

The opposite belief — that everything is decided for us in advance — reduces a person to a feather in the wind.[^MT-24] It teaches people to bow before fate, to feel powerless, to depend on others and to lose their own sense of self.

Creativity and respect for individuality go hand in hand. The sciences, arts and literature of ancient Greece flourished because people valued individuality. In much the same way, the backwardness of medieval Europe was a direct result of the Catholic Church's suppression of the individual spirit.

**Fourth.** There is a close connection between the Mu'tazilites' belief in reason and their commitment to social justice. The very people who led the way in enlightened thinking were also champions of equality. It is no wonder that they became known as the People of Justice and the Oneness of God.[^MT-25] Al-Masudi[^MT-26] called them the People of Justice. One modern scholar[^MT-27] has devoted a chapter to explaining what freedom meant for the Mu'tazilites in social terms. We will test that account by looking at their political activity.

**Fifth.** The Mu'tazilites enriched Islamic thought by giving reason <!-- PDF page 95 | printed page 97 --> a firm place within it. But they also did more to uphold and defend Islamic beliefs against their opponents than the scholars of the prophetic traditions and other traditionalists did. Followers of the older religions who were hostile to Islam sought to undermine it through secret movements or open campaigns. In the Abbasid era these became widespread, in what was known as *zandaqa*, or heresy. The jurists and scholars of the prophetic traditions could not defend Islam merely by quoting sacred texts when faced with opponents who argued from reason and logic. The Mu'tazilites met them on their own ground. Their debates and writings in answer to materialists, believers in rival divine powers, the Sumaniyya, the followers of Bardaisan, Jews and Christians[^MT-28] scarcely need an introduction. Suffice it to say that many followers of other faiths embraced Islam out of conviction, thanks to the leading Mu'tazilite thinkers who defended it with reasoned arguments. In doing so, they earned Islamic thought considerable respect.[^MT-29]

Let us now look at the political activity of the Mu'tazilites in the light of their ideas and what they actually did under the Umayyads and the Abbasids.

The Mu'tazilites emerged in Basra around 100 AH as a school of religious thought with beliefs of its own. Its founder was Wasil ibn Ata, who withdrew from the study circle of his teacher, al-Hasan al-Basri. His followers became known as the Wasiliyya, or the Mu'tazilites. Accounts differ as to how the latter name came about. Exploring its origins can shed a great deal of light on their political thought and social background. Al-Shahrastani[^MT-30] traces it to something al-Hasan al-Basri said when Wasil ibn <!-- PDF page 96 | printed page 98 --> Ata left his circle: “Wasil has withdrawn from us.” On this account, he and his followers came to be called the Mu'tazilites — those who withdraw. Al-Baghdadi[^MT-31] believes that the name arose because they departed from the consensus of the Muslim community over the status of someone who commits a grave sin. Al-Masudi,[^MT-32] on the other hand, thought it referred to their belief that a person guilty of such a sin stood apart from both believers and unbelievers, in a position between the two. The Mu'tazilite al-Murtada[^MT-33] held that they had given themselves the name because they rejected beliefs and innovations that had been introduced into the religion.

What stands out in these differing explanations by Sunni historians of religious sects is the hostility and prejudice towards the Mu'tazilites. This makes it difficult to take them at face value. Al-Murtada's account, too, displays a pointless bias. Both approaches lean on the literal meaning of the word. It is more logical to look for the name's origins in a particular intellectual and political stance, as with the names of the other groups. The scholar Nallino believes that the word originally referred to the political stance of the Prophet's companions who remained neutral during the great civil war. The Mu'tazilites borrowed the name to express their own neutral position in the dispute between the groups over the status of a person who commits a grave sin. Nallino seems to have been influenced by an account in al-Nawbakhti:[^MT-34]

> When Uthman was killed, the people pledged allegiance to Ali, peace be upon him, and were known as the community. Later they split into three groups. One remained loyal to Ali ibn Abi Talib. Another withdrew, together with Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas, Abd Allah ibn Umar, Muhammad ibn Maslama and Usama ibn Zayd. <!-- PDF page 97 | printed page 99 --> These men withdrew from Ali, peace be upon him, and refused either to fight against him or to fight alongside him, even though they had pledged their allegiance to him and accepted his leadership. Thus they were called the Mu'tazilites, and became the forebears of the Mu'tazilites for all time.

This view gains support from al-Subki, who says that he came across a Mu'tazilite book that began by naming Abd Allah ibn Masud as a pioneer of their movement.[^MT-35] As we know, he was one of the Prophet's leading companions who withdrew from the fighting during the great civil war because they doubted the justice of the rival sides' claims.

The political thinking of the Mu'tazilites bears the marks of enlightened moderation. Their belief that the person who commits a grave sin stands somewhere between faith and unbelief was a middle course between the extremes of the Kharijites, who declared that person an unbeliever, the leniency of the Sunnis, who regarded that person as a believer, and the Murji'ites, who evaded the issue by refusing to pass judgement at all. Wasil ibn Ata seems to have tried in his day to bring the opposition forces together, heal their divisions and unite their efforts against the Umayyad caliphate. His views on leadership bear this out. He agreed with the Kharijites that the Muslim community had the right to choose its own leader, whether from the tribe of Quraysh or from elsewhere. He also came close to the Shia position when he argued that people in every age were bound to have a leader.[^MT-36] As we have already seen, all the opposition parties were adapting their political beliefs at that time. Their ideas drew closer together, and they came to one another's aid in their opposition to the Umayyads during the dynasty's final years.
