Nothing illustrates this coming together of ideas better than the fact that the Mu'tazilites drew most of their principles from <!-- PDF page 98 | printed page 100 --> the enlightened ideas circulating in Basra at the time. From the Qadarites they took the belief in free choice and in people's responsibility for their own actions. From the Jahmiyya — the followers of the Murji'ite Jahm ibn Safwan — they adopted the rejection of distinct divine attributes, the belief that the Quran was created and the view that God cannot be seen with the eyes in the life to come. This explains why some scholars have confused the Jahmiyya[^MT-37] with the Mu'tazilites and the Qadarites. The duty to promote what is right and oppose what is wrong — one of the five basic principles of the Mu'tazilites — was among the most important beliefs of the Kharijites. The Mu'tazilites disagreed with the Shia over the doctrine that the Prophet had designated a successor and the belief that the leaders of the community were protected from error. Yet they shared many of the Shia's views on leadership, as well as their willingness to interpret the deeper meaning of sacred texts. Al-Murtada[^MT-38] even thinks it likely that Wasil and Amr ibn Ubayd, the two leading figures in the movement, studied under Abu Hashim ibn Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyya. And despite their disagreement with al-Hasan al-Basri over the person who commits a grave sin, they agreed with him on divine justice and free choice.[^MT-39]

In short, the Mu'tazilites saw nothing wrong with building their school of thought by choosing from the wisest ideas of their time, ideas that had originally been inspired by Islam. They then gave those ideas a philosophical form, drawing on the heritage of other cultures in the process.

With its five basic principles,[^MT-40] the political and social outlook of the Mu'tazilite movement was in harmony with the demands of the mawali, who called for the Islamic law to be put into practice, with its promise of <!-- PDF page 99 | printed page 101 --> justice and equality. It is worth remembering that the leading figures of the Mu'tazilite movement were mawali who were sincere in their Muslim faith and sought to put its teachings into practice through reform and education rather than violence and revolution. Wasil ibn Ata and Amr ibn Ubayd, the founders of the movement, were renowned for their devotion and upright character. Both were mawali.[^MT-41] Abu al-Hudhayl al-Allaf, the leading Mu'tazilite of Basra, was a client of the Abd al-Qays tribe.[^MT-42] Ibrahim ibn Sayyar al-Nazzam, the teacher of al-Jahiz, was a Basran from among the clients of the family of Ziyad.[^MT-43] Thumama ibn Ashras, another leading figure among the Mu'tazilites of Basra, was a client of the Banu Numayr.[^MT-44] And al-Jahiz, the most famous Mu'tazilite scholar, a man of extraordinarily wide learning, was also one of the mawali of Basra.[^MT-45]

Because their leaders were not Arabs, because they drew deeply on the heritage of the ancient world in shaping their ideas, and because they resented the Umayyad government, they were accused of ethnic partisanship, heresy and opportunism. Yet the leaders of the Mu'tazilites fought ethnic prejudice even as they sought to give the movement for the equality of peoples — *Shu'ubiyya* — a truly international, Islamic meaning. In other words, they called for unity among the Muslim peoples on the basis of equality. Al-Jahiz writes:

> The mawali are more like the Arabs, closer to them and more intimately bound to them, because the teachings of Islam have made them one with the Arabs. They count as Arabs in claims of kinship, in the shared responsibility of kin to pay compensation, and in matters of inheritance. If this is so, the *Banawi*[^MT-46] is a man of Khurasan, and if the man of Khurasan is the client of an Arab, then the man of Khurasan, the *Banawi*, the client <!-- PDF page 100 | printed page 102 --> and the Arab are all one. At the very least, the things they have in common far outweigh the things that divide them. Indeed, in the most important matters, in all that really counts and in the main bonds of kinship, they agree. Once this is understood, hearts become more forgiving, the complications disappear, old grudges die out and there is no longer any reason to resent one another. All that remains is the envy and rivalry that will always exist among close relatives and neighbours.[^MT-47]

The accusation of heresy falls apart in the face of their deep devotion to Islam and their defence of it against its opponents, even when those opponents came from their own people. They were, then, “a school of thought passionately devoted to religion and determined to defend it”.[^MT-48] Nor can their opposition to Umayyad rule be put down to personal ambition. They were known for their simple way of life and their refusal to seek favours.[^MT-49] Despite their poverty and great need, they lived honourably, refused to stoop to dishonest ways of making a living and avoided anything that might bring their conduct into question.[^MT-50] A modern researcher was therefore right to say of them:[^MT-51]

> The Mu'tazilite school was something like a community of educated people who judged a person's place in society by their culture and ideas. They had no faith in ethnic prejudice. Their struggle was fought in the worlds of thought and politics. What brought them together were the values of the new society and the elements that made up its civilisation, <!-- PDF page 101 | printed page 103 --> a blend of the Arab heritage and the new intellectual gains from cultures that had been introduced or revived in the lands conquered by the Muslim Arabs.

The Mu'tazilites opposed the Umayyads because, in their eyes, they were tyrants who had seized the caliphate, imposed their rule by the sword and justified it with the belief that all human actions are predetermined. The Mu'tazilites, by contrast, championed freedom of choice. There is a close connection between the belief in predestination and tyranny, just as there is between the belief in human choice and freedom.[^MT-52] That is why Muawiya is regarded as the first to teach and defend the doctrine of predestination. He “claimed that his actions were decreed and created by God, using this as an excuse for what he did, making people believe that he was in the right and that God had made him their leader and entrusted him with power”.[^MT-53]

The belief in free choice had a political meaning for the Mu'tazilites. It called on people to resist unjustly seized power, on the grounds that such power had been brought into existence by human actions and could only be brought down by human actions in return, since people are free to decide how they act.[^MT-54] There is therefore no getting away from the fact that the Mu'tazilites rejected the rule of Muawiya and his family.[^MT-55] Ahmad Amin,[^MT-56] on the other hand, thought their ideas favoured Muawiya more than Ali, because the leaders of the Mu'tazilites were not persecuted under the Umayyads. But Amin himself later changed his mind, writing that “all the leading Mu'tazilite thinkers support Ali's position in the war between him and Muawiya”.[^MT-57]

<!-- PDF page 102 | printed page 104 -->

Al-Khayyat[^MT-58] confirms this: “The Mu'tazilites disown Muawiya, Amr ibn al-As and those who sided with them.” Some of their leading thinkers went so far as to accuse Muawiya of atheism and unbelief.[^MT-59] There is no contradiction whatsoever between the Mu'tazilites' belief in the position between faith and unbelief[^MT-60] and their refusal to recognise the Umayyad government as legitimate. In the case of someone who commits a grave sin, that middle position means that the person is guilty of wrongdoing and is neither a believer nor an unbeliever.[^MT-61] The person's wrongdoing places them at odds with the conduct expected of believers.[^MT-62] Someone in this position has no right to lead the Muslim community and must be removed from office, even by force. Judge Abd al-Jabbar says:[^MT-63]

> It is established by the agreement of the Prophet's companions that a leader must be removed for conduct that amounts to grave wrongdoing, since the companions did not disagree on this point.

The reasons why Muawiya was unfit to lead the community were therefore plain beyond doubt; at the very least, he was guilty of wrongdoing in many respects. This judgement extended to the Umayyads in general: “There is no need for us to discuss the invalidity of the leadership of Muawiya and those who followed him, the descendants of Marwan and others.”[^MT-64] To the early Mu'tazilite leaders, the Umayyad caliphate was a monarchy in the style of Heraclius, the Byzantine emperor. Their disgraceful conduct <!-- PDF page 103 | printed page 105 --> and their actions contrary to Islamic law made them unworthy of the caliphate.[^MT-65]

Since the Umayyads had seized the caliphate unlawfully, it was the duty of the Muslim community to struggle in keeping with the principle of promoting what is right and opposing what is wrong. Most opposition groups shared this principle. They held that the use of force was a duty in resisting injustice and establishing what was right,[^MT-66] unlike the scholars of the prophetic traditions, who forbade armed resistance even against an unjust ruler.[^MT-67] But the opposition parties differed in how they understood that duty. The Kharijites regarded rebellion against tyrannical rulers as necessary without hesitation or delay. Some of the Imami Shia reserved this right for the imam alone; the community could offer advice but could not take up arms. The Mu'tazilites took a position between the Kharijites and the Shia. They recognised the community's right to resist injustice by force, but with certain reservations, which they expressed in these terms: “If we are a united group and have good reason to believe that we can overcome those who oppose us, and if the rising is led by a just imam.”[^MT-68] Such caution brought together clear thinking and careful judgement about rebellion with the justice of the cause for which it was undertaken. On this basis the Mu'tazilites excused those among the Prophet's companions, the generation that followed them and the champions of justice who had held back from opposing the Umayyads rather than throwing away their lives at the hands of the rabble of Syria. “There can be no justified rebellion unless there is good reason to expect the revolution to succeed.”[^MT-69]

<!-- PDF page 104 | printed page 106 -->

So the Mu'tazilites' reluctance to confront the Umayyads was neither cowardice nor submission. They had learned from the bitter experiences of their predecessors, the Qadarites and the followers of Ghaylan, who had risen in unfavourable circumstances and been cut down by the swords of the Umayyads.

Their approach to the Umayyads and the early Abbasids therefore took two forms: an effort to influence the caliphate and guide it towards reform, or organised underground activity. They did in fact succeed in winning over some of the early Umayyad caliphs, as well as some Abbasid rulers, who adopted their views and committed themselves to reform. The Umayyad caliph Yazid ibn al-Walid ibn Abd al-Malik, for instance, believed in human free will and encouraged people to accept it. He was the first caliph to embrace the five principles of the Mu'tazilites.[^MT-70] They backed him in his contest with al-Walid ibn Yazid until he secured the caliphate.[^MT-71] Yazid's rise to power can be seen as a political effort by the Mu'tazilites to set things right: they supported him for his piety and upright character against a rival notorious for his debauchery. Little wonder that Yazid looked to the Mu'tazilites for guidance and tried to put Islamic law into practice. Their leaders rose to great prominence under his rule. They came to dominate the affairs of government and guide them in accordance with their principles, even advising the caliph on his choice of successors who shared his religious devotion and integrity.[^MT-72] Wellhausen[^MT-73] was therefore right to say that the influence of <!-- PDF page 105 | printed page 107 --> the Mu'tazilites on Yazid extended beyond religion into politics and government. Their success shows that they were true to their own understanding of the duty to promote what is right and oppose what is wrong: they supported a just and devout leader when they were sure that the conditions were favourable for success.[^MT-74]

They likewise succeeded in winning over Marwan ibn Muhammad, the last of the Umayyad caliphs. He adopted their beliefs and made a determined effort to introduce reforms. But the problems he faced were too great to overcome, and Umayyad rule came to an end in 132 AH. So the Mu'tazilites had backed two losing horses: the first died within months of becoming caliph, and the second was brought down by the Abbasid revolution. They took the same approach to the Abbasids, offering counsel and advice and seeking to influence the government, as we shall see in more detail later.

The other side of their approach was organised underground activity towards the end of Umayyad rule. Like the parties of the left, they began sending their advocates to the far reaches of the Islamic world to spread their teachings. It is extremely difficult to investigate the part the Mu'tazilites played in this respect, because the books dealing with their movement have been lost, above all those written by Wasil ibn Ata. Al-Khayyat[^MT-75] mentions a work of his known as *The Book of the Movement*, of which no trace has been found. We know that Wasil was the one who organised the underground movement, devised its methods and plans, and defined its aims and purposes. It seems to us that the movement as Wasil conceived it had two purposes: one religious, the other political. He sent his teachers to distant regions to spread Islam in the far reaches of India, Turkestan and North Africa. He himself <!-- PDF page 106 | printed page 108 --> played a part in this work, travelling far and wide to preach Islam. Three thousand men embraced the faith through him.[^MT-76] One of the Mu'tazilite poets said of him:

> A teacher inspired in all he sets out to do,  
> A man of countless thoughts, a traveller of distant horizons.

The leading Mu'tazilite thinkers carried on this work both in his lifetime and after his death. Ibn Khallikan[^MT-77] tells us that Abu al-Hudhayl al-Allaf spread Islam among large numbers of followers of the old Persian religion. The debates of the Mu'tazilites with their opponents in other Islamic schools and with the leaders of other religions leave no doubt about the part they played.

The political side of the underground Mu'tazilite movement grew out of their conviction that there must be a leader who “enforces judgements and the prescribed penalties, raises armies, distributes the spoils of war and alms, does justice to those who have been wronged and exacts justice from wrongdoers, appoints governors and judges in every region, and sends Quran readers and preachers to every corner of the land”.[^MT-78] When the Umayyads disappointed their hopes of achieving this aim, and they realised that the Abbasids too had turned their backs on justice and reform, they resolved to advocate a leader from among their own ranks, through a tightly organised movement with a clearly defined policy. Al-Murtada[^MT-79] says that Basra was the centre of the movement and that Wasil sent his advocates to North Africa, Khurasan, Yemen, Upper Mesopotamia, Kufa and Armenia, as well as Syria and the Hijaz.[^MT-80] He is reported to have been “successful in <!-- PDF page 107 | printed page 109 --> founding and organising his association and setting out its plans”,[^MT-81] and in choosing men who were not simply thinkers absorbed in ideas, but who took part in political and social life[^MT-82] and called for a state founded on “justice and the oneness of God”. Judge Abd al-Jabbar gives us information about Wasil's efforts and his principal advocates in the provinces, information he certainly drew from al-Jahiz.[^MT-83] In his words:

> Wasil sent his emissaries far and wide to call people to the religion of God. To North Africa he sent Abd Allah ibn al-Harith, and the people answered his call. There is a town there called al-Bayda, said to have a hundred thousand people bearing arms, whose inhabitants are known as the Wasiliyya. He sent al-Qasim ibn al-Sa'di to Yemen, Ayyub ibn al-Awtar to Upper Mesopotamia, Hafs ibn Salim to Khurasan, al-Hasan ibn Dhakwan and Sulayman ibn Arqam to Kufa, and Uthman ibn Abi Uthman al-Tawil to Armenia.

The teachers were to spend a year devoted to worship in the mosques, until people came to trust their piety and devotion. They would then offer religious guidance. Once they had won over the people of the region, they would write to the leader of the movement in Basra, who would set a day for them to declare their Mu'tazilite beliefs openly.[^MT-84] An important poem quoted by al-Jahiz provides striking evidence that the Mu'tazilites turned to organised underground activity, <!-- PDF page 108 | printed page 110 --> just as the other opposition parties had done.[^MT-85]

What is certain, though, is that the Mu'tazilites never succeeded in establishing a state based on their own beliefs, as the Kharijites and the Shia had done. Their efforts led only to the formation of close-knit communities living as minorities in states established in North Africa by the Kharijites and the descendants of Ali. Interestingly, these communities lived in harmony with the rulers descended from Ali whose protection they enjoyed, while they were hostile to the rulers of the Kharijite states. In the Idrisid state, founded by descendants of Ali in the far western reaches of North Africa in 172 AH, the Mu'tazilites lived peacefully in Tangier and won the trust of its rulers.

In the Ibadi Rustamid state, founded in central North Africa in 162 AH, however, the Mu'tazilites pitched their tents outside its capital, Tahert,[^MT-86] and continually caused trouble for its rulers. The two sides clashed repeatedly with words and weapons, sometimes winning and sometimes losing. The Wasiliyya community in the Rustamid state, <!-- PDF page 109 | printed page 111 --> numbering thirty thousand, remained a source of unrest and turmoil until the state fell in 297 AH.[^MT-87]

The same was true of their stance towards the Sufri state of the Banu Midrar in south-western North Africa. There, the Mu'tazilites worked to undermine the state in favour of its neighbours, the descendants of Ali.[^MT-88]

Their cooperation with the descendants of Ali in both east and west can be traced to their anger at the early Abbasids and their inability to bring down Abbasid rule through revolution. They therefore placed their hopes in supporting the movements of Ali's descendants that opposed the caliphate, or in winning them over and steering them towards Mu'tazilite beliefs. Let us look at this more closely.

The Mu'tazilites did not welcome the Abbasid caliphate despite its favouring the mawali. This shows that ethnic partisanship played no part in their political stance. Since they were not in a position to take up arms in the name of promoting right and opposing wrong, they contented themselves with giving counsel and offering well-meant advice. Their leaders refused the posts and cooperation that the caliph al-Mansur requested of them.[^MT-89]

This explains their sympathy for the descendants of Ali, who had raised the banner of opposition against their own cousins. The intellectual affinity between the Shia and the Mu'tazilites had deep roots, going back to the days when Zayd ibn Ali Zayn al-Abidin studied under Wasil ibn Ata. Indeed, it was said that all of Zayd's companions were Mu'tazilites.[^MT-90] The Mu'tazilites therefore joined the Zaydis in the uprisings <!-- PDF page 110 | printed page 112 --> led by Muhammad al-Nafs al-Zakiyya (the Pure Soul) and his brother Ibrahim against al-Mansur, who did not hesitate to crush the Zaydis and the Mu'tazilites alike.[^MT-91] When al-Mansur summoned Amr ibn Ubayd, the leader of the Mu'tazilites, to find out where he stood, Amr made no attempt to conceal his sympathy for the rebels.[^MT-92]

Naturally, the Mu'tazilites remained hostile to the caliphate under al-Mahdi, who went to great lengths to persecute groups whose beliefs differed from those of the Sunnis. Things continued much as before under Harun al-Rashid. Anyone who imagines that the Mu'tazilites were able to breathe freely under al-Rashid and began to set their sights on power is mistaken.[^MT-93] In reality, al-Rashid banned religious debate and imprisoned scholars of theology,[^MT-94] including Thumama ibn Ashras.[^MT-95] It is hardly surprising that the Mu'tazilites fled Iraq under both al-Rashid and his son al-Amin, for fear of persecution.[^MT-96]

The Mu'tazilites finally succeeded in bringing the caliphate under their influence with the reign of al-Ma'mun, who <!-- PDF page 111 | printed page 113 --> adopted their beliefs, drew their leading thinkers into his inner circle and declared Mu'tazilite thought the official doctrine of the state. Their standing grew enormously, and their numbers swelled during his reign.[^MT-97] They took up important posts and came to dominate the government and its administration. Their most famous figure was Ahmad ibn Abi Duad, who served as a minister to al-Ma'mun, al-Mu'tasim and al-Wathiq. Under these rulers, the doctrine that the Quran was created became official policy. Scholars and jurists were put to the test, and those among the traditionalists who held that the Quran had existed eternally were persecuted, most notably Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal.[^MT-98]

Under the caliph al-Mutawakkil the tide turned back in favour of the Sunnis, and the Mu'tazilites endured their bitterest ordeal. One of al-Mutawakkil's first acts as caliph was to forbid people to teach that the Quran was created and to issue orders to that effect throughout the empire.[^MT-99] The traditionalists then turned on the Mu'tazilites, burning their books and subjecting them to severe persecution. The resurgence of Sunni influence, which lasted until 334 AH, went hand in hand with political chaos, social injustice and intellectual decline.[^MT-100] The fortunes of the Mu'tazilites fell not only because of persecution by the state and the growing influence of the Hanbalites, but also because the movement itself splintered into thirty-two rival groups. It was during this period that Ibn al-Rawandi broke away and denounced their views in his book *The Disgrace of the Mu'tazilites*. Abu al-Hasan al-Ashari likewise abandoned Mu'tazilite thought and founded <!-- PDF page 112 | printed page 114 --> a school of thought that set out to defend Sunni beliefs using rational arguments.

When the Buyids took control of Iraq in 334 AH, the Mu'tazilites emerged once more. The Buyids were Shia, and they went to great lengths to humiliate the caliphs and strip them of their influence and power. The Mu'tazilites saw an opportunity to influence the new rulers and succeeded in winning some of them over to their beliefs. The Buyid ruler Adud al-Dawla was himself a supporter of Mu'tazilite thought.[^MT-101] The Shia and the Mu'tazilites now made common cause against the Sunnis, and Mu'tazilite ideas found their way into Shia movements opposed to the Sunni caliphate, such as the Qarmatians and the Batiniyya, whose teachings emphasised the hidden meaning of sacred texts.[^MT-102] As Adam Mez puts it, “In matters of belief and doctrine, the Shia were at that time the heirs of the Mu'tazilites.”[^MT-103] One indication of the influence the Mu'tazilites enjoyed under the Buyids is that some of their members held the highest offices. Al-Sahib ibn Abbad became a minister, for instance, while Ibn al-Amid became governor of the region of Rayy.[^MT-104]

Yet the revival of Mu'tazilite thought under the Buyids was little more than a final flicker of life before its death. A new wave of Sunni Turkish conquerors — the Seljuks — swept in from the east and took Baghdad in 448 AH.[^MT-105] Under the Seljuks, the Ash'arites and the Sufis rose to prominence, and Mu'tazilite thought was dealt a fatal blow. The Nizamiyya schools founded by Nizam al-Mulk throughout the Seljuk state promoted Ash'arite teachings, while the emergence of al-Ghazali in this period helped secure the triumph of traditionalist thought and drove the Mu'tazilites from the worlds of ideas <!-- PDF page 113 | printed page 115 --> and politics.[^MT-106]

Thus the political activity of the Mu'tazilites was true to the spirit of their beliefs. It bears witness to the profound connection between enlightened, reasoned thinking and the pursuit of social justice.

<!-- PDF page 114 | blank page (printed page 116) -->

---

<!-- PDF page 115 | printed page 117 -->

## The Qarmatians

### A Pioneering Experiment in Socialism

No movement in Islamic history is more shrouded in mystery than the Qarmatian movement in Iraq and Bahrain. This is not because historical evidence is scarce: Sunni and Shia sources have plenty to say about it. Nor is the problem that their accounts disagree. Almost all of them tell the story from the same point of view — one of extreme hostility and prejudice towards the movement.

The Kharijites, too, suffered at the hands of Sunni and Shia historians who spread falsehoods about them. But we have been able to piece together their side of the story from the little that survives of their writings. With the Qarmatians, we have found nothing of their own written heritage that might lift the veil on what they really thought and believed, the principles behind their movement, or the way they organised their society. Without their side of the story, how can a historian feel confident in the conclusions of their research?

This does not mean that the Qarmatian movement left no intellectual legacy of its own, or that it lacked <!-- PDF page 116 | printed page 118 --> thinkers, people who developed its ideas, or historians who recorded its story. In his *Fihrist* (a catalogue of books), Ibn al-Nadim lists many works by the movement's pioneers and advocates. Among the best known was Abdan, who wrote and compiled so many books and treatises that he came to be known as Abdan the Writer. He wrote no fewer than ten valuable books on Qarmatian beliefs,[^QA-1] revealing the depth and breadth of his knowledge. Al-Nuwayri[^QA-2] describes him as “a man of understanding and skill”. Other Qarmatian writers and thinkers produced a great deal of literature, following Abdan's lead and taking him as their model. In fact, some people doubted whether these works really were written by the people whose names they bore, and attributed them to Abdan himself. Later generations of Qarmatians are said to have passed off their own books as Abdan's to encourage more people to read them.[^QA-3] Clearly, Abdan was not simply a famous advocate who devoted himself to spreading the cause. He founded a school of thought and inspired a community of thinkers who remained true to his ideas long after his death. Yet nothing of that school's legacy has come down to us. All the material on which the study of the Qarmatians rests therefore comes from Sunni and Shia sources alone, sources that are hostile to the movement from beginning to end. And there lies the real problem: the danger of writing a movement's history entirely through the eyes of its enemies, all of whom agreed that it deserved condemnation.

That agreement is no reason to take everything Sunni and Shia historians said about the Qarmatians at face value. Their accounts contain falsehoods and baseless accusations. On this point we disagree with one specialist,[^QA-4] who wrote: <!-- PDF page 117 | printed page 119 --> “It is unreasonable to accuse all these people of making up stories about the Qarmatians, given that they agree with one another.” Surely it is more reasonable to see this very agreement as grounds for doubt rather than proof of the truth.

The political and religious hostility of Sunni and Ismaili historians towards the movement is plain to see. The Qarmatians had built their state at the expense of Abbasid power in Iraq and Bahrain. They had also broken away from the Ismaili movement and refused to recognise the authority of its imams, even though their movement had originally grown out of Ismailism. As for matters of faith, no one can deny the differences between the Qarmatians and the Abbasids. At the same time, the Qarmatians differed from the Ismailis in their interpretation of many religious questions. There is another point to consider. Sunni and Shia historians alike belonged to the “people of the pen” — the educated class whose privileged position depended on the favours and gifts of those in power. It is hardly surprising that both groups looked down on a movement made up of oppressed workers and peasants.

A fair-minded historian must be careful when drawing on these historians' writings. We must put their accounts to the test of reason and try to understand the social realities that gave rise to this movement and others like it. Only then does the extent of the prejudice, the slander and the distortion in the Sunni and Shia accounts come into view. A patient, critical reading can expose the contradictions in their accounts despite their apparent agreement, not to mention the confusion and inconsistencies within the work of a single historian. The accusations and fabrications then collapse at their very foundations. Here is an example.

<!-- PDF page 118 | printed page 120 -->

Sunni and Shia historians all accused the Qarmatians of “abolishing the religious duties prescribed by Islamic law”. Al-Malati[^QA-5] reported that “they claim that prayer, almsgiving, fasting, pilgrimage and all the other religious duties are voluntary, not obligatory”. Al-Baghdadi likewise wrote that “they claimed that anyone who understood the meaning of worship was no longer obliged to perform it”.[^QA-6] Al-Ghazali[^QA-7] took the same line: “The Qarmatians gave up the practice of worship.”

We believe this accusation has no basis in fact. Consider the evidence. Al-Ghazali[^QA-8] himself said that a Qarmatian teacher “required anyone who had embraced the cause to make thirty pilgrimages to the House of God if they failed to fulfil their duties”. Al-Tabari[^QA-9] tells us that the Qarmatians “did not perform the full ritual bath after sexual impurity, but made do with the ritual washing before prayer to cleanse themselves”. Elsewhere, he tells the story of a wealthy landowner who had grown thoroughly exasperated with a Qarmatian peasant working on his estates because the man was so determined to observe his prayers.[^QA-10] The Shia historian al-Maqrizi[^QA-11] tells us that “the Qarmatian preacher Zikrawayh rose in the esteem of the people and gained a position of standing in Islamic law and religion”. He also says that Hamdan ibn al-Ash'ath, the leader of the movement, “was known for his simple life and devotion to worship. His followers called themselves believers whom God had granted victory, defenders of His religion and reformers on earth”.[^QA-12] These passages, and others like them, disprove the claim that <!-- PDF page 119 | printed page 121 --> the Qarmatians sought to tear down the pillars of Islamic law and abandon religious worship.

The words al-Maqrizi quotes above are particularly revealing. They tell us a great deal about the nature of the movement and what it hoped to achieve. It was a social revolution in the name of religion, not a rebellion against religion. Its followers believed in God, defended their faith and rose up to bring about reform. It is worth noting that the social revolutions of this age took place under the banner of a religious movement. One perceptive scholar[^QA-13] recognised this when he spoke of “political and social issues being covered or contained within a religious or theological framework”. Social movements needed a religious justification to establish their legitimacy, just as religious movements could only bear fruit in fertile social soil. This led Adam Mez[^QA-14] to conclude that movements centred on the Mahdi (the awaited leader who would restore justice) were, from the very beginning, political movements that turned to the masses. There is no need to repeat what we said earlier about Ibn Khaldun's famous theory that states are founded when a religious cause and the people bound together in its support are in harmony. We find striking confirmation of this in the birth of the Qarmatian state. The Ismaili movement was in tune with the hopes of the discontented masses in the agricultural lands of Iraq and Bahrain in the latter half of the third century AH.

So what were the actual conditions that brought the social revolution and the Ismaili movement together? To answer that question we need to look at the political, social and cultural life of <!-- PDF page 120 | printed page 122 --> Iraq and Bahrain on the eve of the founding of the Qarmatian state. Al-Nuwayri[^QA-15] sums up these circumstances brilliantly in a few words: “Conflict among those in power, the ruin of Iraq and the decay of the provinces. Because of this, the advocates of the movement and their followers gained a foothold, extended their power across the land and made their influence felt.” By “conflict among those in power”, al-Nuwayri means the political chaos that weakened the caliphate and left the Turks in control of its affairs. They stripped the caliphs of their powers and authority until all that remained were the outward trappings of power: the mention of their names in the Friday sermon or their inscription on coins. The real power lay in the hands of a small circle of military commanders and Turkish chiefs.

The Turkish people consisted of pastoral tribes that roamed the steppes of Central Asia — the lands of Turkestan. Successive waves of devastation swept westwards from this region, overwhelming centres of civilisation. Among them were the famous incursions of the Huns, led by Attila, against the Roman Empire, and the repeated waves of Mongol invasions that swept through western Asia, leaving ruin and destruction in their wake. Some of these tribes lived a simple and harsh existence in the region beyond the Oxus River. Some embraced Islam following the conquests of Qutayba ibn Muslim and Abd Allah ibn Khazim during the Umayyad period. Under the Abbasids, Islam became firmly established as far as the borders of China, and the whole of Turkestan came under Abbasid rule. In the time of al-Ma'mun, the Turks made their appearance on the political stage when he recruited a unit <!-- PDF page 121 | printed page 123 --> of Turkish troops into his army. By the reign of al-Mu'tasim, the Turks had become the dominant force in the Abbasid army, its backbone and mainstay. As more and more of them arrived in the capital, al-Mu'tasim built the city of Samarra for them. He put their courage and skill on horseback to use in crushing revolts and movements that opposed him. In the second Abbasid period, however, the Turks went from being an instrument of the caliphate's will to being a sword hanging over its head. They took command themselves and stripped the caliphs of their authority. The Turkish “commander of commanders” became the real ruler, appointing and dismissing caliphs as he pleased.

Ordinary people were no more immune to the danger and violence than the caliphs were. They suffered robbery, plunder and extortion at the hands of the troops.[^QA-16] The very heart of the state became a battleground between the caliphs and the Turks, who were also bitterly divided among themselves. Political chaos spread through Iraq. The government lost its grip on the outlying regions, and the drive for independence from the state grew stronger. Separatist movements spread like a disease, until many lands had broken away from the caliphate. This political chaos created favourable conditions for the seeds of the Qarmatian movement to take root.

When al-Nuwayri speaks of “the ruin of Iraq and the decay of the provinces”, he means that social problems had grown more acute as society changed and economic troubles deepened. Looking at the different forces within society and the changes they went through, we can see that the Qarmatian movement embraced the oppressed classes of every ethnic and tribal background. This <!-- PDF page 122 | printed page 124 --> calls into question the claims of some Orientalists — such as Blochet and Carra de Vaux — who portrayed the movement as ethnic and nationalist.[^QA-17] In reality, the movement challenged the very ethnic rivalries that the Abbasids had inflamed. The dynasty had thrown the doors wide open to conflict between different peoples and groups, setting the Persians against the Arabs and then using the rough Turkish troops to curb Persian power. In the end, the Turks came out on top, took power for themselves and inflicted severe punishment on the other peoples.

The Qarmatian movement drew in people of every background who had been oppressed by Turkish domination in the agricultural lands of Iraq. Al-Buraqi tells us that the region, known as the Sawad, “contained various classes of people from different nations”, and that people joined the movement regardless of their differences.[^QA-18] Ibn al-Jawzi[^QA-19] tells us that “the Qarmatians were of several kinds... people of the Sawad, Kurds and uncouth foreigners”, as well as “uncouth Arabs”, to use al-Tabari's words.[^QA-20] The fact that these groups were described as “uncouth”, “the lowest of the low” and “the common people” is socially revealing. It tells us that the oppressed classes of every ethnic background welcomed the movement and flocked to join it.

The people of the Sawad whom Ibn al-Jawzi has in mind were the Nabataean peasants who lived in the marshlands known as al-Batiha.[^QA-21] Alongside them were the *Zanj*, Africans brought from <!-- PDF page 123 | printed page 125 --> the east coast of Africa to work on estates and reclaim land in the region of Basra. There were also the *Zutt*, poor Indians whom al-Hajjaj had brought to Iraq and forced to cultivate land that its owners had abandoned. Their conditions deteriorated under the Abbasids. When they rose in revolt, the Turks crushed their uprising with extreme brutality. The Kurds, a tribal people living in northern Iraq, suffered hardship and persecution in the Abbasid period, as well as plunder by Persian and Turkish troops in the frontier districts of al-Jazira bordering Byzantine territory. By “uncouth foreigners”, the historians meant the Persians who lived in Iraqi towns and worked in trades and crafts that Arabs considered beneath them. They suffered humiliation and degradation under Turkish rule. And by “uncouth Arabs” they meant the Arab tribes of the Samawa region. These tribes had returned to their old nomadic life after being discharged from the Abbasid army and removed from the military payroll during the reign of al-Mu'tasim. The same miserable conditions brought all these peoples together. By the end of the third century AH, they formed a social class with little control over its own fate — a class drawn to the Qarmatian movement.

As for the economic conditions that brought social tensions to a head, one scholar sums them up as follows:

> This period marks a decisive turning point in the economic life of Iraq. Turkish domination, together with the natural course of economic development, brought about many changes and new practices. Land grants took on a military character for the first time. Unlawful duties and taxes multiplied, and the currency was manipulated as a means of making savings. Banking reached its highest level of development, an important capitalist class emerged, <!-- PDF page 124 | printed page 126 --> and an organised movement arose among the working class.[^QA-22]

Let us look at this in more detail. Life for peasants and farmers had grown steadily worse on the eve of the Qarmatian movement. In his book *The Experiences of Nations*, Miskawayh gives us plenty of information on the subject. Military landholding became the most distinctive feature of the age. The weakness of the caliphate and the shrinking of its power had reduced the state's income. Matters were made worse by the poor administration of the provinces under Turkish chiefs, whose lack of civilisation was ingrained in them. The caliphate saw no alternative but to grant soldiers estates in return for their military service, instead of paying them salaries from the treasury.[^QA-23] Knowing little about agriculture, the soldiers could not manage the land themselves. They handed over their farms to the Persian *dahaqin*, members of the traditional landowning class, who made relentless use of the forced labour of peasants and enslaved people on the estates.

Alongside military landholding, a system of landholding by government officials grew up at the expense of state lands and the caliphs' own possessions. The Turks kept pressing the caliphs for more money, and the caliphs had little choice but to give in. They were forced to sell both their private property and the state estates known as *sawafi* to satisfy the Turks' demands. Ministers, senior secretaries and administrators had accumulated money and wealth through exploitation and bribery. They bought up these lands and turned them into private estates of their own. The holdings of the official class grew further with the emergence of a system known as *ilja*, or seeking protection. Small landowners <!-- PDF page 125 | printed page 127 --> turned to powerful officials for protection against the oppression of tax collectors. They registered their land in the names of ministers or secretaries in the government records, and paid part of their money in return for protection. The protectors grew more powerful by degrees, while the small farmers' conditions went from bad to worse. The original landowners were reduced to ordinary peasants.[^QA-24] The system of seeking protection in this period bore a strong resemblance to European feudalism in the Middle Ages, when small landowners voluntarily surrendered their land to powerful lords in the hope of securing some measure of protection and safety in an age of chaos and political unrest.[^QA-25]

Alongside the great military landowners and government officials, a new landowning class emerged from among the wealthy merchants, who had close ties to Turkish chiefs, administrators and military commanders. Trade in this period was geared towards serving the upper classes. Wealthy merchants monopolised the right to reclaim the salt-affected land around Basra and drain the marshes of al-Batiha.[^QA-26] They acquired ownership of these lands under Islamic law, which states that anyone who brings dead land back into cultivation has a rightful claim to it.[^QA-27]

In short, the new feudal landowning class consisted of “military commanders, men in robes (that is, government secretaries) and merchants who had received land grants”.[^QA-28] None of them knew much about farming or had any real agricultural experience, and so agriculture sank <!-- PDF page 126 | printed page 128 --> to its lowest ebb. “Water supplies fell into disrepair and useful works ceased to function.” Meanwhile, the farmers suffered poverty and deprivation because the fruits of their toil went to the landowning class. “Calamities fell upon the cultivators, and their circumstances grew desperate.” Many saw no choice but to flee to the towns and take up independent trades. Others reluctantly submitted to injustice, and those who remained became mere wage labourers. Miskawayh writes of how “some were driven to flee, some patiently endured injustice without redress, and others sought relief by handing their estate over to the man who held the land grant, hoping to escape his ill-treatment and come to terms with him”.

While life for the peasants grew steadily worse, the holders of the great estates grew richer and more powerful, taking advantage of the caliphate's weakness and inability to restrain them. “Those who received the land grants remained free of inspection... and there was no supervision to guard against ruin.”[^QA-29] The gap between the classes widened until social divisions reached their breaking point. The Qarmatian movement found fertile ground among the mass of peasants and people bound to the land.

Workers and artisans in the towns were no better off than the people of the countryside. With so many farmers migrating to the towns, employers had an abundance of labour at their disposal and exploited their workers. And just as the workers resented their employers for squeezing all they could out of them, the employers resented the state for failing to protect them from extortion by the Turkish troops, who constantly raided the workshops <!-- PDF page 127 | printed page 129 --> to rob and plunder them, especially since all the workshops were concentrated in one place.[^QA-30]

Like the farming population of the villages, the working class in the towns was divided into two groups: free people and enslaved people. In the language of the time, the free workers were known as “the common people”. Al-Ghazali looked down on them and dismissed them as ignorant. Most of them were mawali, since Arabs disdained manual work and were the least likely to take up a craft.[^QA-31] Alongside these free workers were groups of people whom employers forced to work in their industries, just as their counterparts in the countryside were forced to cultivate and reclaim the land.

In fact, the urban working class was more aware of the social problems of the day. The very nature of their work brought people who shared a trade together and gave them a sense of solidarity. Each craft had its own head, known as the *ustadh*, or master, who supervised the workers and craftsmen in his workshop. Since the trades and industries of a town were gathered together in the same area, the ties between the masters grew stronger, and they joined forces to resist raids by the troops.

Just as the Abbasid revolution had recruited its advocates from this urban middle class, the Qarmatians found in it exactly the people they were looking for. They chose their advocates from among the masters, so that the movement's message could reach the mass of workers under their influence and bring them into an organised movement. Some scholars have therefore concluded that <!-- PDF page 128 | printed page 130 --> the first Islamic trade associations were founded in this period thanks to the Qarmatian movement. Massignon[^QA-32] wrote that “these associations were a weapon wielded by Qarmatian advocates in their struggle to unite the working class of the Islamic world”. Bernard Lewis supported this view, pointing to the way the Brethren of Purity (a community of thinkers) celebrated work and workers. The origins of these Islamic associations are a thorny question in Islamic history, however. Other scholars disagree. Dr al-Duri argues that the associations appeared in the second century AH, before the Qarmatian movement, and presents a number of sources and pieces of evidence in support of his position.

We can reconcile these two views without accepting either of them completely. The associations probably existed before the Qarmatians emerged, but they did not appear as early as the second century AH, as Dr al-Duri suggests. The actual conditions for their emergence were not yet in place. Until the reign of al-Wathiq (232 AH), the Abbasid caliphate was at the height of its power. The Turks were still an instrument of the caliphate, used wherever it wished to put them to work. It was only in the second Abbasid period that they became a threat, harassing the population and treating craftsmen brutally. And we know, as Dr al-Duri himself points out, that the caliphs of the first Abbasid period enjoyed good relations with the masters of the trades. The caliph personally selected them and guaranteed their safety <!-- PDF page 129 | printed page 131 --> and peace of mind. There was therefore no need for such associations at that time. Their emergence was tied instead to the rise of Turkish domination and the raids on markets and workshops. It was then that the need arose for trade associations to protect people from the danger posed by the troops. The most likely time for their emergence was therefore the fourth decade of the third century AH, not the second century as Dr al-Duri suggests.[^QA-33]

Lewis was also mistaken in linking the birth of the trade associations to the Qarmatian movement on the strength of the *Epistles of the Brethren of Purity* and their celebration of working people. The Epistles did not appear before the first half of the fourth century AH,[^QA-34] many years after the Qarmatians emerged. The reasonable conclusion is that the associations really did exist before the Qarmatian movement, but that the movement gave them a new form and purpose. Originally, they had been open organisations made up exclusively of the masters of the trades, formed to manage their relations with the authorities and defend their interests. Under the influence of the Qarmatian movement, they became underground organisations that brought masters and workers together to overthrow the existing order and establish a new society.[^QA-35]

Trade, meanwhile, flourished in this period thanks to control over the gateways and routes of <!-- PDF page 130 | printed page 132 --> international commerce. After the lands of Turkestan had been brought under Abbasid rule as far as the borders of China, the overland route through Asia was under their control. Caravans set out from eastern Asia and travelled through Turkestan, Persia and Iraq, then on through Syria, Egypt and North Africa, from where the goods were transported to European ports. The Abbasids also dominated the eastern seas. Their ships reached China itself, where a Muslim community of merchants settled in the port of Canton. Abbasid vessels crossed the seas around China, the Indian Ocean and the Persian Gulf.[^QA-36] This gave new life to Muslim ports on the Gulf, such as Siraf and al-Ubulla. Basra grew into a bustling commercial city, alive with activity and movement. Goods travelled from Basra to Baghdad and onwards to the ports of Syria and Europe, or headed towards the Red Sea and were then carried overland to the ports of Egypt. They could also reach the ports of North Africa and from there be shipped to Europe.[^QA-37] The Islamic world reaped enormous profits from international trade. As Adam Mez[^QA-38] puts it:

> Trade was one of the signs of Islam's splendour, and it became <!-- PDF page 131 | printed page 133 --> the reigning force in its lands. Muslim ships and caravans travelled across every sea and country, and Muslim merchants took first place in world trade. Baghdad even set prices for the world in those days, at least where luxury goods were concerned.

The last part of Mez's remark is particularly important. It tells us that trade and the wealth it brought in were devoted first and foremost to the comfort and luxury of the caliphs, military commanders, ministers and government secretaries. It is hardly surprising that the chief goods traded were luxuries, such as carpets, jewels and silk, as well as enslaved people, “whose market depended on senior officials and the wealthy”.[^QA-39] We see confirmation of this in the commercial importance of Baghdad and Basra. Baghdad was home to the caliph, surrounded by the military aristocracy and the powerful ministers and secretaries of the state. Basra was home to a prosperous merchant class. Ibn al-Faqih,[^QA-40] who visited it around 290 AH, wrote that “the people who go furthest afield in search of a livelihood are the people of Basra and Himyar. Anyone who goes to the farthest parts of Farghana or the remotest reaches of al-Sus is sure to meet a man from Basra and a man from Himyar there”.

It is clear from this that Iraqi society, on the eve of the rise of the Qarmatians, had entered a commercial age. Trade had gone from being a secondary activity to becoming one of the main pillars of the economy. This economic transformation shook the structure of society and reshaped it on new foundations. A merchant class emerged that acquired great estates and forced masses of peasants <!-- PDF page 132 | printed page 134 --> and enslaved people to work on them, as we have seen. The luxury enjoyed by this class grew at the expense of the working people. “Capital and luxury were closely linked in the lands of Islam, and the great merchants were those who traded in the goods of luxury and comfort.”[^QA-41] A new group of businessmen and financiers grew up alongside the wealthy merchants. Moneylenders emerged who likewise made their fortunes at the expense of the poor. Most of these were Jews with experience in the arts of banking and finance.[^QA-42]

In short, the political chaos and growing divisions in society had created the actual conditions for the Qarmatian movement to take root in Iraq in the latter half of the third century AH. The rich grew richer and the poor sank deeper into poverty.[^QA-43] Discontent among workers, small farmers and artisans reached its peak.[^QA-44] Yet that discontent could only turn into effective action once people became aware of their shared position as a class. That awareness grew out of the intellectual flowering of the age, and in particular from the turn in Shia thought towards the cause of social justice.

The cultural revival had been gathering strength since the reign of al-Ma'mun, when a great movement to translate works into Arabic got under way. At the House of Wisdom in Baghdad, translators rendered <!-- PDF page 133 | printed page 135 --> the great works of Greek and Eastern learning into Arabic. Muslims discovered the intellectual heritage of the ancient Greek, Persian and Indian civilisations, and eagerly set about studying it and making it their own, encouraged by that enlightened caliph. Islamic thought came increasingly to rest on reason, as political and religious schools of thought began to build their philosophies on foundations of logic.

Different currents of thought emerged in this age, but two stood out. One placed reason and human beings at the heart of its thinking. It was championed by the Mu'tazilites, the pioneers of reasoned thinking in Islam, and was further developed by philosophers such as al-Kindi, al-Farabi and Ibn Sina. The other clung to tradition and the literal meaning of sacred texts. It was represented by the Ash'arites and the Hanbalites, and reached its height in the work of al-Ghazali. The two currents clashed bitterly. What divided them was not simply a disagreement over ideas or religious beliefs: it was above all a clash of political and social positions.

It is only natural that Shia thought should have kept pace with this intellectual revival and drawn on it in shaping its beliefs. The Shia were a party at the mercy of those in power, and they embraced the current of thought that valued reason and humanity in speaking to the masses who resented the authorities. We know how the Abbasids took over the underground Shia movement and deprived their cousins, the descendants of Ali, of the fruits of their struggle against the Umayyads. The first Abbasid period was therefore marked by revolts led by the descendants of Ali, which the Abbasids lost no time in crushing with savage brutality. The Shia movement suffered bitterly, both from Abbasid persecution and from divisions among the descendants of Ali themselves. The Zaydis also split into several groups whose ideas ranged from moderate to extreme. This division doomed all their revolts to failure, despite their number <!-- PDF page 134 | printed page 136 --> and the threat they posed to the authorities.

The other branch, known as the Imami Shia, split into two groups: the Twelvers and the Ismailis. The Ismailis grew in influence until they had drawn most of the Twelvers into their fold. Unlike the Zaydis, the Ismailis turned to an organised underground movement after the Zaydis' ill-considered uprisings had failed.

The Ismailis recognised how dangerously divided Iraqi society had become under Turkish domination. They adapted their beliefs in a way that would appeal to the oppressed classes. In shaping their religious teachings, they drew on the tradition of reasoned thinking, and they saw nothing wrong with taking from the pre-Islamic heritage those ideas that valued reason and humanity.[^QA-45] They also embraced the demand for social justice of the discontented masses of Iraq, bringing these two currents together within a religious framework. Their high standing in religious life, as members of the Prophet's family, gave reasoned thought and the cause of social justice a distinctive religious character.[^QA-46] This helps explain why their movement caught on so widely and why people flocked to it with such enthusiasm. Religion gave <!-- PDF page 135 | printed page 137 --> social revolutions a framework that made them legitimate, as well as providing the ideas that inspired them. As one scholar puts it, “Islamic history knows no revolutionary doctrine that does not rest on a spiritual foundation, and no broad revolutionary movement that does not turn to religion.”[^QA-47] Another researcher explains the point in more detail:[^QA-48]

> In the Islamic era, it was common for groups advocating political revolution to appear under the cover of Shia beliefs. This helped them spread their message and rally their followers until they had secured their position, at which point they revealed their true purpose. The Shia cause was one of the most powerful chords that could be struck in the Islamic world. The organisers of political movements drew on it to spread their message and rally people around them.

Whether it was the Ismaili movement that absorbed Iraq's social revolution, or the social revolution that used the Shia cause as a means to its own ends, what is certain is that the movement and the revolution came together. Their meeting brought the revolution to victory, after which it broke away from Ismailism. The discontented masses could not have achieved their demands without it: the movement kindled revolutionary awareness among them and gave the revolution the legitimacy it needed to begin. Yet the religious movement on its own could never have achieved its aims without giving due weight to the question of social justice.[^QA-49]

<!-- PDF page 136 | printed page 138 -->

It was in this way that the Qarmatian movement became linked to Ismailism. The Ismaili imam lived in concealment in Salamiyya, south of Homs, while his advocates called people to his cause in the provinces. His representative in Iraq, a man named Husayn al-Ahwazi, made contact with Hamdan ibn al-Ash'ath, who was known by the name Qarmat,[^QA-50] and brought him into the Ismaili movement. Hamdan succeeded in spreading the cause in the agricultural lands of Iraq and in Bahrain. Once he felt strong enough to stand on his own, he broke away from the movement and acted independently.

Whatever may be said about the causes of the split,[^QA-51] one thing is certain: the Qarmatian movement benefited from Ismailism while thwarting its attempt to absorb it. This underlines how small a part differences over religious beliefs played in comparison with the movement's social aims. We can see this in the way its leaders felt no hesitation in showing sympathy for the Abbasid caliphs at one moment and declaring their allegiance to the Fatimids at another, depending on what circumstances required to protect their state from the dangers posed by the two greater powers. Nor did they hesitate to invade the lands of both states when opportunities arose.[^QA-52] Anyone who studies the beliefs and institutions of the Qarmatians <!-- PDF page 137 | printed page 139 --> finds fundamental differences between them and those of the Ismailis, despite the insistence of some historians[^QA-53] that “the Qarmatians are a branch of Ismailism”.

There is no doubt that Hamdan ibn al-Ash'ath, that Nabataean villager, was a man of extraordinary ability and keen intelligence. These qualities fitted him to take over the leadership of the Ismaili underground organisation in Iraq from the Ismaili advocate Husayn al-Ahwazi. From 261 to 278 AH, he worked tirelessly to organise the movement and refine its plans. In 278 AH he declared revolt against the Abbasids and defeated their armies one after another. He succeeded in laying the foundations of an independent state in the agricultural lands of Iraq, which he ruled until 296 AH. The Qarmatians turned their sights towards Syria and won several victories there. But the Abbasids managed to halt their expansion and eventually destroy their state in Iraq. They failed to overthrow the Qarmatians of Bahrain, however, whose state survived until 398 AH.

We need not recount the whole history of the Qarmatians in Iraq and Bahrain here. What interests us is the extraordinary ingenuity of the underground movement that brought this state into being and kept it a thorn in the sides of both the Abbasid and Fatimid caliphates for more than a century.

What we know on this subject comes from sources hostile to the Qarmatians, such as *Unveiling the Secrets of the Batiniyya and the Qarmatians* by al-Hammadi al-Yamani and *The Infamies of the Batiniyya* by al-Ghazali. Both books confuse the Qarmatian movement with the Ismaili movement. It is true that the Qarmatians were influenced to a very great extent <!-- PDF page 138 | printed page 140 --> by the Ismailis' underground system, but they had their own distinctive features. Ismailism itself varied in its traditions and institutions depending on the setting in which it took root. “Ismailism in Syria differs in belief, philosophy and social character from Ismailism in Yemen, and Ismailism in Persia is different from Ismailism in North Africa, and so on.”[^QA-54] Ismailism also changed over time: “We find an enormous gulf between the Ismailis of the fourth century AH and their successors in the fifth.”[^QA-55]

Even so, we can learn something of the nature of the Qarmatian movement, its institutions and methods, and the extent to which it was shaped by the social conditions and spirit of the age. The first thing that stands out is how the Qarmatians of Iraq and Bahrain drew on the philosophical and organisational traditions of the Sabians of Harran. The Sabians had a long tradition of expertise in astronomy, which the Qarmatians put to the service of their movement.[^QA-56] They believed the affairs of the world were governed by the seven wandering heavenly bodies.[^QA-57] This explains why their movement began by advocating the seventh Ismaili imam. Following the Ismaili model, they also divided the territory covered by their movement into twelve regions. In each, the preacher had thirty representatives under him, each supervising twenty-four advocates. This pattern was based on astronomy: a year is divided into twelve months, a month into thirty days and a day into twenty-four hours. This is how <!-- PDF page 139 | printed page 141 --> the Qarmatians drew on astronomical knowledge to develop a philosophical view of the universe that shaped the way their movement was organised. Hence the truth of Mez's[^QA-58] remark about “the influence on the Qarmatians of the ancient schools of thought that had existed in Iraq”.

When Hamdan ibn al-Ash'ath took independent charge of the movement and became a “supreme advocate”,[^QA-59] he remained true to the philosophy centred on the number seven.[^QA-60] He divided the movement into seven ranks, each with a body of teachings suited to the individual's place in the organisation. The first set of teachings was for the general public, the second for those slightly above them, and the third for people who had belonged to the movement for a year. A further set of teachings was given to them with each passing year until they reached the seventh rank and received the seventh set.[^QA-61] These final teachings contained the secrets of the faith and its fundamental truths.[^QA-62]

Perhaps the most important of these truths, which Hamdan disclosed to his inner circle of representatives, was that he was calling people to follow him, not the Ismaili imam. This secret was kept from everyone else. “The representatives would give the advocates seals of white clay inscribed with the words: Muhammad ibn Ismail, the imam, the Mahdi, the friend of God.”[^QA-63] This encouraged people to rally to the movement because of its connection with the Prophet's family.

Hamdan was careful to choose his representatives from among his relatives and relatives by marriage.[^QA-64] He then <!-- PDF page 140 | printed page 142 --> assigned them territories in which to spread the movement's message, and they dispersed across those territories.[^QA-65] He maintained close and well-established ties with them, and is credited with using carrier pigeons to exchange letters[^QA-66] with these representatives.

The advocates fell into two ranks. The first was known as the *mustajib*, and the second as the *mukasir*, or debater. Abdan, Hamdan's relative by marriage, is said to have written a book on the criteria for selecting advocates called *The Limits*. It set out the qualities expected of them: knowledge, piety, political understanding, tact, cunning, the ability to argue a case and to speak persuasively, and so on.

The mustajib was expected to be intelligent and presentable, and capable of making an impression and attracting followers. As some scholars have put it, he was “rather like a hunting dog that brings in the quarry”.

Once he had gained experience and proved his ability, he rose to the rank of mukasir, someone who could demolish his opponents' arguments. He therefore needed an intimate knowledge of the movement, extensive learning and the ability to challenge the religious teachers of other schools of thought, debate with them and cast doubt on the beliefs of ordinary people by refuting the authorities their teachers relied on. He also had to be tactful and able to raise the issues that affected people's lives, hold those in power responsible for them, and persuade people that their only hope of deliverance lay in the cause of the “awaited Mahdi” he advocated. <!-- PDF page 141 | printed page 143 --> The mukasir was often chosen from among the elders of powerful kinship groups. They could protect him and stand by him in times of danger, or rally around him and join the movement because of his high standing: “People follow the religion of their rulers.”

Hamdan did not give the advocates complete freedom in spreading the message. Instead, he laid down the methods and means they were to use, and drew up plans that they were expected to follow. Sunni and Shia sources[^QA-67] describe nine stages through which an advocate won followers: assessing a person, winning their friendship, introducing doubts, leaving the matter in suspense, binding them to the movement, deception, obfuscation, casting off their former beliefs and finally shedding them altogether. We think there were only seven stages, in keeping with the philosophy of seven on which the organisation was based. We believe that the stages described as “deception and obfuscation” were inventions of the Qarmatians' enemies, designed to discredit the movement. It is scarcely conceivable that the Qarmatians themselves would have labelled their activities in such terms. Moreover, inserting these two stages between binding a person to the movement and casting off their former beliefs is plainly misleading. Without them, the succession of stages makes sense and is in keeping with the spirit of the system and its philosophy centred on seven.

In the first stage — assessing a person — the advocate carefully considered the person whom he hoped to attract to the movement. He spoke to them in language that appealed to them, giving the impression that he shared their particular beliefs, whether they were Sunni, Kharijite, Shia[^QA-68] or otherwise. He sympathised with the troubles they faced and tried to show that the caliphate was responsible for them.

If the person responded favourably, the advocate became more friendly and reassuring, put their troubles in perspective and assured them that deliverance would inevitably come at the hands of one of the men of <!-- PDF page 142 | printed page 144 --> the Prophet's family (peace and blessings be upon him).

Next came the stage of questioning. The advocate sought to free the prospective follower from their existing religious beliefs by drawing a connection between the prevailing doctrine and social injustice. He argued that the corruption of the rulers' policies sprang from the corruption of their doctrine. What was being called into question, then, was the official doctrine of the state, not the Islamic faith itself, as Sunni and Shia historians would have us believe.

When the follower had come to doubt the official doctrine and was eager for an alternative that offered a way out of their predicament, the advocate waited until he was sure of the sincerity of their resolve. Once convinced, he told them plainly that they would have to pledge to keep the teachings of the alternative faith secret. If he doubted their intentions, he turned away from them. This explains why the stage was called “suspense”: the prospective follower's admission to the movement depended on it.

When the follower showed a willingness to obey an imam from the Prophet's family and carry out the advocate's instructions faithfully and diligently, the advocate took a pledge from them. Under its terms they became bound to the movement.[^QA-69] Hence the name of this stage: “binding”.

<!-- PDF page 143 | printed page 145 -->

Closely connected with it was, of course, the stage known as “casting off”.[^QA-70] It meant that the follower stopped practising the teachings of their former school of thought after declaring their allegiance to the movement. It did not mean, as al-Ghazali claims, that they abandoned the religious duties prescribed by Islamic law.

Once the advocate had demonstrated his commitment to the teachings and activities of the movement, he had shed his former beliefs and become a member of the Qarmatian movement. In this way, an active member had been brought into the movement after passing through the seven stages and accepting their respective teachings. We find support for this in Ibn al-Nadim's[^QA-71] account of the “seven sets of teachings” of the Qarmatian movement.

It is clear from all this that the advocates based their message on the link between religious values and social justice. That is why they succeeded in winning <!-- PDF page 144 | printed page 146 --> supporters. Had their message been purely a matter of religious doctrine, it would never have caught on and spread.[^QA-72] The advocates drew their support from the poor and the working classes, for whom the obscure complexities of religious doctrine were beyond their understanding.[^QA-73] But the social message at the heart of the movement, its affirmation of the principle of justice, struck a chord with the discontented peasants, enslaved labourers, workers and artisans in the towns and villages of the agricultural lands of Iraq and Bahrain. No wonder: the advocates gave them money to help them cope with the burdens and hardships of daily life. They also promised that these people would inherit the estates of their masters and oppressors,[^QA-74] and that the property which passed to them would be theirs by right, in accordance with the verse of the Quran: “We wished to show favour to those who had been oppressed in the land, to make them leaders and to make them the heirs.”

For this reason, we reject the view of one scholar[^QA-75] that the social side of the movement came at the expense of its religious character, and that the advocates misled their followers into believing that “the prophets and the sultans had reduced the masses to a state of social slavery”. He seems to have been influenced by an account given by al-Baghdadi:[^QA-76]

> The Qarmatians believed that the prophets, such as Noah, Moses, Jesus and Muhammad, and everyone who claimed to be a prophet, were men of schemes and trickery who wanted to rule over the common people. They deceived them with their tricks and enslaved them through their religious laws.

Al-Baghdadi was one of those writers on religious sects who regarded anyone who differed from Sunni beliefs as an unbeliever and a heretic.

<!-- PDF page 145 | printed page 147 -->

What is clear is that the Qarmatians condemned rulers, not prophets. Consider the testimony of a traveller who visited the region of Bahrain while the Qarmatians were in power. Hostile as he was towards them, he acknowledged that they recognised Muhammad as a prophet[^QA-77] and that their banners bore verses from the Quran affirming the Islamic ideal of justice and calling for it to be put into practice.

Ibn al-Athir[^QA-78] tells a revealing story. Ali ibn Isa, minister to the Abbasid caliph al-Muqtadir and the owner of vast estates, arrested a Qarmatian and asked him why he had embraced the movement's beliefs. The man replied:

> Because I became convinced that it was right. You and your master — the caliph — are unbelievers. You take what does not belong to you.

The point could hardly be clearer. The Qarmatian movement sought to make Islamic justice a reality, not to tear down Islamic law. It aimed to overthrow rulers precisely because they had turned their backs on that law.

Once the state had been founded, Hamdan ibn al-Ash'ath honoured the trust his followers had placed in him. He tried to put social justice into practice in its fullest form through what became known as *ulfa* — a system of communal solidarity. It was an extraordinary experiment in socialism. As a modern researcher describes it:

> Each person handed over what they owned to the community. From then on, a person's place in society was determined by the services they provided to the community and the work they put into advancing its cause.[^QA-79]

There is no doubt in our minds that Hamdan drew his inspiration from the teachings of Islam. The Quran is full of verses that urge people to live in harmony and work together for the common good. Among them are these:

> Remember God's blessing upon you: you were enemies, and He brought your hearts together, so that by His grace you became <!-- PDF page 146 | printed page 148 --> brothers.
>
> Even if you had spent everything on earth, you could not have brought their hearts together. But God brought them together. He is mighty and wise.
>
> Help one another in goodness and reverence for God, and do not help one another in sin and aggression.

There are many more such verses.

Al-Nuwayri[^QA-80] gives us a fascinating account of this pioneering experiment:

> He then made communal solidarity a duty. The people were to pool their belongings in one place and live as a single family. No one was to have an advantage over a brother or companion through the property they owned. In every village, the organisers appointed a trusted man to look after the villagers' shared possessions: cattle, sheep, jewellery, household goods and everything else. Each man worked hard at his trade and earned his standing through his own efforts. Women contributed what they earned from spinning, and boys brought in the wages they earned watching over birds. No one owned anything individually except their sword and weapons.

The system did not disappear when the Qarmatians fell in Iraq. It lived on in their state in Bahrain and won the admiration of Ibn Hawqal,[^QA-81] who left us a clear account of how that state was governed. It was evidently closer to a democratic republic[^QA-82] than to a hereditary monarchy.[^QA-83]

It seems to us that Hamdan Qarmat did not invent this system out of nothing. Rather, he used his own judgement to put his understanding of social justice in Islam into practice in a way that suited a society built on agriculture and trade. In doing so, he drew on ideas that placed human well-being at their centre <!-- PDF page 147 | printed page 149 --> in earlier civilisations, insofar as they did not conflict with the principles of Islam. His efforts in this respect were rather like those of Umar ibn al-Khattab, who used his own judgement in organising the Islamic state after the great conquests.[^QA-84]

The philosophy behind this system was not influenced by the Gnostic Christian belief that rulers were divine because God dwelt within them, as Ibn Hazm argued[^QA-85] and Adam Mez repeated after him.[^QA-86] Nor was it a revival of ancient Persian movements such as Mazdakism, or the later Khurramite and Babakite teachings that, according to al-Baghdadi, called for sexual freedom and the sharing of property and women.[^QA-87] All these accusations were inventions of the movement's enemies — men like al-Baghdadi, al-Ghazali and al-Hammadi, and those who repeated their claims. They all saw the movement as the work of followers of faiths that Islam had defeated, who supposedly infiltrated Islam to destroy it or replace it, either with their old beliefs or with outright atheism.[^QA-88]

We need to examine the exaggerations and excesses of this view. We also need to establish how far the Qarmatian movement was influenced by ideas from outside Islam, and why these influences take nothing away from its religious and social character.

Those who accuse the Qarmatians of believing that God could dwell in human beings, or that souls could pass from one body to another, are mistaken. Such beliefs would have served the movement no purpose at all. <!-- PDF page 148 | printed page 150 --> “The fact that a person's soul might inhabit a bull or some other creature does nothing to establish the truth of the movement's teachings or demonstrate their greatness.”[^QA-89] Had the Qarmatians believed that the spirit of God dwelt in the Ismaili imam, they would not have rebelled against him, broken away from his movement or taken a hostile stance towards the Fatimid imams. Ibn Hazm's accusation that the Qarmatians treated their ruler Abu Sa'id al-Jannabi as a god also makes little sense in view of the fact that they split into rival factions around members of his family.[^QA-90] As for the accusation that they advocated sexual licence and the sharing of sexual partners, it has been refuted by modern scholars such as Bernard Lewis, Muhammad Ulyan and Abd al-Jalil Hasan.

Lewis[^QA-91] traces this misconception to Nizam al-Mulk, the author of the *Siyasatnama* (*Book of Government*), who regarded the Qarmatian movement as an extension of Mazdakism. Yet Mazdakism itself did not advocate sexual licence. The misconception can also be traced to the prejudice of a Shia historian, Ibn Rizam, who was hostile to the Qarmatians for political reasons. Later writers repeated what these two men had said without stopping to question or examine it. We believe that Nizam al-Mulk, a Sunni, looked down on women, regarding them as corrupt creatures and a source of wickedness and sin.[^QA-92] His judgement of Qarmatian women, who enjoyed a high standing in society, was therefore unjust. They went unveiled, worked side by side with men and shared all their burdens and responsibilities on an equal footing. He regarded this as a form of shameful sexual licence.
