Nizam al-Mulk was also a Sunni <!-- PDF page 149 | printed page 151 --> firmly committed to the views of the Ash'arites and the Hanbalites, as well as being minister of the Seljuk military state, which persecuted Ismaili movements with particular severity.

Ibn Rizam claimed that Hamdan Qarmat gathered men and women together on a particular night, when they would mingle. What drove this accusation was his fierce loyalty to the Ismaili movement, from which the Qarmatians had broken away. He tarred them with the vilest accusations in order to discredit them and diminish their standing.

Professor Ulyan rejects these accusations. The Qarmatians were careful to observe and preserve the laws of inheritance. Women held a respected place in Qarmatian society, and the marriage bond was known to be strong. None of this is consistent with the idea of sexual licence.[^QA-93]

Professor Abd al-Jalil Hasan makes a different argument. Mazdakism and the Khurramite movement were both revolutionary social movements. They declared war on the class system and sought justice and equality. Their revolutionary teachings included a rejection of the outdated social arrangements that kept people locked into their social classes, and an effort to relax excessively strict marriage laws for the benefit of oppressed people. Mazdak, for instance, taught that a man could give up one of his wives so that another man who had fallen into poverty could marry her. This, we believe, was a humane principle adopted by the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) when he brought the Emigrants from Mecca and the Helpers of Medina together in a bond of brotherhood. A man from among the Helpers would divorce one of his wives so that an Emigrant could marry her. Abd al-Jalil also refutes the claim that the Khurramites — whose name, in the interpretation of the movement's enemies, meant a doctrine of pleasure — advocated sexual licence. <!-- PDF page 150 | printed page 152 --> He points to the contradictions in the accounts of their beliefs. While al-Baghdadi, for instance, claims that the Khurramites practised the sharing of sexual partners, elsewhere he says that “they built mosques for Muslims and taught their children the Quran”.[^QA-94]

In short, the accusations levelled at the Qarmatians — that they believed in divine indwelling and the passage of souls from one body to another, and advocated licence and forbidden acts — are at odds with the nature of the movement and its honourable historical record.

What is beyond doubt, however, is that the Qarmatian experiment drew on a legacy of enlightened ideas in both Eastern and Western systems and theories. It adapted those ideas to the needs of the age without coming into conflict with the teachings of Islam. We believe that the Qarmatians drew on the scientific revival and intellectual awakening of the period in spreading their message and organising their society. The advances in astronomy made by the Sabians of Harran, for instance, were reflected in the philosophy behind their underground movement, as we noted earlier. They also drew on the ideas Plato put forward in his *Republic* in establishing their political and social institutions. Plato's ideas influenced later schools of thought in East and West alike and found an echo in Islamic philosophy. Sabine[^QA-95] goes so far as to say that Plato's *Republic* was a book for every age, from which later generations drew inspiration for the most diverse schools of thought.

In the *Republic*, Plato tried to establish the essential principles, or lay down <!-- PDF page 151 | printed page 153 --> the best possible plan for an ideal republic, or “virtuous city”. It would be free of all the evils and wrongs that had plagued every society known to him. Virtue would flourish, justice would prevail and its inhabitants would live in harmony.[^QA-96] But Plato carried his idealism too far, and his *Republic* offered a vision of a perfect society that was difficult to realise. Although he abandoned many of his earlier views when he came to write his *Laws*, recognising that he had gone too far, Muslim philosophers followed in his footsteps and drew heavily on his ideas. The Brethren of Purity, who emerged in the middle of the fourth century AH, after the foundation of the Qarmatian state, drew inspiration from Plato's *Republic* in writing many of their essays. But even before the Qarmatian movement appeared, a Muslim philosopher like al-Farabi had written his *Views of the People of the Virtuous City*, modelled on Plato's *Republic*. There is no doubt that al-Farabi brought together the ideas of Plato, the philosophy of Aristotle and the principles of Islam.[^QA-97] He offered a new vision of an ideal society that was likewise difficult to realise. The Qarmatians, however, were the first to put a distinctively Islamic version of such a vision into practice. We can see this by looking at the points of similarity and agreement between Plato's ideal society and the Qarmatian experiment.

Plato based his philosophy on his understanding of the human soul. He believed that it contained three forces: reason, spirit and desire. Accordingly, he divided the work of his republic among three groups: <!-- PDF page 152 | printed page 154 --> the rulers, the guardians and the producers. All three groups were to be educated and prepared for their roles from childhood. The rulers received a special education to develop wisdom and sound judgement, becoming philosophers. The guardians were taught courage and the willingness to face danger. The producers were brought up to value work, self-restraint and moderation. All three groups were bound together by brotherhood, equality and cooperation, with the happiness of everyone as their sole aim. The only distinction between the groups lay in the work they did. Plato believed that justice could not be achieved so long as private ownership existed. He therefore called for its abolition, regarding it as a grave evil. He also advocated the communal sharing of partners among the guardians, although he later abandoned this idea in his *Laws*, praised family life and stressed the importance of strengthening family bonds.[^QA-98]

How far was this philosophy reflected in the Qarmatian experiment? The sources describe an enlightened and discerning group in whose hands political power rested. At its head was a just ruler, no despot, who sought guidance from a council known as the *aqdaniyya*[^QA-99] — those with the authority to make and undo binding decisions. This principle of consultation is the backbone of political democracy in Islam. The members of the council stood on equal terms with their ruler, who was himself one of them, *primus inter pares* — first among equals. He enjoyed no special privilege over them and ruled with justice and fairness, guided by the advice of his peers. He was known as the master, and they as the “counsellors” (*al-ashira*).[^QA-100] All of them belonged to the movement and had been initiated into the secrets of knowledge and wisdom. Is this form of government not strikingly similar to the rule of philosophers envisaged by <!-- PDF page 153 | printed page 155 --> Plato? These were people who had grasped the universal meaning of justice, beauty and the highest good. No single philosopher held power alone; the philosophers governed through collective consultation.[^QA-101]

In Qarmatian society, responsibility for defence fell to a distinct body of warriors who received a purely military upbringing from childhood, in accordance with an established system of education. They were brought together in special houses and trained in horsemanship and the use of arms. Al-Maqrizi[^QA-102] tells us that the fighting force of al-Ahsa numbered twenty thousand soldiers, ready for sacrifice and renowned for their courage in battle.[^QA-103] It seems clear, then, that the Qarmatians were influenced in this respect by Plato's ideas about the guardians. They were given physical training from childhood and, from the age of twelve, went on to <!-- PDF page 154 | printed page 156 --> military training, devoting themselves entirely to defence.[^QA-104]

The producers of Qarmatian society, meanwhile, carried out their role remarkably well. Nasir Khusraw[^QA-105] tells us that thirty thousand people in al-Ahsa worked on farms and in gardens. Workers and artisans were organised into groups according to their trades, and even women and children had particular tasks to perform, as al-Nuwayri also notes.

The Qarmatians also offered assistance to any craftsman who came to settle in their state, providing homes for such people at the community's expense. There is thus a striking resemblance between the way working people were organised in Qarmatian society and Plato's idea of a distinct group of producers whose lives were founded on dedication, self-restraint, cooperation and moderation.[^QA-106]

The high standing enjoyed by women in Qarmatian society and their equality with men in sharing responsibilities and burdens[^QA-107] also find a parallel in Plato's commitment to equality between the sexes. In his system of education, he made no distinction between boys and girls. He recognised the need to put the full abilities of every member of society to use in the service of the state.[^QA-108]

We can therefore say that the Qarmatians drew heavily on Plato's ideas when they established their experiment in socialism in Iraq and Bahrain. But they adapted those ideas to <!-- PDF page 155 | printed page 157 --> the realities of their society and gave them a distinctive character, founded above all on the Islamic principle of justice.

<!-- PDF page 156 | blank page (printed page 158) -->

---

<!-- PDF page 157 | printed page 159 -->

## A Debate on the Approach Taken in This Book

### 1 — Critical Views

This study caught the attention of scholars and of readers interested in Islamic thought. The magazine *Rose al-Yusuf* opened its pages to their comments, most of which were either critical or supportive of the approach I had taken. It seems useful to reproduce the criticisms and my replies as they appeared at the time. For the favourable responses, I will quote selected passages from the articles in question.

The first to speak out against the study was Ahmad Musa Salim, who wrote an article entitled:

#### The Secret Movements in Islamic History Were a Counter-Revolution

Here is what he wrote:

In the last issue of *Rose al-Yusuf*, I read an article by Dr Mahmoud <!-- PDF page 158 | printed page 160 --> Ismail entitled “Secret Political Movements in Islam”. My first reaction was amazement at the sheer wealth of information brimming over in the good doctor's article. In all its variety, it reminded me of red, silver and yellow ornamental fish swimming around a glass bowl, trying to find their way out. But from the point of view of a believing Muslim society fighting for its future on our Arab soil, these ideas and pieces of information are both false and dangerous. I hoped, therefore, to take up some of the work of the Muslims who had confronted and corrected such ideas before me. I want to apply the test of scholarly honesty to our understanding of the nature of Islam and the history of the Arab nation that fought for it. And I would like to point out to the author, if he is willing to listen, that the ideas he repeats from others, imagining them to be scientific and objective, are nothing but inventions that feed off Islam and attack it. They die the moment they come into contact with the living reality they seek to penetrate.

The author's lengthy article boils down to this: the secret political movements that eventually brought down Arab rule in the Arab homeland — among them the Qarmatians, the Ismailis and the Kharijites — were, in his view, engaged in a legitimate struggle against what he calls the hereditary monarchy of the Umayyads and the Abbasids' Persian-style religious monarchy. He even sees them as early forms of the progressive Islamic left.

The article has a purpose, of course. After a long series of barbs and claims hostile to the Arabs, it aims to persuade modern Muslims that Islam is a revolution that depends on organised underground activity — the very opposite of its true nature. Through such activity, we are supposed to believe, the likes of the Qarmatians and the Ismaili followers of hidden religious teachings proved that Muhammad's call to the Quran and Islam was a revolution of the left.

<!-- PDF page 159 | printed page 161 -->

Before going any further, I would do well to remind the author of a simple truth. An impartial study of the long story of religion — the true religion revealed by God — from the life of Abraham to that of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon them both, shows that the finest moments in the struggle of the prophets were those when they spoke out openly, with a clear and resounding call to God. They stood without fear, without disguises and without secret activity before kings who claimed divine powers and arrogant tyrants. This openness, backed by proof and strength, is part of the very nature of religion: its way of explaining life and its purpose of transforming the human soul through faith.

To brush aside this truth, clear as the sun, the author assembles a whole range of old and new ideas and beliefs hostile to the Arabs. I need only point to a few of them here:

- He tries to present a modern anti-Arab interpretation of Islam based on the materialist view of history. To do so, he summarises Bandali Jawzi's book, *The History of Intellectual Movements in Islam*.

Jawzi sees Muhammad's call as an example of a revolution led by a national leader who felt the pain of slaves and the poor and rallied them against the wealthy ruling class of Mecca. As you can see, this is a naive and sterile attempt to impose materialist thinking on Islam. There is not a trace of scientific, impartial study of the history of religion or the history of the Arabs. As far as we know, both stretch back thousands of years.

- Following in Jawzi's footsteps, the author tries to divide Muslims into a left and a right. Yet for those who believe in Islam, it is neither right nor left. It is <!-- PDF page 160 | printed page 162 --> “certain knowledge”. Its principles for shaping and developing the individual and society go beyond the left's theories of progress, just as they reject the right's means of oppression. Indeed, if we must judge by the standards of materialist thinking, true Islam is entirely on the left compared with the backward, godless right represented by these secret Qarmatian and Ismaili movements. The author celebrates them, even though they were counter-revolutions directed against Islam.

- The author admits that the Qarmatians and others drew on ideas that had no Islamic roots: Greek thought, Jewish traditions concerned with hidden spiritual knowledge, and pagan beliefs from the East. Yet he tries to defend them with prejudiced and academically dishonest claims. With the brazen hostility of an anti-Arab partisan, he accuses the community of the Prophet's city and the first home of Islam of having built its religious mission on the legacy of other civilisations. He claims that the Islamic empire owed its culture and civilisation — as Zionist Orientalist scholarship claims — to the Library of Alexandria, Christian monasteries, Jewish synagogues and Zoroastrian fire temples.

Can this be taken seriously?

- He tries to make the word “Qarmatians” the recurring refrain in his anti-Arab song. Through it, he offers an idea alien to religion and false to history: that the Qarmatians were Muslim revolutionaries fighting for freedom within “communes”, which he considers the ideal form of organised revolutionary action in Islam. Yet it is well known that the Qarmatian movement was one of the secret movements that sought to establish a settler presence in the Arab homeland, aiming to conquer it from within through ideas and beliefs and pave the way for military conquest from outside.

<!-- PDF page 161 | printed page 163 -->

According to Father Anastas al-Karmali, in his explanation of the name derived from their leader, Hamdan ibn Qarmat, the Qarmatians' name comes from the Aramaic word *qarmatuna*, meaning “deceiver” or “wicked man”. Was the leftist Hamdan ibn Qarmat, who emerged from the secret cellars of the counter-revolution against Islam in the town of Salamiyya, a desecrator or a progressive?

Throughout their bloody underground history, the Qarmatians were paid agents of foreign conquest and deceivers who led a counter-revolution against Islam. In pursuit of their plans for settler conquest, they set about slaughtering Muslims, looting property, permitting immoral acts, treating women as common property, attempting to destroy the Kaaba and promoting secret leaders whom they claimed were infallible and divine. Could anyone addressing Muslims honestly call this progressive action?

In closing, my hope is not so much to change Dr Mahmoud Ismail's ideas as to persuade him to reconsider the worth of the evidence and information he relies on. I would also like him to recognise that modern progressive Arab action after the Egyptian revolution of 1952 and the resistance struggle on which it draws — both of them resting on greater clarity, understanding and commitment to the nature of faith and the truth of Islam — are founded on openness, clarity, honesty in matters of belief and revolutionary purity, not on secret activity or covert infiltration.

Above all, I hope everyone who read that article will recognise the dangers it contains. In our age, we badly need to renew our culture from its original sources, on the basis of a sound understanding of Islam and Arab history. Only then will intellectuals break their habit of always making their ideas dance to the tunes of <!-- PDF page 162 | printed page 164 --> the two superpowers: the right or the left.

---

Our reply to Ahmad Musa Salim's criticism appeared in an article entitled:

#### Criticising an Approach Without Having One

Here is the text:

At first, I had no wish to respond to Ahmad Musa Salim. I thought entering into a dispute of this kind would be a rather foolish exercise, leading to nothing but a pointless exchange of insults.

In the end, however, I became convinced that a reply was necessary. It was time to expose the false claims of those who boast of their ability to “confront and correct ideas in the light of scholarly honesty”, and those who wrongly appoint themselves defenders of Islam — as though Islam needed their services! Wielding intimidation as a weapon, they do a disservice to the tolerance of Islam and go against its sound approach to debate through “example and kindly advice”.

It is in this very spirit of true Islam that I will engage with Mr Salim.

The first thing to point out is that he has failed to grasp what the author means by right and left. He confuses Islam as a noble faith with the political system of the Islamic world under the Umayyads and Abbasids. From there, he slips into the mistaken belief that criticism of the caliphate amounts to an attack on Islam, forgetting that the caliphs <!-- PDF page 163 | printed page 165 --> are human beings who can be right or wrong. Some were just and others unjust. History subjects all of them to criticism as political rulers, not as infallible religious leaders. As Dr Taha Hussein puts it, “The consequences of their policies were greater, wider in scope and more momentous than the individuals themselves.”

Consider what Ibn Abi al-Hadid wrote in his *Commentary on the Peak of Eloquence*:

“The Prophet's own companions used to criticise one another. Had they considered themselves above criticism, they would have known it from their own circumstances. The generation that followed them took the same approach towards the companions and spoke in these terms of those who did wrong among them.”

If we are entitled to criticise the policies of the revered Rightly Guided Caliphs, are we to place the Umayyad monarchy and the Persian-style rule of the Abbasids beyond criticism?

What do Yazid ibn Muawiya, with his sinful ways, al-Walid the drunkard, Abu al-Abbas the Blood-Shedder and al-Amin the libertine have in common with Abu Bakr, who was among the first to embrace Islam, Umar with his justice, or Ali with his deep understanding of the faith? The damage done by the policies of many Umayyad and Abbasid caliphs gives us every reason to place them on the right, and their opponents, who fought for the justice Islam calls for, on the left — not to call the latter a counter-revolution. None of this amounts to the slightest attack on Islam, as Mr Salim seems to imagine.

- From this misunderstanding, Mr Salim has drawn an even more mistaken conclusion. He interprets our defence of the left's right to act in secret as an attempt to “persuade modern Muslims that Islam is a revolution that depends on organised underground activity”. We said no such thing. All we said was that underground <!-- PDF page 164 | printed page 166 --> activity was a way of conducting a struggle against a formidable enemy. What has this to do with Islam itself?! This leads him into another fallacy: he denies that such a method can ever be legitimate, because, in his words, “the finest moments in the struggle of the prophets were those when they spoke out openly, with a clear and resounding call”. I would like Mr Salim to explain a fact familiar even to primary school pupils. For nearly three years, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) called people to Islam in secret, until his Lord commanded him: “Proclaim what you have been commanded to proclaim and turn away from those who worship other gods. We are enough to protect you against those who mock you.” Can Mr Salim dispute God's words: “Let believers not take unbelievers as allies in preference to believers. Whoever does so has nothing to do with God, unless you are taking precautions to protect yourselves against them”? Does this verse not justify *taqiyya* — concealing one's beliefs and acting in secret — when faced with a ruthless opponent?

- Mr Salim has muddled his way into inventing terms of his own and attaching them to the author, whom he accuses of *shu'ubiyya* and materialism. He says the author follows “the modern anti-Arab interpretation of Islam, and this dubious interpretation is the very same thing as the materialist interpretation of history”! From this, I am quite certain that he understands neither what *shu'ubiyya* means nor what the materialist interpretation of history is about.

*Shu'ubiyya* is built on ethnic loyalties, ancestry and descent. It takes regionalism and nationalism as its goals. Historical materialism, on the other hand, focuses on the way society is divided into classes and takes an all-embracing, worldwide view. As a historical term, *shu'ubiyya* describes the response of the *mawali* — Muslims of non-Arab origin — to Arab dominance in the Umayyad age. It had two wings. One was moderate and included enlightened people who <!-- PDF page 165 | printed page 167 --> called for equality among all peoples in keeping with the principles of Islam. The other was extremist and insisted on ethnic distinctions. How can we possibly reconcile *shu'ubiyya*, founded on ethnicity, with historical materialism, founded on class? Only in Mr Salim's imagination can the two be made to fit together.

In my approach to history, I do not look for a single explanation for everything. My analysis takes a broad view, considering political, economic and social factors and the way they interact and work together to bring about historical events. There is nothing necessarily dialectical-materialist about such a view. The historian al-Masudi took this approach in his *Meadows of Gold*, in which he described “the circumstances of nations and the regions of the world... their beliefs and customs, the countries, mountains and seas, the kingdoms and states, and the different groups of Arab and non-Arab peoples”. Ibn Khaldun himself also referred to this approach when he developed a philosophy of history, treating it as “the study of human civilisation”. Some European scholars of Islamic history have applied this method, and they were not necessarily Marxists. Readers need only look at the work of the Italian scholar Caetani, the French scholar Massignon and the English scholar Bernard Lewis to see the truth of what I say. Mr Salim's accusations of *shu'ubiyya* and materialism are nothing more than an unacceptable attempt to discredit the author. His attack rebounds on him. His own article betrays that very ethnic bias when he speaks of Arab rule on Arab soil in ages when Islam — not Arab identity — was the dominant influence.

- Mr Salim goes on with his false accusations. I ask him: was he being honest when he twisted the author's words and attributed statements to him that he never made? Here is an example. He says that I “with the brazen hostility of an anti-Arab partisan, accuse the community of the Prophet's city and the first home of Islam of having built its religious mission <!-- PDF page 166 | printed page 168 --> on the legacy of other civilisations, and claim that the Islamic empire owed its culture and civilisation to the Library of Alexandria, Christian monasteries and Jewish synagogues”. Readers can turn back to my exact words: “We do not deny that the Islamic left benefited from the heritage of Greek civilisation, Jewish traditions and the cultures of the East.” There is a world of difference between depending on something and benefiting from it. Elsewhere, we made the same point: “Islamic thought in general, and political thought in particular, could hardly close its eyes to the experiences of earlier societies, which already existed in the Islamic world.” Notice that we spoke of Islamic thought — philosophy and the reasoned study of religious belief — and not of the Islamic religious mission, as Mr Salim claims. He is trying to saddle us with a serious accusation by putting into our mouths the words of European scholars such as Becker, Goldziher and Grégoire, who attacked the Islamic faith with a deplorable degree of prejudice and regarded it as a distorted imitation of certain Eastern Christian sects. I would like to put an end to this attempt by informing him that we have written studies and research papers in which we discussed these views and refuted their proponents' claims with reason and evidence. Mr Salim is welcome to read them.

Mr Salim has misunderstood the term *dar al-Islam*, taking it to mean the Prophet's city. *Dar al-Islam* means the entire Islamic world, as distinct from *dar al-harb* (the “territory of war”), meaning non-Islamic countries. The Prophet's city (peace and blessings be upon him), on the other hand, was known as *dar al-hijra*, the “home of the migration”. Mr Salim should consult *The Rules of Government* to learn what these terms mean before he gets himself into trouble by hurling accusations without any basis.

<!-- PDF page 167 | printed page 169 -->

- What Mr Salim says about the Qarmatians is sheer nonsense. He claims that “the Qarmatian movement was one of the secret movements that sought to establish a settler presence in the Arab homeland... paving the way for its military conquest on behalf of a foreign power”.

I would like to correct some of his information. The Qarmatian leader was not called Hamdan ibn Qarmat, as he says, but Hamdan ibn al-Ash'ath. Qarmat was his nickname, not his father's name. Nor is the word derived from Aramaic in the sense of “deceiver”, as he claims on the authority of Father Anastas al-Karmali. An explanation based on the origin of a word carries little weight when set against explanations that arise from historical reality and fit the nature of the movement. Scholars of Islamic history follow the explanation offered by the Orientalist Ivanov, who says that *qarmat* means “peasant”, and that the people of southern Iraq still use it in that sense. This fits the nature of the movement, for at its heart it was a “peasant revolution”.

Mr Salim also attacks the Qarmatians, accusing them of “permitting immoral acts... and treating women as common property”. This is the same old charge, repeated over and over by the movement's enemies among the official historians. Bernard Lewis refuted it in his doctoral thesis on the origins of Ismailism, on the strength of the high standing women enjoyed in Qarmatian society. A contemporary Arab scholar, Muhammad Alyan, also refuted it in his master's thesis on *The Qarmatians of Iraq*. After critically examining the sources, he concluded: “We cannot support the claim that these practices were widespread. Had they been so, the ties of marriage would have weakened, but nothing of the kind is apparent in their lives. When Zakrawayh's daughter's brothers killed her husband, the stand she took against them showed just how strong the marital bond was... Moreover, the promiscuity <!-- PDF page 168 | printed page 170 --> in question would have weakened family lines among the Qarmatians, if not destroyed them completely. We have seen no evidence of this among the Qarmatians of Iraq or any other Ismaili groups.”

- Mr Salim's call for us to renew our culture from its original sources is a worthy one. But such renewal cannot be accomplished in a backward-looking spirit, with a backward-looking mind. What we desperately need is a fresh reading of the old texts. Dr Hussein Mu'nis called for this when he urged us to rewrite Islamic history. Abd al-Rahman al-Sharqawi also pointed to it in his article “Blood and Truth in Islam”, in issue 2313 of *Rose al-Yusuf*.

- Finally, I regret to say that I cannot grant Ahmad Musa Salim's request that I reconsider the ideas put forward in my article. Instead, I advise him to spend many years reading — may God grant him a long life — and then read the article again. Perhaps God will help him understand it then.

---

Ahmad Musa Salim responded to the preceding reply with an article entitled:

#### The Qarmatians Were Neither Muslims nor Socialists

Here is what he wrote:

It was to Dr Mahmoud Ismail's credit — despite some of his words <!-- PDF page 169 | printed page 171 --> of abuse — that, in his reply to my article, he backed away from some of the cornerstones and sacred truths of his thinking that he had championed so fervently in his earlier articles. He must have felt the weight of the connection between his promotion of the Qarmatian experiment in our own age and the charge that he subscribes to *shu'ubiyya*. Nor could he find a way around my assertion that the method he claims as his own is that of the Marxist Orientalist Bandali Jawzi and his school of thought. So he resorted to evasion and denial. Just to save face, he asks me in his reply: “How can anyone reconcile *shu'ubiyya*, founded on ethnicity, with historical materialism, founded on class, within a single approach?” The simple answer, which he knows but will not admit, is that the Orientalists who follow historical materialism have adopted all the anti-Arab claims of the secret Ismaili movements. They have taken a particular interest in the Qarmatian organisation for obvious reasons, then recast all this ethnic thinking in a new form, founded — despite a few concessions — on historical materialism. There, then, is the explanation of what the doctor could not understand: how *shu'ubiyya* and historical materialism came together in a single approach which he, along with many others, adopted and passed off as one of the pinnacles of scholarship!

The doctor continues his cleverly disguised retreat. He denies that his way of interpreting Islamic history is based on historical materialism and tries to tell us that his all-embracing approach is the product of his own thinking. Then, looking for further support, he declares that it is simply a more developed version of Ibn Khaldun's philosophy of writing history! But his feet eventually carry him back to what he considers the sacred source. He tells us that it is the approach of many Orientalists, even if they are not necessarily Marxists!

The doctor's lengthy journey in reply to my criticism of his methods of thinking and their results was therefore both empty and tedious. Above all, it was empty of the answer <!-- PDF page 170 | printed page 172 --> I was looking for: how could a history teacher justify promoting the Qarmatians as revolutionaries and freedom fighters? How could he present them as a successful experiment in a social system inspired by Islamic justice, and as representatives of the progressive left of Islam and of the socialism of workers and peasants?

The doctor did not answer these basic questions. Nor did he respond to my assertion that his knowledge and information about the Qarmatians came from Bandali Jawzi's book, *With the Intellectual Movements in Islam*. His reply thus evaporates into thin air. He is still called upon by all Islamic thinkers to explain how he can interpret Islamic history on the basis of historical materialism, which casts aside religion and ignores it, looking only for economic factors as it understands the course of social development.

Yet Dr Mahmoud Ismail did not let his reply evaporate entirely. He found it useful to fill the empty space by accusing me of ignorance, falsehood and reactionary thinking — the same cheap weapons used by materialists without firm convictions who follow the opportunistic, mongrel left!

Turning his back on the answer required of him, the doctor proceeds to give me a private lesson: the word *qarmat* means “peasant”, not “deceiver”, because that is what the Orientalist Ivanov said. Hamdan the Qarmatian, the teacher of the Qarmatian movement, is therefore Hamdan the peasant. But the Arab priest and linguist Father Anastas al-Karmali is wrong when he says *qarmat* means “deceiver” or “wicked man”. Why? Because, in the doctor's eyes, Ivanov, who mangles Arabic and knows nothing of Aramaic, which is a dialect of Arabic, is the more reliable authority!

<!-- PDF page 171 | printed page 173 -->

In his kindness and naivety, the doctor even goes so far as to teach me, for which I am most grateful, what *dar al-Islam* means and how it differs from *dar al-harb*. These were Arabic Islamic terms used in the Umayyad and Abbasid ages. The Qarmatians and Ismailis destroyed and reversed their meaning, turning the territory of Islam into the territory of war and the territory of war into the territory of Islam!

The doctor forgets, or chooses to forget, that I spoke of the territory of Islam in reply to one of his dangerous ideas. He speaks of “the Qarmatians and others benefiting from Jewish, Greek and Eastern traditions”. He then generalises from this on the principle that opposing ideas can be brought together. He repeats the claims of hostile Orientalists that Islamic civilisation benefited from elements foreign to it in the Library of Alexandria, Christian monasteries and Jewish synagogues. Taken to its logical conclusion, the doctor's generalisation would apply to the first territory of Islam, the Prophet's city. It was surrounded by these cultures, in the monasteries and along the caravan routes; and all these ideas were also present within it, in the well-known Jewish fortresses!

The doctor has refused to answer, whether out of embarrassment or because he considers the question beneath him. It would therefore be useful, whether he likes it or not, to confront him with the ultimate source of his knowledge about the Qarmatians. Let me illustrate his all-embracing approach to interpreting Islamic history with some passages from the Marxist Orientalist Bandali Jawzi's book, *With the Intellectual Movements in Islam*. He may then learn that this school of thought is the original, and he is the copy; it is the method, and he merely passes it on!

Bandali Jawzi published this famous book in Jerusalem in 1928. <!-- PDF page 172 | printed page 174 --> This former professor at Russia's University of Baku founded a school of historical-materialist interpretation of Islamic history. It sets religion aside, concentrates on economic factors and delights in exaggerations that serve its purposes. This is the approach adopted by Marxist thinkers such as Yuri Ivanov, who applied the same method to the study of Zionism. He divorced it from its original religious influences and regarded it merely as “chauvinism given to war and hostile to communism”. In doing so, he concealed what I regard as its most dangerous driving forces, which have accompanied it for forty centuries. Among the disciples of this school was the Marxist Roger Garaudy. When he visited Egypt some years ago, he too preached the virtues of the Qarmatian experiment to Muslims. Some Islamic thinkers answered him at the time.

The opening of Bandali Jawzi's book is provocative, just like the words of Dr Mahmoud Ismail. He dedicates his book to “the rising generation of Arab youth, to those who have freed their minds from the influence of social, religious and national superstitions”!

After devoting his attention to the Ismailis, whom he describes as interpreting religion, its laws and its rites in such a way as to deny them, the author turns to the Qarmatians. He says proudly of them: “They are bone of the bone and flesh of the flesh of the Ismailis; they do not differ from them.” He describes them as the leaders of communism in their age and says that they tried to put their socialist programme into practice in 897 CE and failed!

Bandali's book paints fanciful pictures of the socialism of the Qarmatian bands. He talks of property held in common in their villages, and how all the earnings of <!-- PDF page 173 | printed page 175 --> the women and children went to their leader. Then he — the Marxist materialist — speaks admiringly of the levy paid by anyone wishing to join them, the price of what they called the meal of brotherly love. Hamdan the deceiver had made them believe that this was food from paradise itself, arriving straight from the “imam of the age” — the Qarmatians' human god!

Bandali then discusses the Qarmatians and religion, describing some of their atrocities. Although he accepts that these occurred, he tries to play them down. He writes: “Abu Tahir al-Jannabi, the leader of the Qarmatians of Bahrain, in fact took every opportunity that presented itself, year after year. He harassed pilgrims travelling to the two holy sanctuaries and tried to prevent them from making the pilgrimage and observing its rites, which he regarded as practices from the age of ignorance before Islam, akin to idol worship. He very nearly put an end to the pilgrimage and its rites and made Muslims forget the way to the sanctuary, with few exceptions. In these raids, he generally confined himself to plundering pilgrims and preventing the pilgrimage, until the year 312 AH (921 CE), when the pilgrims suffered the greatest calamity since Arabs and Muslims first began making the pilgrimage to the Kaaba — at the hands of the Qarmatians!”

These, then, are the Qarmatians as depicted by the Marxist Orientalist Bandali Jawzi in his analysis of the movement. He describes their departure from Islam but stoops to calling them socialists, although they were murderous tyrants and worshippers of human beings, mere tools in the hands of a secret government that, under the influence of non-Arab nationalisms and Jewish subversion, sought to demolish Arab rule in the land of Islam and tear Muslim beliefs apart!

<!-- PDF page 174 | printed page 176 -->

There you have Dr Mahmoud Ismail's all-embracing method in its true colours, straight from its sources!

Yet the leftist Dr Mahmoud Ismail never forgets, in the world of his research, that the very fabric of his thinking belongs to the educated elite, the “intelligentsia”, with all its privileges. In his eyes, my criticism is an unforgivable sin. He takes criticism as an attack, refuses to benefit from anything I say, and wishes me a long life so that I can read enough to decipher the riddles of his infallible words!

In a spirit of tolerance and under the true guidance of Islam, however, I sincerely pray that God will strip Dr Mahmoud Ismail of this false mantle of infallibility as the great scholar of the age. Then he will become a human being like the rest of us, able to understand what other human beings write. He will look back at his articles on the Qarmatians and others, feel ashamed of what he has written, and cover it over with many a leaf of regret!

---

Our reply to this article was as follows:

#### Debate Through Intellectual Intimidation

I hesitated to reply to Ahmad Musa Salim's criticism the first time. I have now made up my mind not to respond again — not out of arrogance or vanity, as he claims, but because I am convinced that debate through reason and evidence is impossible with someone who fights with tongue and spear. He brandishes the weapons of intellectual intimidation and the tribunals of the Inquisition, wrapping them <!-- PDF page 175 | printed page 177 --> in appeals to chauvinistic nationalism and blind religious prejudice. In such circumstances, it is hardly surprising that the voice of reason and logic is drowned out, the freedom of scientific thought is violated, truth is turned into falsehood and an honest word becomes a whip that lashes the back of the person who uttered it.

How can we reach an understanding with someone who twists our words and reads things into them that never even occurred to the person who wrote them? What is the point of a debate in which every independent conclusion is attributed to some Marxist historian? What kind of logic gives one opinion more weight than another simply because its author is an Arab, while the other is an Orientalist who supposedly knows nothing of Aramaic, which is said to be a dialect of Arabic? It is certainly news to me that Aramaic is an Arabic dialect! Even more novel is our classification as materialists without firm convictions, followers of the opportunistic, mongrel left. These inventions in the world of scholarship and its vocabulary are nothing new for Mr Salim. He treated us to plenty of them in his previous article, when he confused the home of the Prophet's migration with the Islamic world, and when he married *shu'ubiyya* to historical materialism!

Mr Salim's latest article focuses on two main points. First, he insists that the author's approach is exactly the same as that of the Marxist Orientalist Bandali Jawzi and his school. Second, he claims that the Qarmatian movement was a counter-revolution, not an experiment in socialism.

Let us begin with the first point. Mr Salim seized on the fact that the author had not addressed this accusation in his reply. Drunk with the sense of victory, he decided the author was retreating and set out to corner him, bringing him face to face with Bandali Jawzi, the alleged source of his knowledge. Before discussing that claim, I would like readers to understand why <!-- PDF page 176 | printed page 178 --> I did not address this particular point in my previous reply. The critic sent his article to the magazine's editorial office in his own handwriting, and I received it so that I could respond. In it, Mr Salim had written the following words: “The author has summarised Bandali Jawzi's book, *The History of Secret Movements in Islam*. We note that this is the very title the author chose for his article.” I prepared a response to this claim at the time. It is still there in the handwritten copy of my reply held at the magazine's editorial office. But Mr Salim contacted the magazine, asking for the book's title to be corrected and the preceding statement removed. I therefore had to remove the paragraph of my reply that dealt with his accusation.

I return, then, to Mr Salim's false allegation that the method the author claims as his own is that of the Marxist Orientalist Bandali Jawzi and his school. As readers know, the critic's first article was a response to our first article on the Kharijites, whose introduction referred to the Abbasid, Ismaili and Qarmatian movements. I declare that the information about the Kharijites was entirely new. It had appeared neither in Jawzi's work nor in anyone else's. A university panel made up of leading professors of Islamic history, who examined the author's doctoral thesis on the Kharijites, can testify to this. The originality of the analysis and interpretation came, naturally enough, from consulting manuscripts and texts, as well as certain sources and works that no one had been able to draw on before.

Nor do I claim to have invented a method of my own. The scientific method is nobody's private property. Jawzi or anyone else cannot lay exclusive claim to a particular method of <!-- PDF page 177 | printed page 179 --> research, and it makes no sense to say that one person has simply borrowed another's method. Those who study the human sciences are not faced with a choice between competing methods, for there are not several different methods. The scientific method is one and indivisible. A researcher in the natural or mathematical sciences arrives at sound results in the study of a phenomenon through observation, experiment and verification. A researcher in the human sciences follows much the same approach, allowing for differences in the subject: gathering information, sorting and comparing it, and then interpreting it and drawing broader conclusions.

If more than one researcher in the natural sciences can arrive at the same results by following the scientific method, the same applies to researchers in the human sciences. To be more specific: if Bandali Jawzi and other Marxist historians arrived at certain conclusions in their study of Islamic history by applying the scientific method, other scholars, whether Arab or Orientalist, may reach exactly the same conclusions without having to be Marxists themselves. Otherwise, how can we explain the similarities in the findings of Jawzi, Barthold, Massignon, Caetani, Bernard Lewis, Taha Hussein, Ahmad Amin, Abd al-Aziz al-Duri and others who have studied Islamic thought? I doubt that they are all Marxists. But they have all applied a single method — the scientific method — and it is the method followed by Bandali Jawzi and Mahmoud Ismail alike.

Let me also reassure the critic. Although following the scientific method may have led the author to the same conclusions as Jawzi on the Qarmatians, he was one of the first to respond to Jawzi's Marxist disciple Roger Garaudy, who visited Egypt some years ago and also preached the virtues of the Qarmatian experiment to Muslims. My response appeared at the time in the magazine *Contemporary <!-- PDF page 178 | printed page 180 --> Thought*, under the title “Garaudy, Islam and Socialism”. The author cares no less deeply about Islam than his critic does. Agreement on the scientific method does not necessarily entail agreement in political or philosophical beliefs.

On the second point, I insist on the originality of my views on the Qarmatians, both in explaining their name and in assessing their movement as a socialist revolution whose understanding of justice accorded with that of Islam. It was not an ethnic movement or a counter-revolution, as Mr Salim imagines.

This time I will draw my arguments from the writings of Arab historians, past and present, lest I be accused of heresy and abandoning the faith. The truth is that historians have differed over the origin of the word “Qarmatians”. Al-Nuwayri says that their leader, Hamdan ibn al-Ash'ath, was nicknamed Qarmat because of his short stature and short legs. Al-Tabari says that it was because his eyes were red, since *qarmat* was a shortened form of the Nabataean word *karmita*, meaning redness of the eyes. I only hope Mr Salim will not accuse al-Tabari of being a “red” in the Marxist sense!

Father Anastas al-Karmali's explanation that the word means “fraudster” or “deceiver”, on the grounds that it comes from the Aramaic word *qarmatuna*, does not go far enough. Even if it is correct, it could have been an invention of the movement's enemies in Damascus during the Qarmatian campaigns in Syria. I have favoured Ivanov's explanation that the word means “peasant”, because it fits the nature of the movement. I find further support for this in a passage al-Maqrizi quotes in his book *Lessons for the True Believers*. He says: “When al-Husayn al-Ahwazi went to Iraq to spread the movement's teachings, he met Hamdan ibn al-Ash'ath in the countryside around Kufa. Hamdan had an ox with him, on which he carried loads. The two walked together for a while...” That plainly tells us something about the nature of the movement: it was a “peasant revolution” with a socialist character.

<!-- PDF page 179 | printed page 181 -->

I will confine myself here to what the late Ahmad Amin wrote in *The Zenith of Islam*:

“The Qarmatians were a branch of the Ismailis. Their movement was initially centred on the city of Wasit, between Kufa and Basra, and its surroundings. The region was home to a mixture of Arabs, Nabataeans and Black Africans [which disproves the claim that the movement was based on ethnic loyalties]. Most of them were poor and deeply unhappy with their government and with the owners of the land they worked. Hamdan imposed taxes on his followers, using the revenue to support the poor and fund communal institutions. It is said that he collected a great deal of money from his followers and distributed it among needy Qarmatians, until not one poor person remained among them. They may therefore be counted among the first socialist societies. Their teachers called for brotherhood among people, whatever their religions, social classes or ethnic backgrounds.”

In fairness, I should acknowledge that some aspects of the beliefs of the left-wing groups we have been studying went to extremes, and that violence marked some of their wars. Their doctrinal extremes stemmed from the old ideas inherited by peoples who had entered the fold of Islam. Their violence, on the other hand, was a natural response to the demands of the conflict. The right was no less violent in hunting them down and seeking to wipe them out. Besides, no revolutionary movement in history has been entirely free of flaws caused by infiltrators, outsiders and opportunists who work to bring it into disrepute. But none of this can justify an attack on the essential nature of these movements as social revolutions with noble aims inspired by the Islamic ideal of justice.

Finally, I cannot commend Ahmad Musa Salim for his most uncommendable claim that Dr Mahmoud has backed down.

<!-- PDF page 180 | printed page 182 -->

---

Abd al-Mughni Sa'id joined the discussion as a third voice with an article entitled:

#### A Modern Approach Must Not Come at the Expense of Authenticity

He writes:

I have followed with great interest the series of articles Dr Mahmoud Ismail began publishing on secret political movements in Islam, as well as Ahmad Musa Salim's response. Both colleagues have given me reason to put pen to paper. I believe the subject is important enough to warrant a third voice — someone to bring its wider implications into focus and point out the pitfalls along the way.

No one would deny the value of the work some of our historians are doing to rewrite particular periods of Islamic history, using modern methods and a modern style of writing. Naturally, when they examine historical events, they look at the broad changes in the economic and social life of Muslim communities as they actually lived at the time. Those communities may have remained faithful to all the principles of Islam, or departed from some of them under pressures from within or through contact with neighbouring societies.

Several of our historians have taken this approach. Taha Hussein's *The Great Civil Strife* and Abd al-Rahman al-Sharqawi's *Muhammad, the Messenger of Freedom* are examples. Al-Sharqawi also followed it to a large extent in *The Struggle of the <!-- PDF page 181 | printed page 183 --> Messenger*. I do not think my colleague Ahmad Musa Salim objects to this approach in principle. But his reservations undoubtedly carry weight, especially when it comes to viewing historical events in purely material terms and attributing their course and development entirely to material causes or motives. We cannot accept that as the basis for explaining the history of Muslim societies. The Islamic approach to history does not give spiritual matters priority over material ones, or material matters priority over spiritual ones. It keeps the two in proper balance.

In *Islam in Modern History*, Wilfred Cantwell Smith does justice to this question in an illuminating comparison between Islamic and Marxist approaches to history. Both Islam and Marxism, he says, attach great importance to the way history unfolds. Neither sees historical events as random occurrences. Both believe that these events follow a certain course, that the course has meaning, and that we can draw from it certain patterns — what the Quran calls God's established ways, or what Marxism calls the laws of history. But here is the difference: Marxism sees only one meaning in a historical event, a purely material one. Islam sees two: one material, belonging to this world, and one spiritual, belonging to the world to come.

If we return to the Quran, we find that its accounts of the missions of the prophets and messengers leave aside the small details and personal circumstances to focus on the overall direction of events and the lesson to be learnt. The missions of the messengers, as the Quran describes them, were far from simple or easy. They all met with ridicule and rejection. Certain forces therefore set themselves against the messengers <!-- PDF page 182 | printed page 184 --> and the justice and freedom they sought to bring about. The messengers had to press on with their cause and continue their struggle in the face of these opposing forces. The Quran describes them as follows:

**First.** Kings and tyrants, as we see in its references to Pharaoh, Qarun and Haman, among others.

**Second.** The rich and those who live in luxury, as the Quran tells us:

“Whenever We sent a warner to a town, its wealthy people said, ‘We reject the message you have brought.’ They said, ‘We have more wealth and children, and we shall not be punished.’ Say, ‘My Lord gives generously to whom He wills and restricts His gifts to whom He wills, but most people do not know.’”

**Third.** The reactionaries, whom the Quran describes as those who cling to the ways of their fathers and grandfathers. “In the same way, whenever We sent a warner before you to a town, its wealthy people said, ‘We found our fathers following a way of life, and we are following in their footsteps.’”

**Fourth.** The followers of the powerful, and the downtrodden whose interests would actually be served by supporting the cause. Instead, they stand against it, siding with the masters and leading figures they serve and from whom they seek honour. How vividly the Quran portrays the position of these oppressed people when they and their masters face God on the Day of Judgement and try to escape responsibility for their submission and their crimes! “They will say, ‘Our Lord, we obeyed our masters and our leaders, and they led us astray.’” But no excuse will be accepted for putting up with humiliation and helping tyrants in their oppression. Instead, they will be asked on the Day of Judgement, “Was God's earth not wide enough for you to move elsewhere?”

**Fifth.** Those who fritter away their time, amuse themselves and follow their whims; those who “buy <!-- PDF page 183 | printed page 185 --> idle talk” and “argue about God without knowledge, guidance or an enlightening scripture”. In today's language, this applies to people who make a hobby of debate and intellectual quarrels but have no intention of contributing any serious work.

As for the groups that supported and stood by the messengers, the Quran describes them as follows:

**First.** The vast majority of the people who were powerless, humiliated and exploited. The enemies of the messengers called them the lowest of the low, the rabble and the slaves, as we see in the struggles of Noah, Muhammad and Jesus.

**Second.** A select group of free citizens who stood for progress. In modern language, they were rather like the leading ranks of a revolutionary movement. Think of the disciples in the story of Jesus and the Companions in the struggle of Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him.

This careful analysis of the social classes that supported the messengers and those that opposed them brings out the human reality of class conflict. It arises from clashes of interest, but it does not operate as a law that inevitably demands bloodshed and violence in every case.

These missions, with the struggles that accompanied them and ran through them, always came to an inevitable conclusion. Either the cause triumphed or it could no longer hold out, and God poured out His wrath upon the wrongdoers through disasters of nature — storms, hurricanes, earthquakes, floods and the like. God, having left human beings free to choose, did not wish to make all people a single community. He would reward or punish them according to their deeds. The Quran calls this inevitable outcome a *sunna*, <!-- PDF page 184 | printed page 186 --> an unchanging law: “Such was God's way with those who passed away before, and God's command is a decree ordained.” It makes the point repeatedly in other verses: “You will find no change in God's way.”

All this makes it quite clear that if we want to establish the roots of our socialist thought, our first recourse must be the Quran and the teachings of the Prophet. When we turn to them, we find that Islam brought forth a theory of socialism long before Marxism set about formulating such a theory in modern economics.

At its heart, the Islamic theory guarantees everyone a decent and dignified share in life. In the language of modern economics, it guarantees an adequate standard of living based on equal opportunities and a shared enjoyment of nature's bounty and the fruits of science and civilisation. Everyone is free to earn more than this minimum through their own hard work, without exploiting the labour of others. Naturally, this means that people will have different levels of income. But these differences are not left to grow without limit. There is a ceiling, a safeguard against their hardening into distinct social classes. Islam therefore imposes a maximum income to stop wealth accumulating in the hands of a few to the point where a class system threatens to emerge.

In short, the aim of Islamic socialism is to build a classless society in which work is the foundation of a citizen's social worth and the measure of it. People earn different amounts according to their work, within a range between a minimum and a maximum standard of living. As development projects increase production and national income, we can narrow that range. In other words, greater or sufficient production leads to a fairer distribution of wealth. This is why we have our own name for a socialist society: a society of sufficiency and justice. Only a ceiling on income can guarantee that we achieve this <!-- PDF page 185 | printed page 187 --> goal, because it is the practical means of ensuring that greater production goes hand in hand with fairer distribution.

If Islam does not specify what that ceiling should be, it is because it is relative: it varies from place to place and from age to age. The ruler must therefore set it in keeping with the circumstances of society and the changes of the times. This is not a matter of unrestricted personal judgement, however. Four principles laid down in the Quran govern the decision:

**First.** Wealth must not be allowed to accumulate in the hands of a few rich people to the point where they can use it to dominate economic and social life and oppress others. “So that wealth does not circulate only among the rich among you.”

**Second.** Nor should wealth accumulate in their hands to the point where they plunge into luxury and ever more extravagant displays of status. Society soon begins to disintegrate, corruption spreads, and eventually it collapses altogether. That is the lesson of the fall of the Roman Empire and the collapse of Arab rule in Andalusia, among others. The Quran draws attention to this lesson: “When We wish to destroy a town, We give commands to those who live in luxury there, but they defy them. The sentence against it then becomes due, and We destroy it utterly.”

**Third.** Suppose that wealth accumulates in the hands of certain individuals who are neither given to luxury nor concerned with status, and have no wish to dominate or control others. Even then, amassing money for its own sake — hoarding it — runs counter to the interests of the community, because it holds money back from the flow of economic activity. This is the wisdom behind Islam's prohibition of hoarding: “Those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend them in the way of <!-- PDF page 186 | printed page 188 --> God, give them news of a painful punishment.”

**Fourth.** Individuals must not accumulate wealth at the expense of other people's rights. Workers must not be cheated out of their wages, nor consumers forced to pay exorbitant prices by those who have a monopoly on the market. The Quran states this principle clearly: “Do not deprive people of what is rightfully theirs, and do not spread corruption throughout the land.”

These four principles, which guide rulers in setting an upper limit on income in any society and in any age, act naturally as a safeguard against the rise of an exploiting, dominant class. In such a classless society, people would be, as the Prophet said, “equal as the teeth of a comb”, even though their incomes varied.

Islam, then, provided the foundations of a scientific theory of socialism. If we are serious about developing socialist thought rooted in our own heritage, we ought to devote more attention to studying these foundations than to studying the life of Abu Dharr al-Ghifari or uprisings such as those of the Zanj and the Qarmatians in southern Iraq and the Gulf. As I explained in the second part of my book *The Development of Socialist Thought*, these uprisings or revolutions were not founded on a body of ideas that could be studied as part of the history of socialist thought. For the most part, they were simply spontaneous revolts against exploitation and tyranny. If they show us anything, it is that the seeds of an awakening lay within a sick society. Unfortunately, those seeds produced only confused efforts that failed to bring back the Islamic socialist system as it had been practised under the Prophet [peace and blessings be <!-- PDF page 187 | printed page 189 --> upon him] and his two great successors, Abu Bakr and Umar. It is even possible that some of these stirrings of an awakening made the illness worse rather than curing it. We see this in the Qarmatian revolt, with its elements that contradicted and departed from Islam.

During that golden age of the Prophet, Islamic socialism was practised through direct democracy. Rulers had neither the trappings of power nor the privileges of an official elite. The Prophet and his two successors ran the affairs of the state from the mosque in Medina, without establishing palaces of government or official institutions inclined towards control and privilege.

But Islam soon suffered a serious setback that gradually took it away from its socialist character. The system of *bay'a* — pledging allegiance to a ruler through popular consultation and election — gave way to hereditary rule. Muslim rulers and their supporters became ruling families. Throughout the Middle Ages, they formed a privileged class that lived in luxury, surrounded itself with displays of wealth, status and distinction, and cut itself off from the mass of the people. How different this was from the simple life the Prophet and the Rightly Guided Caliphs had lived among ordinary people!

Some writers take the cause championed by Abu Dharr al-Ghifari as a living example of the socialist movement. In reality, it was above all a sincere stand against this dangerous change when its first signs began to appear under Uthman. It was the first attempt to warn of the impending setback and prevent it. But the forces behind that setback had already begun to emerge and spread, as the Islamic state expanded, wealth poured in from conquest, and contact with the Romans and the West brought the influence of their systems of government. Umar ibn al-Khattab had the insight to recognise the danger of allowing these forces to gather and grow. He firmly restrained <!-- PDF page 188 | printed page 190 --> the desire for special privileges among the governors of the provinces and their sons. He also forbade the building of grand seats of government like those of the Romans and Persians, and even demolished some of them to ward off the causes of discord.

The forces behind this setback grew stronger under both the Umayyads and the Abbasids. It was only natural that popular uprisings should occur under both dynasties. But in studying these uprisings we must make a distinction. Were they impulsive reactions, marked above all by the immaturity of their revolutionary spirit? Or were they serious and sincere efforts to revive the Islamic system as practised under the Prophet and the Rightly Guided Caliphs?

Personally, I have no objection whatsoever to our modern historians studying and analysing these uprisings. I sincerely hope, however, that they will remain objective in what they write and avoid reading more into events than the evidence can bear. What matters to me is that we bring out the originality of our own thought. We should not simply label certain popular uprisings in Islamic history as socialist movements ahead of their time, and then find ourselves analysing and judging them in the language of our own age. I am not singling out any particular book or historian. I am warning against a method of research that carries within it more chances of getting things wrong than of getting them right. May God guide us to success.

---

We responded to Abd al-Mughni Sa'id's views in an article entitled:

<!-- PDF page 189 | printed page 191 -->

#### A Modern Approach Strengthens Authenticity Rather Than Undermining It

Here is the text:

Abd al-Mughni Sa'id has spared us the need to defend the modern historian's right to look at Islamic history through the lens of society. We are delighted that he has joined the discussion as a third voice, in a spirit of fairness and objectivity.

I have known his generosity of spirit and breadth of mind ever since I was a university student regularly attending the Naji Seminar, which he ran in the early sixties. I hope, then, that he will allow me to disagree with him on a central point in his article. He argues that the Quran and the teachings of the Prophet contain a “scientific theory of socialism”, and that Islam offers an approach to historical research which “sees two meanings in historical events, one material and worldly, the other spiritual and relating to the life to come”.

Let me make clear from the outset that our disagreement is not fundamental. I am not denying Islam's socialist character, nor am I belittling the value of its view of history. But I believe that neither the socialist thought in Islam nor the historical accounts in the Quran come to us in the form of a theory of socialism or a method of historical research. What the Quran and the teachings of the Prophet give us in this regard are general principles. To put it simply, Islam contains the spirit of the theory, not the theory itself; the essence of the method, not the method itself.

This in no way detracts from the authenticity of the Quran. On the contrary, it confirms it. Indeed, it is one of the signs of its miraculous nature, alongside others. The Quran gives humanity general principles aimed at the good of humankind in every aspect of political <!-- PDF page 190 | printed page 192 -->, economic and intellectual life. The word of God is far above being a collection of specific philosophical theories. It is broader and higher than that. In the language of philosophy, we might say that the supreme active intellect laid down the general principles and left it to the receptive intellect — the human mind, the greatest thing God created in His greatest creation, the human being — to formulate theories and devise methods.

The human mind can get things right, and it can get them wrong. No theory explaining the phenomena of the universe is guaranteed to last for ever. What reason establishes, reason can also overturn. The survival of a theory depends on how well it grasps the laws of change. As circumstances change, theories are revised or fade away, making room for others that better fit the new reality. General principles, on the other hand, remain. They are broad and rich enough to embrace theories and methods without being confined by them.

Few Islamic thinkers have grasped this miraculous aspect of the Quran. Most have either clung to the literal wording of the texts, bringing Islamic thought to a standstill, or taken interpretation to such extremes that they lost their bearings. Radically conservative thinkers and extreme liberals arrived at the same result: both overlooked, or chose to overlook, the true relationship between the divine intellect and the human mind.

The Mu'tazilites tried to deepen their understanding of that relationship by freeing the human mind from its chains. But traditionalism was the dominant current. They exhausted their energies in breaking down reactionary rules of thought and restraining the excesses of the liberal current. In the ensuing struggle, traditionalism prevailed, represented by the Ash'arites and the Hanbalites and reaching its height with al-Ghazali. The voice of Ibn Rushd — the voice of reason — was drowned out in the clamour of traditionalism, which grew stronger in the hands of Sufis such as Ibn Arabi and Ibn Sab'in. Sufism turned into a form of unthinking dervish devotion <!-- PDF page 191 | printed page 193 --> that remained the cornerstone of Islamic thought until the dawn of the modern age. Here lie the real causes of the setback that befell the Islamic world. And here, too, lie the reasons for the European Renaissance, which picked up the thread Ibn Rushd had left behind.

The Quran, then, can inspire modern theories and methods. If dialectics — the study of change and the forces at work within it — is one of the most significant advances modern science has made in explaining the phenomena of the universe and discovering the laws of its movement, I believe that a Muslim philosopher such as Ibn Khaldun recognised its importance long before Hegel astonished the world with his discovery. Consider what Ibn Khaldun wrote in his *Muqaddima*:

“One of the subtle mistakes historians make is to forget that the circumstances of nations and generations change as the ages change and the days pass. When people acquire royal power and take control of a state, they inevitably fall back on the customs of their predecessors and adopt many of them, without forgetting the customs of their own generation. The customs of the state thus differ somewhat from those of the first generation. When another state succeeds them, it blends their customs with its own and introduces further changes, departing still more from the first state's ways. This gradual process of change continues until there is a complete difference between the two.”

Does that passage not sum up the movement of history and uncover its laws before Hegel discovered them and Marx built the foundations of dialectical materialism upon them? This is why I say that a modern approach strengthens our heritage rather than diminishing it.

Is democracy, the very heart of modern political theory, not what the Quran called for in the principle — not the theory — of *shura*, or consultation? If the Quran and the sayings of the Prophet contained a theory of government, why did the Prophet not recommend it <!-- PDF page 192 | printed page 194 --> (peace and blessings be upon him) before his death? The greatness of Islam, then, lies in the fact that it did not hand down theories or devise fixed methods. Instead, it established broad principles and values within which Muslims could shape their systems of government and politics to meet the needs of their own times.

On economic matters, I agree with all Abd al-Mughni Sa'id's thinking about socialism in Islam. Our only disagreement is over his claim that there is an “Islamic scientific theory of socialism”. I too believe that the Quran contains noble principles of social justice, and that from them we can develop a theory not very different from modern scientific socialism. Islam and scientific socialism start from the same point in identifying what stands in the way of justice. The cause lies in human nature, in our love of power and our craving to dominate. God says: “When harm touches him, he is distraught, and when good comes his way, he is withholding.” And He says: “The soul is ever inclined towards evil.”

Give free rein to the urge to possess and dominate, and the result is social injustice. A minority exploits the resources of the whole community. And this injustice is of human making: God does not wrong people in the least; it is people who wrong themselves.

The oppressive minority defies what is right: “When We wish to destroy a town, We give commands to those who live in luxury there, but they defy them.” Conflict between the wealthy minority and the masses of the oppressed is inevitable. Islam urges the oppressed to rise up. The Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said: “Whoever among you sees a wrong, let him change it...” And God knows that wrongdoers are not deterred by kindness: “We destroyed generations before you when they committed injustice. Their messengers brought them clear proofs, but they would not believe.” It takes force and severity to deter them, and the outcome necessarily favours <!-- PDF page 193 | printed page 195 --> the oppressed: “We wished to show favour to those who were oppressed in the land, to make them leaders and to make them the inheritors...” These clear verses and others testify to the socialist character of Islam. Inspired by them, lawmakers can develop economic theories suited to the age and keeping pace with the course of development. The overall spirit of Islam seeks social justice. In achieving it lie the welfare of humanity and the flourishing of civilisation. Ibn Khaldun says:

“Since injustice threatens the very survival of humanity through the destruction of civilisation that it brings about, the wisdom of prohibiting it is clear. Its prohibition is therefore important, and the evidence for it in the Quran and the teachings of the Prophet is too abundant to be listed and counted exhaustively.”

Once again, Ibn Khaldun puts his finger on one of the foundations of scientific socialism. He grasped the essence of what is known as “surplus value”. Here are his words:

“Among the worst forms of injustice, and the most destructive to civilisation, are the imposition of forced labour and the wrongful exploitation of the people. Labour is a form of wealth, for a person's livelihood and earnings are the value of the work that people perform in society. All their efforts and labour are thus wealth and earnings for them; indeed, they have no other earnings. The people who work to build up civilisation derive their living and their income from that very work. If they are forced to labour at tasks that are not their own, and made to serve others without pay in the work by which they earn their living, their earnings vanish and the value of their labour — their wealth — is taken from them. They suffer harm and lose a large share of their livelihood, or indeed their livelihood altogether. If this happens repeatedly, their hopes of improving civilisation are ruined. They cease to strive for it altogether, and civilisation falls into decline and ruin.”

My last point of disagreement with Abd al-Mughni Sa'id concerns history in <!-- PDF page 194 | printed page 196 --> the Quran. I will not go into Smith's comparison between the Islamic approach, with its material and spiritual aspects, and historical materialism, with its materialist outlook. Historical materialism has already been studied exhaustively. I refer the reader to Plekhanov's *The Development of the Monist View of History*. I will simply shed some light on the Quran's understanding of history and on the extent to which Muslim historians applied it in their own historical works.

There is no dispute that history, as presented in the Quran, is meant to teach and guide us. This is clear from its many stories of the prophets and messengers, and from its accounts of vanished societies such as Ad and Thamud — what are known as the “tales of the ancients”. The Quran recounts all this for a moral purpose, in keeping with the reasons for its revelation. God says: “Travel through the land and see what became of the wrongdoers.”

The Quran's stories bring out God's power to create, His greatness in governing the universe and His knowledge of what was and what will be. But they also show us that human beings are free in their actions and have control over their own will. That is why people are responsible for their deeds: the reward or punishment is of the same nature as the deed itself. Human beings, as individuals and as communities, are therefore the makers of history. There is no contradiction here between the power of the Creator and people's creation of their own actions, or their complete freedom to shape the reality in which they live. Our deeds in this life are the gateway to the next: “Whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it.”

The point is that history is made by human beings, and their deeds are the historian's material. Anything beyond that lies outside the scope of historical research. Spiritual matters cannot be investigated by reason: “Say: the spirit is a matter for my Lord, and you have been given only a little knowledge.” The believer <!-- PDF page 195 | printed page 197 --> accepts these matters as matters of faith: “Those who believe in the unseen, perform the prayer, and give from what We have provided for them.” To delve into them is futile and dangerous. Ibn Khaldun warned against this when he said: “In the study of divine matters, there is no room for broadening speculation or multiplying questions.”

The Quran therefore urges people to exercise reason and unleash its powers in studying the phenomena of the universe that lie within its reach. We can discover their causes and the factors that bring them about without calling their first cause into question in the slightest. On the contrary, searching for and examining the causes of things deepens and strengthens faith. God says: “It is those of His servants who have knowledge who truly stand in awe of God.”

From the historical stories of the Quran we can therefore draw scientific guidance for historical research. Its basis is to set aside matters of the unseen and use reason to study what we can understand: to discover the nature of things, their causes and the laws governing their development.
