We can go further still. The lessons contained in the Quran's stories show us that it is possible to look ahead to the future — a distinctive feature of modern science. By examining the “tales of the ancients”, we can discern a law of inevitability in the common fate of states founded on injustice and tyranny.

Yet the fact remains that the Islamic school of history before Ibn Khaldun did not follow this scientific approach. Historians understood the historical stories in the Quran as history itself. They did not dare explain events and occurrences, because they saw them as a fate already written. They were content to recount events and reports passed down from one generation to the next, on the assumption that history was a branch of knowledge received through tradition. Few applied reason to what had been handed down to them. Even then, they did so in a limited way, confining themselves to examining the accounts of those from whom they drew their information <!-- PDF page 196 | printed page 198 --> on the basis of how trustworthy the people reporting them were. They borrowed this practice from the scholars of the Prophet's sayings. It is well known that the beginnings of Islamic historical scholarship were bound up with the study of those sayings. It is a mistake to regard people who worked on history in those days as “historians”; a more accurate name for them is chroniclers. Scholars in other fields looked down upon them and treated their subject with contempt, considering it little more than “the telling of stories and the recounting of reports, the limits of its knowledge being anecdotes and evening tales”. Ibn Khaldun was right to say that the great Muslim historians had gathered the accounts of past times, only for interlopers to mix them with falsehoods they imagined and invented, embellishing them with unreliable stories they fabricated. They failed to observe or take account of the causes of events and circumstances. Careful investigation was rare; error and illusion were the close companions of these accounts. In fairness, we should acknowledge the efforts of some of the early historians, such as al-Tabari, al-Masudi and Miskawayh. But, as Ibn Khaldun says, they were “so few that one could almost count them on one's fingers”.

It was Ibn Khaldun who transformed the methods of historical research in Islam. He was unquestionably the thinker who gave the field its theoretical foundations. One scholar described him as “the world's first philosopher of history”, and that is no exaggeration. Ibn Khaldun's thinking did not spring out of nowhere. I am convinced that he drew it from the Quran, which honoured reason and freed it from its chains. Its verses on this subject are too numerous to count: “Will you not see?” “Will you not look?” “Will you not reason?” And so on. If the Mu'tazilites deserve the credit for bringing reason to the study of faith, Ibn Khaldun pioneered a reasoned understanding of history. For him, history meant “careful reflection, investigation and explanation of things and their origins, and a deep knowledge of how events occur and what causes them”. There is so much to say about Ibn Khaldun <!-- PDF page 197 | printed page 199 -->, and extensive studies have been devoted to him. It will suffice here to quote Alban Widgery: “Ibn Khaldun is the founder of the science of history because he saw it as a distinct branch of knowledge, concerned with the whole range of social phenomena that make up actual history, revealing the various influences at work within it and the continuing chains of causes and effects.” What Widgery calls the “continuing chains of causes and effects” in Ibn Khaldun's method means that our philosopher understood the nature of dialectics. That understanding is the cornerstone of historical materialism, which seeks to identify the laws of historical change. With its help, we can explain the events of the past, establish the facts of the present and anticipate the direction of the future.

In this regard Ibn Khaldun says: “I devised it as a remarkable approach among the various methods, an original way and a new style... so that you may understand the circumstances of the times and generations before you, and those that come after you.”

In short, the Quran contains general principles, not specific theories or methods cast in a rigid mould. Guided by those principles, lawmakers can develop coherent theories of politics and economics, and researchers can draw inspiration from them to develop a sound scientific approach to history.

If a historian and philosopher such as Ibn Khaldun was able to give history a clear form without in the least diminishing the exalted standing of the Quran, then modern historians ought to carry his work forward, drawing on the advances made in our own age. The miraculous nature of the Quran lies in the fact that it does not conflict with scientific progress, so long as its aim is human happiness and the good of humanity.

<!-- PDF page 198 | printed page 200 -->

---

Rashad Muhammad Khalil joined the discussion with the following article:

#### Arab Thought Under False Accusation

I followed with keen interest the debate between Dr Mahmoud Ismail and Ahmad Musa Salim on the subject of secret political movements in Islam. I was astonished that Dr Ismail should set aside the very principles of research in a study of such importance. He takes the claims of Bandali Jawzi and others like him as established truths, without troubling himself to undertake the careful investigation that intellectual honesty demands — especially when the subject concerns the very foundations of our historical and cultural heritage.

One would have expected the doctor to establish the facts from trustworthy sources before rushing to explain them or adopting other people's explanations. If reliable sources failed him, the rules of scientific research required him to suspend judgement and refrain from interpretation. Instead, we saw him discard all the Arabic sources, which, despite their differences, agree on a view contrary to his own, and then appeal to the authority of people such as Bernard Lewis, Émile Masqueray and Percy Smith. As if these gentlemen from abroad had devoted themselves with perfect integrity to correcting the lies that virtually all Muslim scholars and historians had conspired to tell! He did not stop for a moment to ask himself: what comes next, once we accuse our most distinguished Muslim scholars and historians, Shia and Sunni alike — al-Ghazali, al-Ash'ari, al-Shahrastani, Ibn Hazm, Ibn Taymiyya, al-Tabari, Ibn Kathir, Fakhr al-Din al-Razi and many others — of conspiring to make up the claim that <!-- PDF page 199 | printed page 201 --> the Ismailis (the Batinis, or followers of hidden meanings) and the Qarmatians stood outside Islam rather than constituting schools of thought within it, as Dr Ismail maintains, following the views of certain Orientalists? Their aim, according to those scholars, was to abolish Islamic law in its entirety and deny the existence of the Creator; they believed in no religion and acknowledged no resurrection.

If all these scholars and historians conspired to lie and mislead us, then surely we ought to reject everything that has reached us through them: the Quran, the teachings and sayings of the Prophet, and the rest of our history. If we cannot trust them to tell the truth about the Ismailis and Qarmatians, how can we be sure they did not also agree to lie about our entire heritage, which has reached us only through them? To whom, then, shall we turn for knowledge of our religion and history? To Lewis, Émile and Smith?

An Arab who was keen to demolish the role of the Arabs in history — Dr Abd al-Rahman Badawi — has already written a book called *The History of Atheism in Islam*! In it he collected studies by Orientalists interested in the history of heresy in Islam, which, in Dr Badawi's view, represents the free current of the Arab spirit. But I wonder: was the work of Orientalists such as Goldziher, Heinrich Seder and Paul Kraus motivated purely by the pursuit of knowledge? And was that the sole motivation of these scholars and others like Margoliouth, O'Leary and Gauthier in their efforts to prove that the Arabs, because of their supposedly backward intellectual nature, were incapable of producing a civilisation or thought of any value? That everything they possessed, even the Quran itself, was borrowed from Hellenic (Greek) thought through the Syrians, Jews and Christians?

<!-- PDF page 200 | printed page 202 -->

I do not mean by this to weigh up the merits and failings of Orientalist scholarship as a whole. A full assessment would reveal a valuable contribution, deserving our gratitude, from sincere scholars — and even some whose motives were less sincere — in uncovering the Arab-Islamic heritage and bringing its worth to light. We need to benefit from the positive side of Orientalist scholarship. But we must never take our religion from these scholars or come to understand our heritage through their minds! We are in desperate need of a fresh examination of our heritage, using Arab minds and a sound scientific method. Our heritage needs to be gathered together, organised and carefully checked. We need a way of establishing the reliability of historical accounts. We have before us the methods of the scholars of the Prophet's sayings, who assessed the trustworthiness of those who passed them down, and Ibn Khaldun's approach to history.

It is possible to correct our understanding of history and judge it in the light of the actual conditions of its time. But it will take painstaking work and a scientific approach whose requirements must be met in full. Only then will a picture of this history emerge that differs from those offered by traditional and modern scholars alike. Drawing on what I know of this history, I would like to outline a few ideas. I hope that *Rose al-Yusuf* will be open to a detailed discussion of them, thereby rendering a great service to the task of setting this history straight.

I believe such research will show that Arab society, both before and after Islam, was a classless society, unlike the structure of other societies. Nor did it know slavery in the form in which other societies knew it. We will see that the Arabs who went forth under the banner of Islam fought a sincere struggle — and I do not exclude the Umayyads or Abbasids — to free the societies whose states they overthrew from servitude to kings, priests and feudal landowners, and bring them into the service of the one God, under a system of justice and standards before whose revealed law everyone in society stood equal <!-- PDF page 201 | printed page 203 --> without discrimination. It will also become clear that the Arabs' exclusive hold on power was not an act of selfishness. It was a necessity dictated by what was right and by the common good, both because they were the guardians of this religion and because of the solidarity needed to uphold the authority of the state, establish justice and protect its borders, as Ibn Khaldun explains. Had the Arabs failed in this duty, those who resented the loss of their kingdoms would have risen against them — as they often tried to do. Islam would have become, if it were remembered at all, no more than a passing item in the historical record!

We will see that the bloody conflict among the Companions of the Messenger of God, which ended with the establishment of Umayyad rule, was not entirely what we imagine it to have been. It was not a struggle driven by aggression and injustice. Rather, it was a conflict between free, capable believers, each exercising their own judgement on a matter for which there was no explicit religious text: the form the state should take. In the end, the struggle was decided in favour of the person most capable of running the state, even if he was not the most distinguished in his early devotion to Islam, his struggle for it or his piety. Mu'awiya himself acknowledged this when he said: “People, I am not the best among you. There are people among you who are better than I am: Abd Allah ibn Umar, Abd Allah ibn Amr and other men of excellence. But perhaps I can be of the greatest benefit to you in governing, be the best at facing your enemy, and bring you the richest yield.”

Indeed, the study of the Arabic language and pre-Islamic poetry reveals a highly developed Arab civilisation before Islam, one that had arrived at the noblest and soundest understanding of God and of existence (the world), and at the most precise moral standard humanity had reached without divine revelation. In these fields, Arab thought was more advanced than Greek thought in its various schools, and than the schools influenced by it. The approach Arab thought had taken in building this civilisation was a sound scientific one, based on observing phenomena, classifying and organising them, and drawing conclusions. Islam's encounter with this thought was a struggle to correct and refine it, not to demolish and replace it. And Islam emerged <!-- PDF page 202 | printed page 204 --> from the Arabian Peninsula to wage a fierce struggle against pagan beliefs clothed in Hellenic philosophy, Indian myths and Persian heresies. Arab thought, strengthened by Islam, taught more than it learnt and fought to free humanity from empty argument, superstition and myth. It was a fierce struggle, with victories and losses. Arab thought built its second civilisation under Islam, and then its sun shone upon Europe, where it helped build a third civilisation through science, as the fair-minded German Orientalist “Siegfried” acknowledged after Europe had turned its back on it!

Finally, we will establish that the Ismaili and Qarmatian movements did not arise for the sake of justice, but to destroy Islam and Arab thought. And this brings us to a final question: was it merely a coincidence that the leader of the Ismaili movement was Maimun ibn Daysan ibn Sa'id the Jew, a descendant of al-Shal'la'? And that Jews are also the leaders of the modern Masonic movement? Was it a coincidence that both movements used secrecy, deception, fraud and degrees of initiation, killed elderly people, women, children and adults, and brought destruction? And that both movements had the same aim of destroying religions, especially Islam, and humiliating peoples, especially the Arabs?

The hatred of the Jews for Ishmael, who received his father's birthright instead of their forefather Isaac, and for the Arab descendants of Ishmael who inherited Abraham's blessing, has never subsided and will not subside until the descendants of Ishmael are humiliated and the kingdom of Israel is established on their land! Is it not time for us to wake up and take notice? I hope this will happen before it is too late.

---

Our reply to Rashad Muhammad Khalil appeared in an article entitled:

<!-- PDF page 203 | printed page 205 -->

#### Who Is Really Making False Accusations?

Here is the text:

In the issue before last of *Rose al-Yusuf*, Rashad Khalil presented us with an article entitled “Arab Thought Under False Accusation”. It raises a number of questions about the Islamic heritage. Some concern Muslim thinkers and Orientalist scholarship. Others deal with broad issues such as the place of Arab civilisation in history, or specific matters such as the origins of the Ismailis. Beyond this, the article contains its author's own views and ideas about Arab society before and after Islam, and his interpretation of the question of the caliphate and the struggle over it in the early days of Islam.

The finger of accusation, on the charge of slandering Arab thought, points at the author of “Secret Political Movements in Islam”. And that author accuses the self-appointed guardians of Arab thought of precisely the same thing. Arab-Islamic thought is neither anyone's monopoly nor an estate to be passed down from one generation to the next. The reason for its present backwardness lies with those who have cut it off from the course of human intellectual development and surrounded it with walls of priestly authority. There is no need to point to their connections with the forces of domination and colonialism; that is a fact recorded in our modern and contemporary history. It is enough to recall how habitually they have brandished the sword of their own interests in the name of religion against anyone who sought to free Arab thought from their guardianship — from al-Afghani and Muhammad Abduh to Khalid Muhammad Khalid and Abd al-Rahman al-Sharqawi. It is these people whom I accuse of falsehood.

<!-- PDF page 204 | printed page 206 -->

I must discuss Khalil's views on all the questions he has raised. Given their number and variety, I find myself obliged to refer the reader to certain works for further clarification.

The first point concerns Muslim thinkers and historians such as al-Ghazali, al-Ash'ari, al-Shahrastani, Ibn Hazm, Ibn Taymiyya, al-Tabari and Ibn Kathir, who denounced the Ismaili movement. To disagree with them on one particular subject is not to say that they conspired to lie and mislead us in everything they left behind. There were also Muslim thinkers who disagreed with them and refuted their claims about the Ismailis. Among them was Ibn Khaldun, whose method Khalil himself urged us to adopt.

The disagreements between historians of Islam arose from their religious affiliations, which in turn grew out of their social circumstances. All those who were biased against the Ismailis represented the traditionalist current in religious thought and, socially, belonged to the upper or at least the middle classes. Take al-Ash'ari, a descendant of Abu Musa al-Ash'ari, one of the governors of Iraq in the early days of Islam. He broke away from the Mu'tazilites, the pioneers of rational thought, and laid the foundations of a traditionalist religious current that was politically reactionary, serving the Abbasids. Al-Ghazali strengthened this current and grew wealthier through his association with the Seljuk minister Nizam al-Mulk. It is well known that Ibn Hazm, Ibn Taymiyya and al-Shahrastani, all Sunnis, regarded the Sunni school as “the group destined for salvation” and all other groups as “doomed”.\*

This traditionalist current gained ground until it overwhelmed the rational approach that had begun with the Mu'tazilites and ended with Ibn Rushd. Traditionalism reached its peak in the hands of Sufis such as Ibn Arabi and Ibn Sab'in. Little by little, the voice of reason faded, until it was drowned out by the clamour of unthinking dervish devotion, which weighed heavily on Islamic thought.

<!-- PDF page 205 | printed page 207 -->

As for al-Tabari and Ibn Kathir, they wrote their histories in the Abbasid age, when the descendants of Ali suffered even greater hardship and persecution than they had under the Umayyads. One of their poets went so far as to say:

> Would that the tyranny of the sons of Marwan might return to us!  
> Would that the justice of the Abbasids might burn in hell!

That is why historians did not dare do justice to the Ismaili movements that opposed the Abbasid caliphate.\*\*

Linked to this point is the accusation that we ignored the original sources and drew instead on Orientalists such as Jawzi, Smith, Masqueray and Lewis. A glance at what we wrote about Masqueray and Smith will show that we merely acknowledged their efforts to uncover works from our heritage dealing with the Kharijites. We did not borrow a single opinion from either of them. As for Jawzi, we dealt with the claim that we borrowed from him in a previous article. And no fair-minded researcher can call into question the objectivity of the modern historian Bernard Lewis, under whom some of our finest contemporary historians of Islam studied.

As for Seder and Kraus, we made no reference to either of them whatsoever, directly or indirectly. The same is true of Goldziher. Indeed, in a study of ours entitled *On Islamic Thought*, we disagreed with his views on the study of the Prophet's sayings and Gnosticism. In that same study we examined O'Leary's views on Islamic mysticism and rejected them, as we did Grégoire's views linking Islamic beliefs with Hellenism. No one denies the contribution of Orientalist scholarship to our heritage — something Khalil himself <!-- PDF page 206 | printed page 208 --> has acknowledged — so there is no need to dwell on it. But there is a question I would like to put to Khalil, the librarian of the world's largest Islamic university: what has he contributed to the study of our heritage in the way of bibliographical work? Perhaps he feels a little ashamed when he sees the monumental work produced by a colleague of his at the British Museum named Pearson, whose *Index Islamicus* brought together Islamic studies and periodical publications from scholarly journals around the world. Surely Khalil would be better serving Arab thought by doing work of this kind, rather than uttering resounding words and hollow speeches from the pulpit!

The next point concerns his hazy, utopian picture of Arab society: “Arab society before and after Islam was classless, unlike the structure of other societies.” In reality, pre-Islamic nomadic society saw class conflict over water and pasture, a phenomenon recorded in literary works in the accounts known as the “Days of the Arabs”. If the struggle took the outward form of tribal conflict, it was because the tribe was the backbone of the social system. This also explains the existence of tribal alliances: smaller tribes banded together to resist the domination of more powerful ones. In urban society, with its more developed political system, class conflict stood out in sharp relief. Consider a city like Mecca: at the top of its social pyramid stood the wealthy merchant class, and at the bottom were enslaved workers of Persian, Byzantine, Ethiopian and other origins. What a gulf separated the position of a man like Abu Sufyan from that of a man like Bilal, who called the faithful to prayer for the Prophet!\*

When Islam appeared, the oppressed classes of slaves and *mawali* formed its ranks <!-- PDF page 207 | printed page 209 --> against the wealthy merchants, who saw Islam as a threat to their interests. The triumph of Islam struck a blow at a class system founded on wealth and ancestry. Piety became the measure of distinction between one Muslim and another. Muslim society enjoyed social justice in the time of the Prophet and his first two successors. Under Uthman, a new aristocracy of leading Companions, such as Talha, al-Zubayr and Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, reasserted itself, taking advantage of Uthman's weakness and advanced age. Ali ibn Abi Talib took up the cause of the oppressed classes. The struggle between all these forces eventually ended in the triumph of the aristocracy of Abu Sufyan's family.

In short, despite its outwardly political and religious character, the struggle over the caliphate was first and foremost a class struggle, not a difference of judgement over an uncertain question.\*\*

The next point concerns Khalil's exaggerated assessment of the standing of Arab civilisation before Islam. What conceivable reasoning could justify his claim that “Arab thought before Islam was more advanced than Greek thought in its various schools and the schools influenced by it”?

I hardly think I need to comment!

The greatness of Islamic civilisation stems from its openness to human thought, both Eastern and Western, free from prejudice or rigidity. It translated that thought, made it its own and added to it. In doing so, it enriched world civilisation, both by preserving classical thought and by developing it and presenting it in an Islamic form. The place of Islamic civilisation in history becomes clear when we see what it drew from those that came before it and what it gave to those that followed.\*

<!-- PDF page 208 | printed page 210 -->

The last point of disagreement concerns his portrayal of the Ismaili movement as “a counter-revolution carefully planned by Jews out of hatred for Ishmael, who received his father's birthright instead of their forefather Isaac”, and his claim that the role of Maimun ibn Sa'id ibn Daysan in the Ismaili movement casts doubt on its true nature. Arab scholars and Orientalists have already examined this subject exhaustively. They concluded that the attempts to discredit the Ismaili movement originated with its Abbasid enemies. I will confine myself here to the words of Ibn Khaldun:

“Among the unreliable reports is the view taken by many historians and supposedly reliable authorities regarding the Ubaydids, the Shia caliphs of Kairouan and Cairo, denying their descent from the family of the Prophet (may God's blessings be upon them) and casting aspersions on their lineage... They rely on accounts fabricated for the weaker Abbasid caliphs, in order to curry favour with them by disparaging those who opposed them and inventing suspicions about their enemies.”\*\*

---

<!-- PDF page 209 | printed page 211 -->

### 2 — Supportive Views

Ra'fat Saif, Ibrahim Omar, Aida Awf and others also wrote articles and expressed views in support of my approach. Here are some passages from their contributions.

**Ra'fat Saif wrote:**

“... I had been following with interest this series of articles in which the talented researcher Dr Mahmoud Ismail examines and analyses *Secret Political Movements in Islam*. My interest grew even more when he came under fierce criticism for what he had written. Before long, that criticism turned into accusations aimed more at the man himself than at the substance of his work. Those accusations have no sound, objective basis. They are really an emotional, overwrought reaction, hardly becoming of anyone who sets out to
<!-- PDF page 210 | printed page 212 -->
discuss an intellectual question of such novelty and importance. I am not here to defend Dr Mahmoud or his way of thinking. He is a university professor, free to decide what ideas to accept and what beliefs to hold. What I had hoped for, however, was a calm, reasoned exchange of ideas as the basis for both debate and practice.

“... We can only truly understand history and write about it through a dialogue between ourselves and others. Every historical event has its own distinctive character. The search for evidence and the effort to understand history undoubtedly give direction to the mind of the historian as they write and analyse. Their duty, then, is to approach events with a high degree of detachment, free of fear and hang-ups, so long as they are seeking to illuminate a social outlook grounded in a clear method.

“That is what Dr Mahmoud Ismail did in his study of secret political movements in Islam. He tried to trace the growth and development of an idea until it took settled form, using a variety of methods and tools. This matters enormously. The real challenge of history depends on how sound and impartial these acts of thought are: through them we move from the thing itself to knowledge of it, and from the human reality that gave shape to the way it was understood in the past to history. It is clear, then, that history in all its dimensions, for Dr Mahmoud Ismail, is a kind of genuine cleansing — indeed, a liberation from the unconscious assumptions of society.

“Suppose, for the sake of argument, that Dr Mahmoud Ismail is indeed trying in his own way to explain Islamic history and its events through the lens of historical materialism. That is absolutely no reason to hurl accusations at him — hostility to the Arabs, atheism, opportunistic leftism and so on. If two people disagree because each follows a different
<!-- PDF page 211 | printed page 213 -->
approach, that is entirely reasonable. It is even acceptable for two people who share an approach to disagree on how to apply it. But for one of them to attack the other personally, trying to discredit him and turn the nation, the world and religion against him by accusing him of heresy, religious deviance and tampering with sacred things — that is something we neither accept nor respect.”

---

Under the title

#### Those Who Have Risen from Their Coffins

Ibrahim Omar wrote:

“There can be no doubt that everyone interested in bringing the brighter aspects of our heritage back to life has eagerly followed Dr Mahmoud Ismail's scholarly endeavour, which Ahmad Musa Salim turned into a war of words. We applaud Dr Mahmoud for taking up the torch and venturing into the most important corner of the question of our heritage. His study sought a just solution for the masses crushed beneath the weight of social injustice. But we were astonished to see some people turn this initiative into an exchange of insults with someone who has had the courage to tackle questions about our very existence and our future, all the while repeating ready-made answers and lessons learnt by rote.

“We were amazed, too, when Ahmad Musa Salim suddenly began talking about scientific methods and how successfully they could be applied to the human sciences. Can we really take his talk about scientific methods seriously after he has wasted both his time and ours on the origins of words like
<!-- PDF page 212 | printed page 214 -->
*qaramit*, *qarmut*, *qarmata* and so on? These are precisely the traps our predecessors fell into during the ages of intellectual darkness. They filled dark pages of our heritage with searches for the origin of a word, explanations of it, texts explaining the explanation and commentaries on the commentaries. Can it really be that this is happening in the final third of the twentieth century, at the hands of Ahmad Musa Salim?”

---

Aida Awf wrote an article entitled

#### A Modest Opinion on a Serious Subject

In it she says:

“As a student of history and a reader with a modest amount of learning, I have followed with interest the debate in our magazine, *Rose al-Youssef*. It has brought back memories of the lively exchanges between writers and thinkers in the first half of this century, which were undoubtedly a bright spark in our intellectual life.

“Perhaps this current debate on secret political movements in Islam marks the beginning of a return to the right path for our culture after so many stumbles. I firmly believe that dialogue, first within ourselves and then among ourselves, is the right starting point for a conscious understanding of who we are, and therefore for finding solutions to our problems.

“If I have anything to say on the subject, it is an honest expression of what I have learnt from the lessons of history. Perhaps the most important lesson of all is objectivity. Yet Salim's arguments trampled on that very principle. I felt as though we were no longer living in the final third of the twentieth century. My mind went back
<!-- PDF page 213 | printed page 215 -->
to the Middle Ages, when anyone bold enough to hold an independent opinion was destined to be excommunicated and cast out of the mercy of the Church — the Church that considered itself the guardian of knowledge, but was in fact an obstacle to its progress in those ages.

“The second lesson of history is that Europe only found the means to achieve its revival thanks to those who rebelled against the Church, from within the Church itself, in what became known as the Reformation. Names come to mind here: Martin Luther, Calvin, Zwingli, Erasmus and other thinkers who tore away the false covering that concealed the truth and created the conditions in which the human and experimental sciences could flourish.

“The question is this: are we still living in that period? And when will we break free from its captivity? That is the responsibility of our thinkers. Surely we have had enough of burying our heads in the sand, refusing to face reality in the hope of finding a false sense of security.”

---

Since the term *shu'ubiyya* cropped up so frequently in the debate, and some people had such a limited understanding of what it meant, we decided to shed some light on it. I wrote an article entitled:

#### Shu'ubiyya in Historical Perspective

Here is the text:

The term *shu'ubiyya* has come up repeatedly in the continuing debate on secret political movements in Islam. It may be useful to shed some light on what the word means and what it signifies in
<!-- PDF page 214 | printed page 216 -->
Islamic history, especially as dictionaries neither satisfy our curiosity nor give us a full account of its meaning.

The emergence of *shu'ubiyya* is historically tied to the Abbasid age. Anyone who browses the historical records and literary works of the period can see this without much difficulty. Indeed, whole books were written for this very purpose, and their authors became known as *shu'ubis*.

What did this phenomenon mean? What actual conditions gave rise to it? What were its wider social implications? And what were its political and cultural consequences, both its bright and its dark sides?

These are the questions we will try to answer.

The Arabic word *shu'ubiyya* comes from *sha'b*, meaning a people. God says in the Quran: “O people, We created you from a male and a female, and made you peoples and tribes so that you might come to know one another.”

It was from peoples such as the Arabs, Persians, Turks, Berbers and Copts that the Islamic community came into being. With the rise of Islam and its spread from the eastern reaches of China to the Atlantic Ocean, the old conflicts between these peoples, or most of them, disappeared. By God's grace, they all became brothers. Ethnic prejudice and claims to supremacy based on tribal loyalty vanished from the lands of Islam in its early days. Faith became the measure by which one Muslim might be distinguished from another, rather than whether one was Arab or non-Arab: “An Arab has no superiority over a non-Arab except in reverence for God.”

Under the Umayyads, the religious standard was pushed aside. Arab identity became the deciding factor: Arabs were the masters, and everyone else was subject to them. Viewed from the perspective of
<!-- PDF page 215 | printed page 217 -->
social class, Islamic society was now made up of two main classes: the Arab aristocracy and the working class of the *mawali*, the non-Arab Muslims. The weight of Arab domination was enough to smother their movements, and throughout the Umayyad age they remained oppressed peoples.

The rise of the Abbasid state dealt a blow to the Arab aristocracy. This created conditions in which other peoples could enter the struggle, invoking the principle of equality that the Abbasid movement had embraced in the name of Islam.

To be fair, certain groups drawn from all these peoples were sincere in calling for Islamic teachings on equality to be put into practice. They formed a moderate, enlightened wing that gave *shu'ubiyya* its brighter meaning. They were known as the Advocates of Equality. At the heart of this wing were religious thinkers and scholars, Arab and non-Arab alike. Consider these words of Ibn Qutayba, himself an Arab:

> ... The fairest among them, in my view, are those who hold that all people share one father and one mother. They were created from dust and returned to dust. This is their highest ancestry, and it reminds people of understanding not to indulge in self-importance, arrogance or pride in their forefathers. Then they all return to God, where the ties of ancestry are severed and inherited distinctions count for nothing, except for those whose true distinction is reverence for God.

Ibn al-Muqaffa, who was Persian, did not deny the Arabs their merits or intelligence. He disagreed with some of his own people and reproached them for belittling the Arabs. An enlightened scholar, he neither showed prejudice in favour of his own people nor begrudged the Arabs their merits. Consider these wonderful words of his:

> ... If I have missed out on my share of noble ancestry, I shall not miss out on my share of knowledge. The Arabs governed without a model laid down for them
<!-- PDF page 216 | printed page 218 -->
> or inherited precedents to follow. They educated themselves, and their aspirations lifted them up. Their hearts and their eloquence raised them to greatness. Through them God inaugurated His religion and His caliphate until the Day of Gathering. Whoever denies them their due is the loser; whoever denies their merits loses the argument.

The other wing of *shu'ubiyya* was extremist and split into two camps at opposite ends of the spectrum. One consisted of prejudiced Arabs; the other of non-Arabs who believed that they deserved to rule and were more entitled to the leading position. Each side set about exaggerating its own achievements and cataloguing its rival's faults with equal excess.

Having lost the influence they had enjoyed under the Umayyads, the Arabs were dismayed to see people of Iranian origin rise to the top of society in the early years of Abbasid rule. They fought desperately to restore their former standing. They tried all sorts of means, including overthrowing the Abbasid caliphate. They supported Abu Muhammad al-Sufyani's revolt in Syria in 134 AH and backed Abd Allah ibn Ali, who had pro-Arab leanings, in his uprising against his nephew, the caliph al-Mansur. Their efforts failed, however, and the Khurasani troops ruthlessly crushed the movement. They turned instead to a different approach: intrigue and conspiracy.

We have no doubt about the part played by al-Fadl ibn al-Rabi, together with Harun al-Rashid's wife Zubayda — both of them Arabs — in turning al-Rashid against the Barmakids until he brought them down. This relative success gave the Arabs another opportunity to emerge on the political scene during the struggle between al-Amin and al-Ma'mun. They sided with al-Amin, but al-Ma'mun's victory, achieved with Persian support, dealt them a crushing blow from which they never recovered before al-Mu'tasim came down hard on them and scattered what remained of their forces across Syria and the Arabian Peninsula. He put an end to their influence by removing them from the register of military stipends and excluding them from all civilian and military posts.

<!-- PDF page 217 | printed page 219 -->

This political defeat, together with the loss of their social superiority and the economic losses they suffered, made them long for the past. That longing found expression in a kind of overwrought lament that filled Arabic writings of the period, reviving the glories of the Arabs before and after Islam. Al-Hamadhani wrote about the history and civilisation of the ancient Arabs, while others devoted themselves to writing about genealogy, idols and pre-Islamic Arabic literature.

Taken as a whole, these writings set out to prove that the Arabs were the finest of peoples in their authenticity and ancient lineage, that no other people loved or enjoyed freedom more than they did. They extolled the Arabs' unique moral qualities, such as courage in coming to the aid of others and generosity, their unrivalled wisdom and proverbs, and their gifts of eloquence and expression. All this was in addition to their honourable historical role in championing Islam and undertaking to spread its message throughout the lands of the East and West.

Nor did these writers forget to denounce other peoples, dwell on their faults and belittle their standing, giving vent to the resentment hidden in their hearts. We acknowledge that their works contained sound arguments and, often enough, proofs that silenced their opponents. Yet they also contained a great deal of exaggeration, prejudice and departure from the truth. Taken as a whole, they bore the stamp of *shu'ubiyya*.

The second camp of extremist *shu'ubiyya* drew large sections of its support from non-Arab peoples, especially the Persians and Turks, who took turns in occupying the leading position during the Abbasid age: first the Persians and then the Turks.

The Persians, to whom the overthrow of Umayyad rule was owed, saw their star rise. Persian ways came to dominate the institutions and customs of the caliphate,
<!-- PDF page 218 | printed page 220 -->
and they held the reins of government and politics until the reign of al-Mu'tasim, who freed himself from Persian influence and enlisted the help of the Turks. The first Abbasid period ended with the ascendancy of the Turks. During the second, the two sides took turns holding the leading position at the heart of the state. Eventually that heart grew weak, and the caliphate lost control of its outer regions. The unity of the Islamic world fell apart as regions sought independence. Other peoples now had the chance to play their own part on the political stage, driven by regional loyalties, national feeling and religious solidarity, which sprang first and foremost from purely economic motives.

For these people, *shu'ubiyya* rested on claims of ethnic superiority and the superiority of their own civilisations. The Persians took pride in their experience of government and their brilliance in philosophy and science; the Indians boasted that their land was the fountainhead of wisdom; the Egyptians and Greeks that they were pioneers of human civilisation; the Turks of their supposedly invincible military power, and so on. In the eyes of all these groups, the Arabs were uncouth nomads unfit for civilised life, with doubtful ancestries, who had lived in an age of ignorance before Islam. These *shu'ubis* argued that the fact that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) was an Arab could not be used to justify Arab supremacy, because Islam was a universal message, not the exclusive possession of the Arabs.

The writings of this group of *shu'ubis* are filled with bitter prejudice against the Arabs and delight in their misfortunes. They descend into vulgarity in their eagerness to enumerate Arab faults. They falsely attribute traditional sayings to support their arguments and even invented sayings attributed to the Prophet for this purpose. Among the works worth mentioning here are *Redress for the Non-Arabs Against the Arabs* by the Persian Sa'id ibn Humayd al-Bakhtakan, and *The Greater Book of Faults* and
<!-- PDF page 219 | printed page 221 -->
*The Lesser Book of Faults* by al-Haytham ibn Adi, *Thieves of the Arabs* by Ma'mar ibn al-Muthanna, and other books that express the extremism of their non-Arab *shu'ubi* authors.

Such, then, is a brief picture of *shu'ubiyya*. From it we can see that the phenomenon was not so much a doctrine or creed with its own set of principles and foundations as a tendency, an expression of the positions taken by different forces in society, even though it clothed itself in the language of ethnicity. It arose, first and last, in connection with the question of justice — or, more precisely, with the enormous gulf between Islam's tolerant teachings and its lofty human ideals, on the one hand, and the rulers' disregard for those teachings in the way they governed, on the other. It was this glaring contradiction that set the conflict ablaze.

Had this been nothing more than a struggle between rival peoples and tribal loyalties, there would never have been that enlightened group, the Advocates of Equality, who understood *shu'ubiyya* as the unity of peoples under the justice of Islam.

The many states of the extremist wing of *shu'ubiyya* turned against one another. Peoples rose and others faded away. And Islam endured, in spite of the extremists, Arab and non-Arab alike.

<!-- PDF page 220 | blank -->

[^MJ-35]: Abu Muawiya the Blind said that al-Amash had told him: “I saw Abd al-Rahman ibn Abi Layla (one of the Murji'ite leaders) when al-Hajjaj had him brought to a standstill and said to him, ‘Curse the liars, Ali ibn Abi Talib, Abd Allah ibn al-Zubayr and al-Mukhtar ibn Abi Ubayd.’ Abd al-Rahman replied, ‘May God curse the liars.’ Then he began a fresh statement, saying, ‘Ali ibn Abi Talib, Abd Allah ibn al-Zubayr and al-Mukhtar ibn Abi Ubayd.’” Al-Amash added: “I realised that he had started a new sentence: the names stood on their own and were not part of the curse.” See Ibn Saad, *The Great Biographical Classes* (*al-Tabaqat al-Kubra*), vol. 6, pp. 112–113.

    It seems to us that the rapprochement between the Murji'ites and the Shia at that time was temporary, made necessary by their shared aim of opposing the Umayyads. The Shia actually harboured a deep hostility towards the Murji'ites, while the latter adapted their beliefs to suit the circumstances. Nothing illustrates this better than the conduct of one of their leaders, Amir ibn Sharahil al-Shabi. He declared himself a Shia and joined the revolt of al-Mukhtar ibn Abi Ubayd al-Thaqafi, then abandoned that position when the revolt failed. He took part in the movement of Abd al-Rahman ibn al-Ashath, but when that too failed, he declared his repentance before al-Hajjaj, who appointed him judge of Kufa. When the Umayyad state fell, he proclaimed his loyalty to the Abbasids, who kept him in office. A man whose allegiances changed so readily offers evidence of “the opportunism associated with the Murji'ites”. Consider al-Shabi's own words: “Love the righteous among the believers and among the Banu Hashim, but do not become a Shia. Leave judgement on what you do not know for later, but do not become a Murji'ite. Know that good deeds come from God and bad deeds from yourself, but do not become a Qadarite. Love anyone you see doing good, even if he is a man from Sind with a disfigured nose.” See Ibn Saad, vol. 5, p. 245 and following, vol. 6, pp. 248–249. This shows that the Murji'ites “gave their allegiance to whoever prevailed”.

Naturally, the result favoured the Murji'ites. See Wellhausen, *The Arab State*, p. 473; Hasan Mahmud, *The Islamic World in the Abbasid Age*, p. 21.

[^AB-13]: Manichaeism was an ancient Persian religion named after its prophet Mani. At its heart was the belief in two opposing divine powers, one of good and the other of evil. The first was represented by light and the second by darkness. The first created the angels and the world of spirits, while the material world came from the second. Manichaean beliefs played a significant part in Islamic and Christian philosophy in the Middle Ages. See Van Vloten, p. 83.

[^MT-4]: Ibn al-Rawandi was a Persian from Isfahan who lived in Baghdad. He embraced Mu'tazilite thought, later rejected it and joined the group known as the Rafida, a name used for certain Shia. He wrote a book attacking the Mu'tazilites, *The Disgraces of the Mu'tazilites*, attributing to them views quite different from their own. Their enemies seized on these statements to attack them and discredit their beliefs. In fact, Ibn al-Rawandi was a heretic who attacked belief in God as Lord and the prophethood of Muhammad; his writings cannot therefore be taken as reliable. The Mu'tazilite al-Khayyat refuted them in his book *The Vindication*, an answer to the atheist Ibn al-Rawandi.

[^MT-59]: We see no reason to say that the Mu'tazilites borrowed this principle from Aristotle's idea of the golden mean — Aristotle held that virtue was the middle ground between excess and deficiency. [^MT-86]: This poem by the Mu'tazilite poet Safwan al-Ansari is an exceptionally important document for understanding the political activity of the Mu'tazilites. We quote the following lines from it:

    > His men are in every frontier town beyond the people of China,  
    > As far as the distant Sous and beyond the Berbers.  
    > Men who preach the cause, whose resolve cannot be broken  
    > By the scorn of a tyrant or the schemes of a trickster.  
    > When called to travel in winter, they willingly go;  
    > In summer, too, they fear no scorching month.  
    > They leave their homes behind, giving freely and bearing the cost,  
    > Facing great dangers and the hardship of the road.  
    > He made their efforts prosper and struck a spark from their flint,  
    > And kindled a flame of triumph that overpowered their opponents.  
    > They are the anchors of God's earth in every town,  
    > Its source of counsel and of wisdom in disputes.  
    > In every debate they reach the very heart of the matter,  
    > As a butcher's blade finds its mark in the bone.  
    > You see them as though birds were resting on their heads,  
    > Upon turbans known to the communities around them.

    See *Eloquence and Exposition*, vol. 1, p. 25.

[^MT-91]: Al-Jahshiyari, *History of the Ministers*, p. 128. Ibn Qutayba relates that Amr ibn Ubayd, the leader of the Mu'tazilites after Wasil, once spoke to al-Mansur. One of the caliph's companions took offence at what Amr said, and al-Mansur fell silent. Pointing at the man, Amr said to al-Mansur: “This man has kept you company for twenty years and has never seen fit to offer you a word of advice. And beyond your door, nothing is being done in accordance with the Book of God or the example of His Prophet.” Al-Mansur replied, “What am I to do? Here, I have put my signet ring in your hand. Come with your companions and relieve me of the burden.” Amr replied, “Call us to your side by acting justly, and we will gladly help you. A thousand wrongs await redress at your door. Put some of them right, and we will know you are sincere.” See *Choice Accounts*, vol. 3, p. 237.

[^QA-32]: *The Infamies of the Batiniyya*, p. 53. Despite his standing as a scholar, al-Ghazali could not free himself from the outlook of his class. He lived in comfort and luxury, a favourite of the Seljuk sultans' courts and the palaces of their ministers. His close ties to the Seljuk minister Nizam al-Mulk are too well known to need explanation. When he looks down on ordinary people and calls them ignorant, he is therefore expressing the contempt of a privileged class more than he is simply saying that these people lack knowledge, as one might at first suppose. We find much the same thing in a later age when al-Jabarti looks down on ordinary people and calls them the “rabble” (*harafish*), distinguishing them from the prosperous class of the “children of respectable families”. This clearly reveals the general link between traditionalist thinking and political power, and the affinity of reasoned thought with social justice.

[^QA-33]: See our views on this subject in the March 1973 issue of *al-Katib*, in the discussion held by the journal under the title “Religion and the Scientific Method”.

[^QA-35]: On this subject, see the chapter that De Boer wrote on the Brethren of Purity in his *History of Islamic Philosophy*, as well as Dr Jabbur Abd al-Nur's book *The Brethren of Purity*. This community emerged in the second half of the fourth century AH as an underground circle of thinkers based in Basra, with branches in many Iraqi towns. Its appearance as an intellectual movement that sought to spread enlightened thinking and make the “Brethren” aware of the evils of the Abbasid system was a natural consequence of the failure of the revolutionary movements, which the caliphate had met with violence and brutality. Its concentration on the towns of Iraq shows how impossible it was to pursue the path of revolution, which had succeeded only in the outlying regions. After the fall of the Iraqi Qarmatians in 316 AH, the Brethren of Purity gave the movement a new intellectual direction, relying on the word rather than the sword. They produced a series of essays on astronomy, mathematics, geography, music, ethics and philosophy, and circulated them within their community to prepare its members intellectually as a revolutionary vanguard opposed to those in power. In a recent study of the Brethren of Purity, we confirmed these views and discovered that the community dated back to the middle of the third century AH.

[^QA-45]: The Persian heritage played an important part here. The Ismailis and Qarmatians also drew on the intellectual revival in the city of Harran, the centre of the Sabians, which radiated new ideas in philosophy, astronomy and medicine.

[^QA-46]: Religious ideas were essential to the success of social revolutions in this period. Purely rationalist movements met with no success, and their pioneers were branded apostates and heretics. This explains why the Mu'tazilites were unable to draw the discontented masses to their cause, despite the fact that their beliefs combined reasoned thinking with social justice. Social revolutions with no religious foundation also failed, such as the peasants' revolt led by al-Mubarqa al-Yamani in the reign of al-Mu'tasim in 227 AH, and the Zanj revolt in the middle of the third century AH, despite the successes the latter achieved. See al-Tabari, vol. 8, p. 44 and following; Abd al-Jalil Hasan, “The Revolt of the Angry”, *al-Katib* journal, issue 117, pp. 154–155.

[^QA-49]: Lewis quotes several lines of verse that make this point:

    My beloved scolds me for leaving off prayer.  
    I tell her, “Out of my sight! You are divorced.”  
    By God, I will not pray to God while I am penniless.  
    Let the honoured sheikh and Fa'iq pray to Him.  
    Why should I pray? Where are my home and dwelling?  
    Where are my horses, my jewels and my belts?  
    Should I pray when I have not even a handspan of land to my name?  
    Would I not be a hypocrite?  
    But if God grants me plenty, I will never cease  
    To pray to Him as long as lightning flashes in the sky.

    See *The Origins of Ismailism*, p. 191. [The poem is rendered for meaning; the fourth and fifth couplets contain difficult wording in the source.]

[^QA-69]: Here is the text of the pledge. The advocate says to the person who has responded to the movement:

    > You bind yourself by God's covenant and pledge, by the protection of His Messenger (peace be upon him), and by the covenant and pledge that God took from the prophets. You will keep secret what you have heard and will hear from me, what you have learned of my affairs and the affairs of the man residing in this town for the rightful leader, the imam, the Mahdi; the affairs of his brothers, companions, children and household; the affairs of those who obey him in this faith; and the sincere followers of the Mahdi and his Shia, men and women, young and old. You will reveal nothing of it, great or small, by which anyone might learn of it, except what I permit you to speak of, or what the person in charge in this town or elsewhere permits you to reveal. In that case you will act within the limits we set for you and will not exceed them. You bind yourself to fulfil what I have set out for you and undertake by God's covenant and pledge to protect me and everyone I name and identify to you from whatever you would protect yourself against. You will be sincerely loyal to us and to the imam for God's sake, openly and in your heart. You will betray neither God, nor His friend, nor any of his helpers and allies, nor anyone connected with him and with us through kinship, wealth or favour. You may not invoke any opinion or other pledge to annul this covenant. If you do any of these things knowing that you have broken it, then you are cut off from God and His messengers, the first and the last, from the angels nearest to Him, and from all the scriptures He revealed to His earlier prophets. You are outside every faith, outside the party of God and within the party of Satan and his allies. May God utterly forsake you and hasten vengeance and punishment upon you if you break anything I have made you swear to, by twisting its meaning or otherwise. If you break any of it, you must make thirty pilgrimages to His House, as a binding vow, walking barefoot. And if you break it, everything you own at the time of your breach is to be given in charity to the poor and needy who are not related to you; every enslaved person you own on that day is to be freed; and every woman who is your wife or whom you marry in future is to be divorced three times if you break any of this. If you intend or conceal in your heart anything contrary to what I mean by this oath, the oath nevertheless binds you from beginning to end. God bears witness to the truth of your intention and the resolve of your conscience. God is sufficient as a witness between me and you. Say: yes. Then the person who has responded says: yes.

    [The wording of a few clauses in this historical pledge is difficult in the scan; the English preserves its apparent meaning.]

[^QA-70]: We do not believe al-Ghazali's account of the stages of “deception and obfuscation” is reliable. By these he means misleading the follower on certain matters of faith so that they abandon acts of worship and adhere to the interpretations of the imams in whom God has placed the secrets of knowledge. In addition to what we have already said, our doubts are reinforced by the fact that al-Daylami differs from al-Ghazali over the name of the second stage: while al-Ghazali calls it “obfuscation”, al-Daylami calls it “establishing the foundations”. See *Principles of the Beliefs of the Family of Muhammad*, p. 42.

[^QA-103]: The Qarmatians' wars were marked by violence and cruelty. This sprang both from their passionate commitment to their cause and from the brutality and persecution they had suffered at the hands of their enemies. Historians hostile to the Qarmatians portray their warfare as savage and accuse them of a particular thirst for blood. They use the episode of the attack on Mecca and the removal of the Black Stone to Bahrain as further grounds for their hostility. Yet the incident had political motives, and did not reflect any innate tendency towards plunder or extortion, or any desire to desecrate the holy places of Islam. The purpose of the campaign was to expose the Abbasid caliphate's inability to defend the holy lands. The Qarmatians even refused an Abbasid offer of “fifty thousand gold dinars” for the return of the Black Stone to the Kaaba (Ibn Khaldun, *The Book of Lessons*, vol. 4, p. 89). They subsequently returned it after achieving their political aims. In fact, people living at the time did not regard the incident as an outrage of the magnitude that later generations did. The sanctity of the Kaaba had already been violated twice under the Umayyads [one weapon detail omitted], and Sufis at the time regarded the veneration of the Black Stone as a form of idolatry. (See Adam Mez, vol. 2, p. 64.) Scholars ought therefore to moderate their excesses and view this incident as a product of its age and the political circumstances of the time.
